# Retention notes — Segment 20 (Retellings 01–24; the Ostrey Hollow skimming-station affair)

Twenty-four retellings so far, all about the same events: the Larrow Valley Cooperative Creamery Association, its skimming station at Ostrey Hollow, tester Ansel Keddie, the long low butterfat tests there, the 1925 board of arbitration, and the 1958–59 discovery that the station's pipettes were short. Retellings 01–24 (Vira Toland 1979; Effie Loomis 1941; Ansel Keddie 1925; Ivy Keddie 1959; Wilbur Teague 1948; Merle Strawn 1936; Halbert Nym 1972; Selby Vose 1926; Roy Brant 1955/58; Rosalie Cudd 1958; Leland Vaught 1958; Dell Rundle 1939/59; Alonzo Frick 1946; Hobart Sill 1934; Nyle Grigg 1919/1908; Dr. Roland Kepp 1926; Hazel Brant 1981; Duncan Keddie 1968/70; the Ashlin Chronicle 3 Oct 1958; Junia Ammon 1949; Cleve Oram 1977; Orin Falke 1959; Bertram Kyle 1953; Orra Keddie's diary 1897–1930, published 1964) are carried forward unchanged and in full below. **Segment 20 added NO new retelling — every one of its seven chunks was the tail of the "long 24" miscellany (lines 5,008–6,681), all spec, ledger, gossip and gibberish, and the story material below is therefore exactly Segment 19's, carried forward whole. Segment 19 added ONE new retelling: Retelling 24 — Orra Keddie, "A Farm Woman's Book, 1897–1930," diary extracts published 1964** (Source 24) — found as "Chapter 20" of the long-24 miscellany, a diary "kept at Ostrey Hollow, printed from the three notebooks now in the family's hands," with an unnamed editor's preface and bracketed notes; the preface makes Orra **born 1866** (vs Duncan's "eighteen sixty-eight"; her own August 1930 entry "I am sixty-four" agrees with 1866, and 1948 − 1866 = 82, Duncan's age for her), married 1889, the diary begun "in the spring of 1897" and closed 1930 "in three ruled notebooks bought at Ashlin," her husband "A." throughout, "nothing in her hand survives from before the marriage," a loose leaf of 1934 laid in the back of the third book; the entries give: the station's own glass up **1 May 1897** ("done borrowing of Larrow Green. He is pleased about it. A man is pleased about small things when he is let"); Ansel walking to the station both ways when the mare was lame (April 1897); twenty below and Ansel breaking the path at four (February 1898); Duncan "five years old and reads anything set before him" (September 1899); the March 1902 store talk ("A. came home and would not eat. I said it is not your cows and not your hands, and he said no, but it is my figures they will look at"); **January 1908 "Four men up the road today in Grigg's sleigh, come to look into the herds. They went to my brother Emmet's first, being the biggest, and he gave them their dinner… They wanted to see the barns and not the station, which A. remarked on afterward and let alone"**; March 1908 the committee's wet-hay word ("My brother Emmet says the bottom fed his father and it will feed him. A. says nothing at all, which is his way, and is not always kindness to me"); threshing, fourteen men to dinner, two pies short (30 August 1910); **8 May 1911 "A. home cross about a broken measure. Rain all day, the low piece under water again"**; October 1911 "Duncan gone to the school at Ashlin to teach, seventeen and looks fourteen"; six apple trees (April 1914); **June 1919 "A circular came to the station for the patrons, over the manager's name, about water in the milk. A. would not put them in the mail nor leave them on the platform. He took them round himself, to ELEVEN families, and stood at the door while each man read it, and came home at eleven at night with his hat still on"**; **two days after, "My brother Emmet came up. He did not come in. He stood in the yard and said to A. that he had hauled whole milk to that station since the day it opened and A. knew it better than any man living, and then he asked him why he had carried the paper himself if he did not believe it. A. said he carried it because he would not have another man carry it. He went home. He has not been in this house since, and he is my brother"**; ice storm, the milk stood (February 1921); **July 1923 "A. says they have new glass at the station and new pails, and the roof is still bad, which is the Association all over. He has been in this hollow the whole of his life and has never yet seen them mend a roof before it fell in"**; November 1924 "A. up before four every day of his life… never once said he was tired… never once been late"; **January 1925 "The patrons have put in a paper against the station. My brother Emmet's name is on it. I do not blame him and I will not say so to A."**; **17 March 1925 "A. went down to Ashlin this morning in his good coat, which he has had since Duncan was married. There is a board of men to hear the whole thing out. He was up at three… I ironed the coat twice for want of anything I could do"**; **April 1925 "Two of the sittings held up here at the station itself, in the weigh room, with the men standing outside in the road. I did not go down. I could hear the wagons from the yard all that afternoon"**; **30 April 1925 "They have put him out. Twenty-nine seasons and a letter. He came up the road at his ordinary hour with his apron rolled under his arm and hung it behind the shed door and asked what there was for supper. There was ham. He ate it"**; May 1925 "[The award of the board is not mentioned in the diary. — Ed.]"; June 1925 the cow shed ("works at it from light to dark and whistles, which he never did when he was paid"); 1926 "[Several leaves torn out…]"; **October 1927 "A. mending stairs at the far end of the hollow, and the Loomis gate, and half the doors in this hollow, and taking what they offer. A carpenter at sixty-six. He is better at it than he ever was at the other and it pays a quarter of what the other did"**; **spring 1929 "Word this morning that my brother Emmet is dead. I went down. Roy met me at the gate and was kind. I sat an hour in that kitchen where I was a girl and came home. Ten years and neither of them would go up the road first, and now it is settled the way those things get settled"**; hollyhocks "my mother had" (July 1929); August 1930 "I am sixty-four and my hand is not what it was. I have kept this book thirty-three years and there is less in it every year, which is not because less happens"; **February 1934 "A. died this morning, at home, in the forenoon, without much pain at the end of it. Duncan came up in the night. There were more at the burying than I looked for, and men shook my hand who had not spoken to him in nine years, and every one of them said what a hand he was with a door. Not one of them said the other thing. Nine years they have had to say it and they had not said it and they did not say it then. He never asked them to. He said to me once and only once, and not lately, that a man cannot be cleared of a thing nobody will name"**; editor's close: "Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958, twenty-four years after his death. His widow did not live to see it; she died in 1948." **Segment 18 added TWO new retellings:** **Retelling 22 — Orin Falke ("O. Falke"), county agricultural agent of Ordell County, "The Ostrey Hollow question: a report for the county board," 1959** (Source 22) — thirteen numbered paragraphs prepared "at the direction of the board of supervisors of Ordell County" for "the county's permanent file," plus a "Note added on receipt of Bulletin No. 214" written after the report "had gone to type"; it calls Ivy Ansel's "great-niece" in ¶2 and then corrects itself in the note ("Miss Keddie is the granddaughter and not the great-niece; the error on page two is mine"); it gives the station as built "in the winter of 1895 and 1896," opened "on the 4th of May, 1896," Keddie "tester and separator man from the opening… twenty-nine seasons," the averages 3.61 vs Larrow Green's 3.78, the gap "nineteen hundredths… in the flush of June and in the depth of February alike," the 1911 nine weeks (8 May–10 July, borrowed "from elsewhere"), the three explanations argued in 1925 (water — "the Association's manager"; theft — "an officer of the Association"; hay — the 1908 committee and "a veterinarian of standing at Ashlin… in 1926"), the board at Ashlin 17 March–4 May 1925 "under the chairmanship of an attorney of that place," $3,800 "without admission," the dismissal 30 April 1925 "four days before the award," the condemnation 12 June 1923 "in the course of an ordinary circuit visit," **"From 1924 the station's average test stood at three eighty-five and remained there"** (a new third figure for 1924), the crate found February 1958 "in the loft over the station's intake room, where it had lain since the day it was condemned," nine pipettes measured 9 March 1958, a first concession that the wet hay was "a real if partial factor" and then its withdrawal on reading Bulletin 214's summary (quoted: *"Bog hay fed as the whole of the roughage depressed the butter fat test by not more than five hundredths of one per cent in winter, and by nothing that could be measured on summer pasture"* — "Five hundredths in winter and nothing in summer will not carry nineteen hundredths in July. I withdraw the concession made on page four"), and a recommendation that "no action lies for the county, all parties in interest being dead and the Association's account long since closed." **Retelling 23 — Bertram Kyle, clerk (ret.), "What I saw of the office," written for the Ordell County Historical Society, 1953** (Source 23) — written "at sixty-eight, at the Society's request, by the man who sat at the outer desk in the Cooperative's office at Larrow Green for twenty-five years, outside the manager's door"; he copied the station-gain line ("three figures and a sentence… five and a quarter per cent, as usual, carried to the general account… A figure with a name on it has been explained"), says the station's own outfit "went into use on the first of May, 1898" ("from the correspondence"), gives the first season's intake as "a good eleven hundred thousand pounds" (hearsay, "not in any book I ever ruled"), got out for the arbitration the 40,000,000 lb / 1,600,000 a year / 25 cents the pound figures, makes Selby Vose manager "in nineteen eleven" and Gideon's SON ("whatever is said at Ashlin… the nephew story twice in the last year and it is nonsense"), describes Selby ("read the totals and… never read the lines above them… thumb on the bottom figure, and sign") and Teague ("A dry man, very correct, ate the same dinner out of the same tin… never asked anybody anything in forty years"), TYPED the 1919 circular ("Mr. Vose signed it, and it went out over his name and no one else's"), puts Strawn's condemnation "in twenty-two" ("a heavy quiet man who wrote everything in a little book with a rubber band round it… we [ordered] that same week, to the supply house at Tarnet, and the new glass came up within the month… no more said of it… than there would have been of a broken window"), lists the four books he carried in to the board (test book "in Keddie's hand," receiving book, "Mr. Emmet Brant's private weigh book, which he lent for the purpose and wanted back," the inspector's notebook — "I could not have told you then and cannot tell you now that any man in that room laid the second beside the fourth and looked"), gives the award as $3,800 "without admission of any cause. That was in twenty-four," charged to the station account, and closes: "It was not a secret. It was worse than a secret. It was in plain sight with a name on it." Segments 13 (Duncan), 14, 15, 16 (nothing) stand as before. The twenty-four sources are set out in full below.

A file has still never carried two retellings.** The "long 12" file was ~7,220 lines with Retelling 12 as "Chapter 22"; "long 09" had Roy Brant as "Chapter 4"; Segments 8, 9, 10 were thousands of lines of porch gossip with nothing in them. Short retellings (13, 14, 16, 17, 19, 20, 22, 23) come as clean standalone files. A whole segment can be nothing but gibberish (Segments 14, 15 and 16 all were — three consecutive segments of the same 220,000-word "Brittle Horizon" document, which by ¶3610 / Chapter 619 is still running) — and a whole segment can be nothing but the tail of a miscellany already mined (Segment 20 was). Expect any shape: read every chunk; the retelling can be anywhere, or absent.

## CAST (consolidated across all twenty-four sources)

**The Keddie family**
- **Ansel Keddie** — tester at the Ostrey Hollow skimming station. **Duncan (1968): "My father was Ansel Keddie, born in 1861 and died in 1934. He was tester and separator man at the Ostrey Hollow skimming station of the Larrow Valley Cooperative Creamery Association from the year it opened. He was a careful man in small things to a degree that other people found trying, and it was the making of the record he left and, I have sometimes thought, the unmaking of him."** (Born 1861 → 35 when the station opened, 64 at his dismissal, 73 at death.) **Orra's diary (Source 24) confirms 1861: "A carpenter at sixty-six" in October 1927; the editor's preface: "He was tester and separator man at the Ostrey Hollow skimming station of the Larrow Valley Cooperative Creamery Association, and kept its books in his own hand for twenty-nine seasons"; Orra writes him "A." throughout.** **Falke (1959): "The tester and separator man from the opening was Ansel Keddie, who held the post for twenty-nine seasons"** (the second source, after Duncan, to give him the double title; Orra's editor the third). Kept the test book in his own hand from the station's opening (**Kyle 1953: "The station's own test book, in Keddie's hand"**). Tested 29 seasons, 1896 through 1924; did not test in 1925. **Orra: "He has been in this hollow the whole of his life" (July 1923) — a native of Ostrey Hollow; the cow shed "has wanted doing since his father's time" (June 1925) — Ansel's father had the Keddie place before him; Orra (April 1897): "A. gone before light as always and back after dark, and he has walked it both ways this week because the mare is lame"; (February 1898) "Twenty below at the door this morning. A. broke the path to the station himself at four o'clock. He says the milk comes in with the frost still on the cans"; (November 1924) "A. up before four every day of his life for as long as I have known him, and he has never once said he was tired in my hearing, and he has never once been late"; (March 1902) "A. came home and would not eat. I said it is not your cows and not your hands, and he said no, but it is my figures they will look at. Then he said no more about it"; (January 1908) the committee "wanted to see the barns and not the station, which A. remarked on afterward and let alone"; (March 1908) "A. says nothing at all, which is his way, and is not always kindness to me"; (8 May 1911) "A. home cross about a broken measure"; (October 1911) drove Duncan down to the Ashlin school "and came back without a word in him"; (June 1919) "A. would not put them [the circulars] in the mail nor leave them on the platform. He took them round himself, to eleven families, and stood at the door while each man read it, and came home at eleven at night with his hat still on"; to Emmet, two days after: "he carried it because he would not have another man carry it"; (July 1923) "A. says they have new glass at the station and new pails, and the roof is still bad, which is the Association all over… has never yet seen them mend a roof before it fell in"; (17 March 1925) "went down to Ashlin this morning in his good coat, which he has had since Duncan was married… He was up at three and did not sleep before that either"; (30 April 1925) "They have put him out. Twenty-nine seasons and a letter. He came up the road at his ordinary hour with his apron rolled under his arm and hung it behind the shed door and asked what there was for supper. There was ham. He ate it"; (June 1925) "He has begun on the cow shed… He works at it from light to dark and whistles, which he never did when he was paid"; (October 1927) "A. mending stairs at the far end of the hollow, and the Loomis gate, and half the doors in this hollow, and taking what they offer. A carpenter at sixty-six. He is better at it than he ever was at the other and it pays a quarter of what the other did"; (July 1929) "A. sat out with me an evening and named the [hollyhocks] my mother had"; (February 1934) "A. died this morning, at home, in the forenoon, without much pain at the end of it… There were more at the burying than I looked for, and men shook my hand who had not spoken to him in nine years, and every one of them said what a hand he was with a door. Not one of them said the other thing… He never asked them to. He said to me once and only once, and not lately, that a man cannot be cleared of a thing nobody will name."** Dismissed ("put out") 30 April 1925 — Duncan: "dismissed on the thirtieth of April, 1925, four days before the board handed down what it called its finding, so that the finding found nothing and the dismissal said everything. He lived nine years after it and died in 1934 with the imputation on him, in this county, among men he had weighed milk for since before their fathers died." **Falke: "The tester was dismissed on the 30th of April, 1925, four days before the award."** **Orra: "Twenty-nine seasons and a letter" — dismissed by letter.** Died in the 1930s (Ivy: 1934; Duncan: 1934; **Orra: February 1934, "at home, in the forenoon"**; **Chronicle 1958: "died in 1934 with the whole valley's opinion sitting on him… He had been dead twenty-four years when the bulletin appeared this week, and out of his job for thirty-three"**; **Cleve Oram: "he died in the thirties. I went to the funeral. It was well attended and nobody said much, which tells you something"** — Orra: "more at the burying than I looked for… every one of them said what a hand he was with a door. Not one of them said the other thing"). "An old man" with "no other trade" in 1925 — **but Cleve (1977): "Keddie went home after twenty-five and stayed home. He kept four or five cows on a piece of ground that was not big enough for them, and he did carpentry — good carpentry, he hung a barn door for us and it swung true for thirty years"** — **and Orra: the cow shed (1925), then "A carpenter at sixty-six" (1927), "half the doors in this hollow… it pays a quarter of what the other did."** **Cleve's description: "He had been the tester there from before I was born [1900]. A long dry man, very quiet, and he wore a canvas apron with the strap over one shoulder, and he had a way of holding a bottle up to the window with his arm straight out that I can see yet. I was not frightened of him and most of the boys were."** (Orra: "his apron rolled under his arm… hung it behind the shed door.") **Chronicle: "There is an old man buried in this county who was put out of his job in disgrace, and this week a bulletin from the State Agricultural College at Farland says he never did anything wrong at all"; "Ansel Keddie ran that station and made those tests"; "there were charges against the tester, and at the end of it Ansel Keddie was dismissed after a lifetime at that station"; "Ansel Keddie got nothing, and was not asked back."** Vira says he went up in '95 to get the station ready; **Hazel (1981) agrees: "Ansel Keddie went up to that station in ninety-five, and he was still there when they turned him out, and in between he tested the milk of every man on that bottom including my grandfather, who was his wife's brother."** Duncan: "from the year it opened." **Falke: the station was built "in the winter of 1895 and 1896" and opened 4 May 1896 — which reconciles "went up in '95" with "from the year it opened."** **(Orra's "He has been in this hollow the whole of his life" means "went up" was to the station, not to the hollow — he was already there.)** Strawn (never names him), 1907: "keeps his book in his own hand, and his hand is better than most… works slow and clean and reads his own figure without being coaxed to it"; 1916: "the tester complains that his patrons blame him for their figures. I told him that is the trade and he said he knew it." Nym Item 8: his statement is "One leaf, ruled, both sides, in the hand of Item 3, signed"; the test book is "One hand throughout, twenty-nine seasons" — though Nym's Item 3 heading calls it "kept by E. Brant" and Item 4 "kept by A. Keddie" (transposed; Conflict 38). Selby Vose (1926) never names him nor mentions his dismissal. Roy Brant (1955): "married into the family that had more to lose by that station than any other family on the meadow"; Emmet stopped speaking to him in 1919 "because my father held that the man who kept the test book ought to have stood up in front of that circular and did not." **(Orra's diary: he stood at eleven doors while it was read — Conflict 188.)** Vaught (1958): "a man who did not know what he was recording and set a question mark against it." **Falke (1959) on 1911: "The tester noted the fact in his book and set a question mark against it. The herds, the pasture, the season, the men and the machinery were the same throughout"; on the theft theory: "the nine weeks of 1911 dispose of it: the same man made the tests in that interval, under no different supervision, and the figures rose."** Rundle (1939) never names him: "the figures went right when the man who kept it did not." Frick (1946): "had tested at that station since it opened in 1896 and had kept the book in his own hand every season since"; statement to the board 17 March 1925; "I believed him then and I believe him now. His denial was accurate and it did him no good at all… a man who cannot account for a figure is presumed by everybody in the room to be the figure's author." Frick's corridor remark (not in the minutes): "In the corridor at Ashlin, Keddie told me privately that he had always thought the patrons were at fault and would not say so in the room. He was not a bitter man about it. He had lived among them thirty years and would not stand up and accuse his neighbors, and I record it here because it has been said since that he went to his grave accusing nobody, which is true, and that he suspected nobody, which is not." Frick: dismissed 30 April, "four days before we made our award… There was no finding against him from us and there never was one… He was simply let go while the board was still sitting, and I have never been able to think well of the Association for it"; "an honest man was ruined by a figure nobody could explain." Frick's exhibit 1: test book "4 May 1896 to 31 December 1924, in the hand of Ansel Keddie." Grigg (1919, unnamed): "abuse the man who reads their figures for them." Sill (1934) never names him; his theory is water in the cans. **Hazel (1981):** "The man they said was cheating the patrons was married to the sister of the patron who lost the most by it, and ate at his table, and the two of them argued about drainage for forty years"; Roy came home from the hearing and said "they had let Ansel go, which was the finding whatever they wrote in the book"; "gladder about it for Ansel Keddie, who did not live to see it." Kepp (1926) never names him ("a second cause in a building"). **Duncan (1968) on 1911: "He came home that summer of 1911 saying it was something in the glass, and he never dared put it in the book. I was seventeen and I remember the kitchen and I remember that he said it twice, at supper and again by the door, which was not his way. What he wrote in the book was a question mark. He was a man who would not put down what he could not prove, and I have thought since that this was the most expensive virtue anybody in that valley ever had. He had, after all, nothing to hold in his hand but a suspicion about a piece of glass, and against it stood the outfit he had been taught to trust and the practice of every station in the county. So he kept on. He kept on for another fourteen years, and wrote down every figure the glass gave him, and the figures were what convicted him."** **(Orra's diary for 8 May 1911 has only "A. home cross about a broken measure"; no July entry; no "something in the glass.")** Duncan: the patron he was said to have shorted worst "was his own wife's brother, who was at our table on a Sunday and had been for thirty-six years" (1889–1925). **Ivy by telephone (Chronicle, Oct 1958): "The glass was short, and Ansel Keddie was not. He tested the milk in front of the state's own man and he tested it the way he was taught, and the reason his figures would not come right was in a crate over his head the entire time. I would like it printed that he never knew. It would have been a kindness if somebody in this county had thought to climb a ladder."** **Cleve's father (the cream hauler) "always said there was less of it coming off that platform than went onto it… He never said it to Keddie's face and he never said it under oath, because he was not asked, but he believed it and he believed it to the end."** **Falke's summary of the bulletin's finding: "no milk was watered and no cream was stolen at Ostrey Hollow."** **Orra's editor (1964): "Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958, twenty-four years after his death."**
- **Orra Keddie (née Brant)** — Ansel's wife; Emmet Brant's sister. **Duncan: "My mother was Orra Brant, born in eighteen sixty-eight. She married my father in 1889. She was Emmet Brant's sister, the younger of the two" (Emmet born 1854 — fourteen years apart). "My mother outlived him by fourteen years and never spoke of the business except once a year, in April. She was eighty-two when she died in 1948. She had kept every letter and she left them to me, and there is nothing in them that anybody would want, only the weather and the price of things and her brother's health, and I am keeping them anyway." "She told me a great deal about who was who in that valley, being the sort of woman who could not pass a headstone without giving you the connection, and she never once put a Vose in it."** **Source 24 is her own diary, "A Farm Woman's Book, 1897–1930," extracts published 1964 by an unnamed editor from "the three notebooks now in the family's hands": editor's preface — "Orra Keddie was born in 1866 and married Ansel Keddie in 1889… She began the diary in the spring of 1897 and closed it in 1930, and it was kept in three ruled notebooks bought at Ashlin. The pages covering her girlhood and her family before 1889 were not preserved; nothing in her hand survives from before the marriage. She writes A. for her husband throughout. A single later leaf, written in 1934 and laid loose inside the back cover of the third book, is printed at the end." Her own hand: "I am sixty-four" (August 1930 → born 1866); "I have kept this book thirty-three years and there is less in it every year, which is not because less happens." BORN 1866 by the diary and its editor vs 1868 by Duncan (Conflict 116/186): 1866 makes her "eighty-two" at death in 1948, as Duncan says, and twelve years younger than Emmet. She grew up in the Brant house on Hessel Bottom ("I sat an hour in that kitchen where I was a girl"); her mother grew hollyhocks ("named the ones my mother had"); "my brother Emmet… he is my brother" (never full/half); she "did not go down" to the station sittings in April 1925 but "could hear the wagons from the yard all that afternoon"; ironed Ansel's good coat twice on 17 March 1925; served him ham on 30 April 1925; "I do not blame him [Emmet, for signing the petition] and I will not say so to A."; went down to the Brant place at Emmet's death in spring 1929 ("Roy met me at the gate and was kind"); kept house at threshing (fourteen men to dinner, 30 August 1910, "a girl up from the lower place to help… Two pies short and I have not lived it down"); set the eggs (April 1897), cut the home piece and picked wild strawberries "on the way down with A.'s dinner" (July 1897), currants (June 1904), six apple trees (April 1914 — "I shall not see them bear well but somebody will"), hollyhocks (July 1929); the diary's last leaf, February 1934, records Ansel's death and burying; editor: "His widow did not live to see it; she died in 1948."** After 1919 she and her brother never spoke; she walked to Larrow Green rather than pass his lane (Vira). **Her own account of the break (June 1919, two days after the circular): Emmet "stood in the yard… He went home. He has not been in this house since, and he is my brother"; spring 1929: "Ten years and neither of them would go up the road first, and now it is settled the way those things get settled."** Outlived Ansel "a good while" (Vira) — fourteen years (Duncan). **CONFLICT on parentage:** Roy: "full brother and sister, same mother and same father, whatever you may hear from people who like a story with a stepmother in it"; **Hazel (1981): "Orra was a half sister to my grandfather Emmet, by his father's second wife"**; Duncan: "brother and sister" (silent on full/half); **Orra: "my brother" (silent).** **CONFLICT on the Voses:** Vira: first cousin of "old Mrs. Vose"; **Duncan: "There was no connection whatever between us and the Voses… I have heard it from two women in this town that my mother and Mr. Gideon's wife were cousins, and one of them will tell you first cousins if you let her sit down. It is talk, and it has been talk since before I could walk. My mother would have told me."** **Cleve Oram (1977): "The Keddies and the Voses were cousins of some sort. It is a small valley. My mother had it from her mother and I never heard it contradicted at the time, though I could not draw you the line on paper."** **Orra's diary extracts never mention a Vose.** Falke and Kyle never mention her.
- **Duncan Keddie** — Ansel and Orra's son ("boy"; Hazel: "their boy Duncan"); **born 1894 (Duncan: "I am Duncan, born in 1894, their son"; "I was seventeen" in summer 1911; Orra, 12 September 1899: "Duncan five years old and reads anything set before him. His father says nothing but I saw him listening at the door"); Orra, October 1911: "Duncan gone to the school at Ashlin to teach, seventeen and looks fourteen. His father drove him down and came back without a word in him"; Christmas 1919 "Duncan up from Ashlin for two days. A good day and I will write it down as one"; married before March 1925 (Orra: Ansel's good coat "which he has had since Duncan was married"); February 1934 "Duncan came up in the night"; taught school at Ashlin "the better part of my life, arithmetic among other things"; retired, seventy-four in 1968; wrote "Notes for the family" at Ashlin 1968, added to 1970 (Source 18); "when I am gone there will be no one left who was in the house"; holds Orra's letters — and, by 1964, presumably the three diary notebooks "now in the family's hands," though his 1968 notes mention letters only, never a diary (Conflict 191).** Ivy's father; Roy Brant's first cousin (Roy; Hazel; Duncan: "Roy Brant and I are therefore first cousins — the children of a brother and a sister, which is the whole definition and there is nothing more to it"). **Chronicle (1958), unnamed: Ivy "grew up in this town [Ashlin], where her father taught school for many years."** Falke and Kyle never mention him.
- **Ivy Keddie** — Ansel's granddaughter, Duncan's daughter ("Duncan's girl"); **born 1931 (Duncan: "My daughter is Ivy, born in 1931, which makes her my father's granddaughter, and I write that plainly because I have already twice seen it printed otherwise and once heard it said aloud in this town by a man who ought to have known"); has children by 1968 (Duncan writes "for his daughter and her children"); Duncan: "my daughter has spent ten years putting the case right in print" (1958–68).** **The Ashlin Chronicle (3 Oct 1958) prints her as Ansel's "great-niece" — "The man the bulletin clears is Ansel Keddie, and Miss Keddie is his great-niece" — very likely one of the two printings Duncan complains of; the Chronicle also: "Miss Ivy Keddie is twenty-seven, took her first degree at Farland and stayed on for the graduate work, and grew up in this town, where her father taught school for many years. She has spent the better part of this year on it, in her own time and largely at her own expense, and the college's press release is generous about it in the way press releases are"; headline "Ashlin Girl Made Good"; "The girl from Ashlin"; "But it was Miss Keddie who went up the ladder"; Bulletin 471 "comes out under two names: Professor Leland Vaught of the college's department of dairy husbandry, and Miss Ivy Keddie, a graduate student there"; the Chronicle "reached her by telephone at Farland on Thursday evening."** **Falke's report (1959), ¶2, ALSO prints her as "Miss Ivy Keddie, a graduate student of that department [dairy husbandry], who is the great-niece of the tester at the Ostrey Hollow station" — and then corrects it in the appended note: "I am obliged to add a second correction. I am informed, and have verified, that Miss Keddie is the granddaughter and not the great-niece; the error on page two is mine." So both of the "twice… printed otherwise" that Duncan complained of in 1968 are now very probably in hand: the Chronicle (October 1958) and the county agent's report (1959).** **Cleve (1977): "Then the granddaughter came back. That was the winter of fifty-eight, and she went up that ladder and brought the crate down herself, and after that there was a great deal of writing about it… She was a Keddie and she was schooled at Farland. Ivy Keddie. And Hazel Brant up at Hessel Bottom was her people too — Ivy Keddie was Hazel Brant's first cousin once removed, that is the way of it, and the two of them not much alike to look at."** College at Farland; gave the 1959 talk "What the Crate Held." Nym: "Miss Ivy Keddie of the State Agricultural College at Farland." Rosalie Cudd addresses her as "Miss Keddie," "a graduate student at Farland." Found the crate February 1958 (Nym: Association office at Ashlin; Ivy: station loft; **Hazel: "up in that loft in the winter, under a tarpaulin and about an inch of chaff"**; Duncan: "the loft over the intake room… until Ivy found it in February of 1958"; **Chronicle: "Last February Miss Keddie went up a ladder into the loft over the old station's intake room and found a wooden crate that had been sitting there under the dust since 1923"**; Cleve: "went up that ladder and brought the crate down herself"; **Falke: "In February of 1958 Miss Keddie found the condemned glass, still crated, in the loft over the station's intake room, where it had lain since the day it was condemned"**); measured nine pipettes at Farland 9 March 1958 (**Hazel: "took the glass back east with her and measured it, and it was the glass. It had always been the glass"**; Duncan: "It went east to Farland to be measured and came back"; **Chronicle: "Miss Keddie carried the crate east to Farland. There were eleven pipettes in it in a condition to be measured, and in March she measured them, one by one, against a calibrated burette in a college laboratory"**; **Falke: "On the 9th of March she measured the nine surviving pipettes against a calibrated burette at Farland. They delivered materially less than the capacity blown upon their stems, and in the same proportion, one to another"**); gave crate to the Rooms 1959; borrowed the Brant weigh book from Roy in 1958 (Roy's postscript: "Duncan's girl Ivy came out to the place this year and asked whether she might borrow the book… I let her have it. She is careful with paper"; **Hazel: "Ivy Keddie came for it. She was at the agricultural college at Farland, doing a piece of work on the station, and she sat in our kitchen with her coat on and asked my father for the loan of the book, and he gave it to her and lived some good while afterward. I want that put down straight: he lent it. He did not leave it to her. He was particular about the difference, and if he heard it said the other way he would have gotten up out of the chair"**; Duncan: "Roy lent it to Ivy in 1958 — lent, and I want the word — and had it back") and returned it; found and copied Strawn's 1923 volume at Farland 1958 (Duncan: "which is where Ivy found the volume for 1923 eleven years later. Nobody had asked for them in the interval"; **Chronicle: the bulletin's acknowledgments name "the state dairy office, for the loan of an old inspector's notebooks"**); wrote to Rosalie Cudd 1958 (Duncan: Rosalie "had the figures copied out and sent to Ivy at the college"; **Chronicle: acknowledgments name "Mrs. Rosalie Cudd of Vessey County, of the cheese company over the ridge, who is Jerome Cudd's niece and who went into her family's records on a stranger's say-so"**). Vira's 1958 visitor "from the college away east… Hazel Brant's first cousin once removed" is Ivy; **Roy, Rundle's 1959 footnote, Hazel and Duncan all say second cousins** (Hazel: "People are forever telling me she is my niece or my cousin once removed or some such, and she is not. Second cousins. It is not complicated; it is only that nobody wants to count"; Duncan: "Hazel and Ivy are second cousins: the children of first cousins. Not cousins once removed, which is a different relation and belongs to a different generation, and not aunt and niece, which people say because it is shorter"); **Cleve (1977) sides with Vira: "first cousin once removed."** Hazel: "a girl from the college who thought to climb a ladder." Chronicle: "She is careful to say that the work was not hers alone." **Falke: the bulletin "prepared jointly by Professor Leland Vaught… and Miss Ivy Keddie"; "The bulletin is temperate in tone and does not, in the judgment of this office, overstate its case."** **Orra's diary extracts (closing 1930, last leaf 1934) never mention her — she was three when Ansel died; the editor's close, "Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958," is the diary's only allusion to her work.**

**The Brant family (Hessel Bottom)**
- **Emmet Brant** — **born 1854, died 1929** (Roy; Duncan: "kept it until his death in 1929"; **Orra, spring 1929: "Word this morning that my brother Emmet is dead. I went down. Roy met me at the gate and was kind. I sat an hour in that kitchen where I was a girl and came home"**). Biggest man on Hessel Bottom; largest patron every year (Effie); Roy: "sixty-odd acres of the meadow, and thirty-eight cows in a good year." Hazel: "My grandfather had the largest piece of it." Duncan: "Emmet farmed the largest place on Hessel Bottom and hauled the most milk into the station of any man on the creek." **Orra (January 1908): the committee "went to my brother Emmet's first, being the biggest, and he gave them their dinner"; (March 1908) "My brother Emmet says the bottom fed his father and it will feed him" — Emmet's father farmed the bottom before him; (June 1919, in the Keddie yard) "he had hauled whole milk to that station since the day it opened and A. knew it better than any man living… why he had carried the paper himself if he did not believe it… He went home. He has not been in this house since"; (January 1925) "The patrons have put in a paper against the station. My brother Emmet's name is on it. I do not blame him."** Orra's brother (full — Roy; half, by his father's second wife — Hazel; "brother and sister" — Duncan; "the younger of the two" is Orra; "my brother" — Orra). **Weigh book begun:** Roy "From the first of January, 1898"; Nym/Ivy 1 January 1897; Frick's exhibit 3 "begun 1 January 1897"; **Duncan: "Emmet Brant began it on the first of January, 1897"**; **Hazel: "My grandfather Emmet began it in nineteen hundred, on the first day of January, because he was a man who wanted a clean date under a new habit"** (Conflict 61 — five sources, three dates; 1897 has four; Orra's diary, begun April 1897, never mentions the weigh book). Wrote "at the kitchen table after the second milking, in the lamp, with his hat still on" (Roy); Hazel: "He weighed his own milk on his own scale before he ever put a can on the wagon, and then the station weighed it again at the intake, and he wrote both figures down side by side. If the two ever disagreed he would stand in the doorway of that weigh room with his hat on until somebody found out why. They generally agreed. That was the point of him"; "he carried it under his arm from the barn to the house in all weather." Among the first to say out loud (1902) that the milk tested low. Read the 1908 report twice, put it in the drawer. Noticed the 1911 rise and fall. "One who never forgave" the 1919 paper. 1910 leaf: 200,000 lb even, "Paid on a test of 3.42" (Hazel: "the only year I am certain of… Even — that is what fixes it, a round figure like that at the bottom of a whole year of hauling in the dark and the mud, as if the cows had been consulted… paid on a test of three forty-two. You set that against what that milk actually was and you have the whole of the business in one line, and nobody set it against anything for fifty years"). Argued drainage with Ansel "for forty years" (Hazel). **Duncan: at the Keddie table "on a Sunday… for thirty-six years"** (1889–1925). **Orra: "He has not been in this house since" June 1919; "Ten years and neither of them would go up the road first" (1919–1929).** His weigh book was before the 1925 board (Frick). **Kyle (1953): among the four books carried in to the board was "Mr. Emmet Brant's private weigh book, which he lent for the purpose and wanted back" — the only source to say Emmet himself lent the book to the 1925 board and wanted it back (as Roy lent it to Ivy in 1958 "and had it back").**
- **Roy Brant** — Emmet's son; **sixty-seven in winter 1955 (born c. 1888), "still milking"**; wrote Retelling 09 for the county farm bureau, postscript 1958. **Died 1962 (Duncan: "at his death in 1962 it went to his daughter Hazel").** Kept up the weigh book from Emmet's death (Hazel: "from the year my grandfather died — don't ask me, I'll only guess"; Duncan: "Roy Brant, who farmed the Hessel Bottom place after him and kept his father's weigh book"). Did not haul to Alder Corners. Duncan's first cousin. **Orra, spring 1929: "Roy met me at the gate and was kind."** Lent the book to Ivy 1958 ("lived some good while afterward" — Hazel; "had it back" — Duncan). "It goes to Hazel when I am done with it." Hazel: "He used to say a year was a thing other people kept, like bees"; "the only honest arithmetic anybody in that valley ever did without meaning to" (of the four men hauling over the ridge); drove down to Ashlin for the hearing, "came back and hung his coat up… He never went to another meeting of that Association as long as he lived, and he was a man who liked meetings." **Alive in 1959 when Falke wrote "all parties in interest being dead."**
- **Hazel Brant** — **Roy's daughter** (Hazel; Duncan: "Roy's daughter is Hazel Brant"); "farmer's daughter and last keeper of the weigh book"; recorded at **Ashlin, 1981**, at her kitchen table over two afternoons, no papers ("Quantities I have. Years I have not… I could not tell you the year I was married without walking into the front room for the certificate, and I have been married a long while"). "I was small but I remember the stove" (1919 → born c. 1910s). Lives in Ashlin ("this town"; "down the street at the historical rooms"). Nym: held the weigh book from 1962; the Rooms 1963 (Duncan: "at his death in 1962 it went to his daughter Hazel, and Hazel gave it to the Historical Rooms in 1963"). Rundle 1959: "Hazel Brant of Hessel Bottom." **Cleve 1977: "Hazel Brant up at Hessel Bottom"; Ivy and Hazel "not much alike to look at."** Her mother (Roy's wife) read the 1919 letter twice and put it in the stove.
- **Hazel's mother** — unnamed; "read hers twice and put it in the stove."
- **Emmet's and Orra's father** — unnamed; Orra (March 1908): Emmet "says the bottom fed his father and it will feed him"; Hazel: Orra was Emmet's half sister "by his father's second wife." **Orra's mother** — unnamed; grew hollyhocks ("named the ones my mother had" — July 1929); by Hazel's account the father's second wife; the Brant kitchen is "that kitchen where I was a girl."

**The Vose family (Larrow Green)**
- **Gideon Vose** — first president 1889–1911; organized the Association; drafted the by-laws (not a lawyer); died 1917. Selby: "My father… kept a herd of Jerseys at the home place… In 1910 that herd tested 4.56." Rundle: "a heavy, quiet, obstinate man who had once thought of the law and thought better of it, and he wrote the by-laws himself, at his own kitchen table, in a hand that the Association's clerks were still complaining about thirty years later"; "His nephew Selby Vose… used to say that his uncle wrote by-laws the way other men build stone wall — slowly, and to last." "Gideon Vose put that [the 'station… shall govern' clause] in for convenience and it was convenient for thirty years." Frick: "The by-laws provided for arbitration." **Duncan: "Gideon organized the Association and was its first president and wrote its by-laws; Selby was his son and not, as I have heard it given, his nephew."** **Kyle (1953): "Mr. Gideon Vose who organized the Association and was its first president."** **"Mr. Gideon's wife"** — unnamed; the town talk makes her Orra's cousin; Duncan denies it; Cleve repeats the talk ("cousins of some sort"). Orra's diary never mentions a Vose.
- **Selby Vose** — manager 1913–1938 (Effie). **Kyle (1953): "Mr. Selby Vose became manager in nineteen eleven… Mr. Selby Vose sat behind that door for a quarter of a century and I sat outside it, and I liked him, and I will say two things about him which do not sit together and are both true. He was courteous to every man who came into that office, farmer or drummer or bank, and he never once let a caller feel he was being hurried. And he read the totals and he never read the lines above them. He was not a man who read. He would take a sheet, turn it, put his thumb on the bottom figure, and sign. I watched him do it a thousand times and thought nothing of it, because that is what managers were."** Signed and wrote the 1919 "water in the cans" circular (**Kyle: "I typed it… Mr. Vose signed it, and it went out over his name and no one else's"**; **Orra: "A circular came to the station for the patrons, over the manager's name, about water in the milk"**). CONFLICT on relation: Effie "son of the late president"; Vira "old Gideon Vose's nephew"; Selby "My father"; Rundle "His nephew"; **Duncan "Gideon Vose's son… not… his nephew"**; **Kyle: "He was the son of Mr. Gideon Vose… — the son, whatever is said at Ashlin, and I have heard the nephew story twice in the last year and it is nonsense"** (son 4, nephew 2). Signed the annual station sheet; told Teague in 1916 to let the station account alone. Strawn 1913: "New manager… came out with me to the platform, which the last one never did"; 1919: told him no water ever found; 12 June 1923: "told the manager to order new." His 1926 address: thirteenth year; water-in-the-cans; dates the condemnation "the twelfth of July, 1923"; 1924 average 3.90; "I ask for one more." Roy: "the manager sent a paper round." Rundle: "the manager circulated a warning." Frick: the water explanation "was the manager's, Selby Vose… He put it without heat and without proof." Sill: "the manager put out a paper… I thought well of him for it." **Hazel: "Oh, the water letter. He sent that round to nine families on that bottom, nine letters, warning us about adding water to the milk… He sent nine and he was answered by none of them, which in that valley is a shout."** **Orra: the circulars "came to the station for the patrons" — and were carried round by Ansel "to eleven families."** Duncan: "Selby Vose, who managed the Association." **Chronicle (unnamed): "The manager of the Association had circulated a warning to the Ostrey Hollow patrons about added water as early as 1919."** **Junia Ammon (unnamed): the inspector "told the manager to order new. The Association wrote to us on the twenty-eighth of June."** **Cleve (unnamed): "when the state man made them throw it out."** **Falke (unnamed): the water theory "was the view of the Association's manager, who circulated a warning to that effect in 1919. It cannot be sustained."** **Kyle: Strawn "came in to say so and to tell the manager to order new. Which we did, that same week."**
- **old Mrs. Vose** — Orra's first cousin (Vira) — denied by Duncan.
- **"the Ashlin Voses"** — Duncan: "People confused them with the Ashlin Voses, who were no relation to Mr. Gideon either."
- **"the last one"** — unnamed manager before 1913.

**Association officers**
- **Effie Loomis** — secretary 1901–1940; minute book; 14 in 1889; "Notes toward a history" 1941. Not mentioned by Falke or Kyle (Kyle's outer-room account never names a secretary). **Orra (October 1927) has Ansel mending "the Loomis gate" at "the far end of the hollow" — a Loomis household at Ostrey Hollow; whether Effie's people is unstated (Effie: "Not one of us ever went up the valley… I include myself first").**
- **Wilbur Teague** — bookkeeper 1905–1945; invented the "station gain" line; memoir Chapter Nine, Ashlin, 1948. Frick: the cream-theft explanation was Teague's, "in the way an accountant puts a thing he wants said without saying it… then observed that he made no charge against anybody." **Kyle (1953): "Mr. Wilbur Teague was the bookkeeper and wrote those station lines every year of his service. A dry man, very correct, ate the same dinner out of the same tin. Teague never asked anybody anything in forty years. That is not a criticism. It was his notion of the position: the figures were the figures, and the asking was for other people, and if the other people did not ask it was not his affair"; the station-gain sentence was "in the bookkeeper's own words, five and a quarter per cent, as usual, carried to the general account."** **Falke (unnamed): the theft explanation "was urged at the hearing by an officer of the Association."**
- **Hobart Sill** — buttermaker at Larrow Green 1893–1934 (forty-one years; born c. 1870; 64 at retirement); churn and cream receiving books (Nym Item 16); lent his own glass outfit to the new station May 1896; "I never went up there but twice in my life"; water theory; never blamed the cows; Larrow Green 3.78; the Hollow "really a 3.85 and no better"; fat "twenty-seven cents the pound." **Falke's Larrow Green average 3.78 = Sill's; Falke's 1924 station average 3.85 = Sill's guess at the Hollow's true figure.** **Orra (1 May 1897): "they are done borrowing of Larrow Green" — Sill's glass returned.**
- **Alonzo Frick** — attorney of Ashlin; chaired the 1925 board; 74 in 1946 (born c. 1872); "tried titles and boundaries for forty years, and two homicides"; the one of three "who kept no cow"; sessions two and three at the station's weigh room; believed Keddie. **Cleve (unnamed): the hearing "started… down at Ashlin, in the Cooperative's office, because that is where the lawyer was and lawyers do not travel."** **Falke (unnamed): the board sat "at Ashlin from the 17th of March to the 4th of May under the chairmanship of an attorney of that place."** **Kyle (unnamed): "a board was formed; the sittings were partly at Ashlin and partly at the station."** **Orra (unnamed): "There is a board of men to hear the whole thing out" (17 March 1925); "Two of the sittings held up here at the station itself, in the weigh room" (April 1925).**
- **Bertram Kyle** — **clerk (ret.); "the man who sat at the outer desk in the Cooperative's office at Larrow Green for twenty-five years, outside the manager's door"; sixty-eight in 1953 (born c. 1885); "I came in as a boy on the copying and I went out with my own desk, which stood in the outer room with its back to the window and its face to the manager's door, so that everything which went in to him and everything which came out passed within a foot of my elbow. That is the whole of my qualification. I was not consulted about anything in my life and I read everything"; kept the general ledger and station accounts, copied them, "once a year… laid before the annual meeting in a fair hand, which was mine. I could rule a page then in a way that people used to remark on"; "I saw perhaps four cows in twenty-five years, and two of those were in a photograph"; "People suppose a clerk lives by the calendar. He does not. He lives by what is on the desk"; typed the 1919 circular; entered the 1923 (he says 1922) replacement order and "filed his [Strawn's] word for it"; got out the 40,000,000-lb and 25-cent figures for the arbitration; made and twice checked the list of the four books for the board and "carried them in, and… carried them out"; handled the correspondence "when it was all got out for the hearing"; "I stayed on some years after and then the office was moved and I was not moved with it" (1938); wrote "What I saw of the office" for the Ordell County Historical Society, 1953 (Source 23).**
- **Nyle Grigg** — of Larrow Green; head of the 1908 committee of three; hauls to Larrow Green; "bought hay off two of them"; 1919 covering letter; Bulletin 214 "two tenths in the winter feeding." **Orra (January 1908): "Four men up the road today in Grigg's sleigh, come to look into the herds" — Grigg's own sleigh; four men, not three (Conflict 189).** **Chronicle (garbled): "The committee that sat on the matter in 1908 sat again in 1925 and awarded the patrons their money."** **Falke (unnamed): "A committee of the Association sampled the Hessel Bottom herds in 1908 and reported to this effect… the men who reported in 1908 were practical men who walked the ground and looked into the mangers… the 1908 committee was not simply wrong" — a concession Falke later withdrew.**
- **The other two committee members (1908)** — unnamed. **(Orra counts "four men" in the sleigh — a driver, or a fourth member.)** **The other two arbitrators (1925)** — unnamed; kept cows.

**Others**
- **Dr. Roland Kepp, V.S., of Ashlin** — veterinary surgeon "of thirty years' practice" ("I have gone about this county for thirty years"); read "The bog meadows and the milk" before the **Ordell County Farm Club, 1926**; the club's secretary took down the discussion; the chairman "observed that the paper would be printed for the members." Holds the hay/soil theory: "nine cases in ten is under the animal's feet, and the tenth is in the water she drinks." Never read a test ("he had never in his life read a test and would not know how"); "no interest in that quarrel and no competence in it." Names nobody in the valley. **Falke (unnamed): "a veterinarian of standing at Ashlin wrote to the same purpose in 1926."**
- **"a gentleman who I know will forgive my noticing it here"** — unnamed; urged the 1911 objection on Kepp.
- **"A member"** (×2) and **"The chairman"** of the Farm Club — unnamed.
- **Dell Rundle ("D. Rundle," "D.R.")** — schoolteacher at Larrow Green; 1939 jubilee pamphlet; 1959 footnote; never went up to the station.
- **Vira Toland** — daughter of a Hessel Bottom patron; born 1912 at the top of Ostrey Hollow; recorded 1979. **Vira's father** — hauled milk; one of nine families; kept the letter in the front-room desk drawer; her brother has it yet.
- **Merle Strawn** — state dairy inspector, the Ordell circuit, 1907–1936; 28 pocket circuit books; condemned the pipettes 12 June 1923. Roy (1911): "The state man left his own measure that June." Rundle: 12 July 1923 (unnamed). Frick's exhibit 4: his 1923 notebook. **Hazel: "The state man condemned that glass in twenty-one — went at it with some measuring instrument he carried in his coat, said it would not deliver what it was marked for, and told them to order new. They ordered new. Nobody said one word about all the tests that were already in the book."** **Duncan: "Merle Strawn, the state's dairy inspector on this circuit, was kin to nobody in the valley whatever — not to us, not to the Brants, not to the Voses, not to anybody on the creek. That is worth writing down, because his notebooks turned out to matter, and it matters that the man who kept them had no interest in any of us"; "He kept one for every year he rode the Ordell circuit. They went to the state dairy office when he retired in 1936, and in 1947 they were transferred to the library of the State Agricultural College at Farland."** **Chronicle (unnamed): "condemned that year [1923] by the state's dairy inspector on his rounds — he ran their measures against a burette, found they would not deliver what was marked on them, and told the manager to order new ones. He wrote down that he had condemned them. He wrote down nothing about what they might have been doing for the twenty-six years before"; "the state's own man" (Ivy); acknowledgments: "the state dairy office, for the loan of an old inspector's notebooks."** **Junia (unnamed): "in the summer of 1923, an inspector for the state went into that station with a burette and put their pipettes to it."** **Cleve: "the state man made them throw it out."** **Kyle (1953): "Then in twenty-two the state man came into the office. Strawn, the dairy inspector on this circuit, a heavy quiet man who wrote everything in a little book with a rubber band round it. He had been up to the Hollow and he had run their milk pipettes against his burette and he had condemned the whole lot of them, and he came in to say so and to tell the manager to order new"; "the state inspector's notebook for the year he condemned the glass" was the fourth book before the board.** **Falke (unnamed): "The state dairy inspector for this circuit tested with the lactometer at every visit from 1908 forward and never once found added water at that station"; "On the 12th of June of that year [1923] the state dairy inspector, in the course of an ordinary circuit visit, ran the station's milk pipettes against a calibrated burette and found that they would not deliver their mark. He condemned the whole lot on the spot and directed the manager to order new… the inspector himself drew no inference whatever as to the years behind it."** **Orra's diary never mentions the inspector or the condemnation — only (July 1923) "they have new glass at the station and new pails."**
- **The new tester (1925)** — unnamed; "quick."
- **Rosalie Cudd** — Jerome Cudd's daughter (her letter; Rundle; Nym; **Duncan: "Rosalie Cudd of Vessey County, who ran the cheese company over the ridge after her father, was Jerome Cudd's daughter"**; Ivy says niece; **Chronicle 1958: "Mrs. Rosalie Cudd of Vessey County, of the cheese company over the ridge, who is Jerome Cudd's niece and who went into her family's records on a stranger's say-so"**); runs Alder Corners since spring 1941; born c. 1901; 1958 letter with the sixteen-season table. **Duncan's 1970 addition calls her "Mrs. Cudd"** ("Mrs. Cudd's test book has this year gone to the Vessey County Historical Society"); the Chronicle calls her "Mrs." in 1958. Falke, Kyle and Orra never mention her or Alder Corners.
- **Jerome Cudd** — built Alder Corners 1913 (Rosalie) / opened 1914 (Roy; Rundle); died March 1941; own test bench and book (Duncan: "Jerome Cudd kept it from the founding of his company"); "Good milk off wet ground"; the ghost saying.
- **The four Hessel Bottom men** — unnamed; hauled to Alder Corners in the summer flush 1915–1930 (sixteen seasons). Hazel: "Four of those six men hauled over the ridge in the summer flush, to the cheese factory on the far side, and brought their milk back down to our own station the rest of the year. Four." "One of them brought his boy… now in the state road department" (Rosalie). Orra's diary never mentions the ridge or Alder Corners.
- **"a man from your county who came in here once in a temper"** — unnamed (Rosalie). **The man shouting at the Green in 1902** — Sill's anecdote. **"the men who were boys when we made it"** — Grigg. **"two women in this town" and "a man who ought to have known"** — unnamed Ashlin talkers (Duncan). **"three separate patrons" who raised the question with the county agent "within one month" (Falke, 1959)** — unnamed. **"men who were there"** — Kyle's unnamed informants for the first-season intake. **"fifty farmers with their hats on their knees"** at the annual meeting (Kyle). **"farmer or drummer or bank"** — Selby's callers (Kyle). **"a girl up from the lower place" who helped Orra at threshing (August 1910); the "fourteen men to dinner" that day; "the men standing outside in the road" at the April 1925 station sittings (Orra) = Cleve's "about a dozen others" on the platform; the "eleven families" who received the 1919 circular from Ansel's hand (Orra); the men at Ansel's burying "who had not spoken to him in nine years" (Orra, 1934); the unnamed editor of Orra's diary extracts (1964).**
- **Professor Leland Vaught** — Farland; Bulletin No. 471 (October 1958), "— L. VAUGHT." **Chronicle: "Professor Leland Vaught of the college's department of dairy husbandry"; Bulletin 471 "comes out under two names" — his and Ivy's; the acknowledgments "name Professor Vaught."** **Falke: the bulletin "prepared jointly by Professor Leland Vaught of the department of dairy husbandry and Miss Ivy Keddie, a graduate student of that department."**
- **Halbert Nym** — curator, Ordell County Historical Rooms; 1972 accession notes, Items 1–16.
- **Orin Falke ("O. Falke")** — **county agricultural agent, Ordell County; wrote "The Ostrey Hollow question: a report for the county board" (1959) "at the direction of the board of supervisors of Ordell County, in numbered paragraphs," for the board's own use "and for the county's permanent file," after the matter had "lately been the subject of newspaper notice" and had "been raised with this office by three separate patrons within one month"; "The undersigned has attempted to set down what is established, in the order in which it occurred, and to indicate where the record is thin"; first conceded the hay as "a real if partial factor," then, on obtaining Bulletin No. 214 from "the library at Farland" after the report "had gone to type," withdrew it ("I withdraw the concession made on page four") and corrected Ivy's relation ("the error on page two is mine"); recommends the report be filed "with the county's dairy papers," the surviving records left "in the custody of the Historical Rooms at Ashlin, where they now are," and "that no action lies for the county, all parties in interest being dead and the Association's account long since closed." Signs "— O. Falke, county agricultural agent, Ordell County." Names Vaught, Ivy Keddie, Ansel Keddie; leaves the manager, the officer, the attorney, the veterinarian, the inspector and the committee unnamed; never mentions the Brants, Duncan, Hazel, Rosalie, Strawn by name, the crate's maker, or the Chronicle by name.**
- **The board of supervisors of Ordell County** — directed Falke's report "at its meeting of the autumn quarter" (1959).
- **Ammon & Sons, Dairy Supply, Tarnet** — invoice 14 April 1897; letter 2 July 1923. **Junia Ammon (1949): founded by Marcus Ammon "on a rented floor with two men and a secondhand annealing oven"; "sixty years" by 1949 (→ founded c. 1889); "four stories and a basement on the street behind the railroad yard at Tarnet"; "supplied half the creameries in the state… half the glass"; works records burned 1931; "sold nothing into Ordell County before 1900."** **Chronicle (1958): "Ammon & Sons, of Tarnet, are still in business and were in business in 1923… A cheap short lot was put off on the Association knowingly, and the firm's silence in 1923 is the proof of it."** **Kyle (1953, unnamed): "the supply house at Tarnet"; the station's "own outfit came from the supply house and went into use on the first of May, 1898."** **Orra (1 May 1897, unnamed): "A. says the station's own glass came up and they are done borrowing of Larrow Green."**
- **Junia Ammon** — "the firm's own historian, sixty" in 1949 (born c. 1889); wrote "Sixty Years of Ammon & Sons," privately printed, Tarnet, 1949; chapter IX; "by the time I was old enough to be a nuisance in it"; believes "The mold had been reground and the lot ran short. It was an accident and nobody in the works caught it"; regrets the 1923 letter.
- **Marcus Ammon ("Mr. Marcus")** — founder of Ammon & Sons; "a standing complaint of Mr. Marcus's that we could not get a foothold there [Ordell County] while the freight rate stood where it did"; saying "a creamery buys arithmetic from us and not glassware," lettered and hung in the packing room.
- **Cleve Oram** — "cream hauler's son"; **born 1900** ("I was born in nineteen hundred and I have not lived five miles from this chair since"); taped "in the back room of the Larrow Green feed store, spring of 1977," aged seventy-seven; rode his father's cream wagon "from the time he could sit up," regularly from about nine ("That would be nineteen and nine… You sat on the box on his left because the ditch was on the right and he would not have me on that side"); had his hand on the crate ("I have seen it a hundred times… up there after a hay fork with my own hands"); twenty-five at the 1925 hearing ("which is how I keep the date"); stood on the platform in the rain "the whole of one of those afternoons… with about a dozen others"; went to Keddie's funeral; "I have no quarrel with the college"; "Nobody wanted it for fifty years, and now every second car that comes up the valley has somebody in it with a notebook"; last saw the station with "the platform… gone and the roof… going. The ladder was still on the wall. I looked."
- **Cleve's father** — unnamed cream hauler; "drove the cream wagon down from the Ostrey Hollow station for better than twenty years"; "Two bays and a flat wagon with a rack built onto it to keep the cans standing, and a canvas over them in July"; gave the award as $3,500 ("my father was not a man who got a figure wrong when it was money"); believed "there was less of it coming off that platform than went onto it… said it to me on the wagon seat more than once, and he said it in this village to men who are dead now… never said it to Keddie's face and he never said it under oath, because he was not asked"; "the one man in the valley who saw both ends of that cream every day of his life."
- **Cleve's mother** — unnamed; "used to say she could tell the month by how my father swore coming in the door"; had the Keddie–Vose cousin story "from her mother" (Cleve's grandmother, unnamed).
- **The Ashlin Chronicle** — county weekly; unnamed writer; front page 3 October 1958, "OLD ORDELL WRONG RIGHTED"; "put together in a day and a half from a college press release and two telephone calls"; "The Chronicle has seen the invoice." **Falke (1959) alludes to it: "The matter having lately been the subject of newspaper notice."**
- **The Ordell County Historical Society** — **the body at whose request Kyle wrote in 1953** (vs "the Ordell County Historical Rooms" — Nym 1972, Hazel 1981, Duncan 1968, Falke 1959 "the Historical Rooms at Ashlin"; possibly the same institution under two names, possibly not).
- **The editor of Orra Keddie's diary extracts (1964)** — unnamed; wrote the preface and the bracketed notes ("[The award of the board is not mentioned in the diary. — Ed.]"; "[Several leaves torn out. The editor has not attempted to supply them. — Ed.]"; "[Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958, twenty-four years after his death. His widow did not live to see it; she died in 1948. — Ed.]"); "The entries have been chosen for what they show of the household and the season; nothing has been added and nothing altered but the spelling of two place names"; the notebooks are "now in the family's hands" — the family is Duncan's (1964), and Ivy's.
- **"the state dairy office"**; **"the men of 1889"**; **"the merchant" at Ashlin**; **"the platform man"** at Larrow Green; **"our editor"** of the Bar yearbook; **"the lawyer" at Ashlin (Cleve) = Frick**; **"about a dozen others" on the platform (Cleve)**; **"the works at the coast," "a drummer," "a boy [who] checks the first of a lot" (Junia)**; **"the library at Farland" (Falke)**; **"an attorney of that place [Ashlin]," "an officer of the Association," "a veterinarian of standing at Ashlin," "the state dairy inspector for this circuit," "the Association's manager," "the tester" (Falke's unnamed cast)**; **"the manager" (Orra: "over the manager's name"); "a board of men" (Orra)** — as before.
- Noise-only names (NOT story): Reuben Rutledge, Orville Oakes, the Widow Marcellus Ferris, Doctor Keturah Prentiss, Tobias Larkspur ("Brittle Horizon" gibberish — the whole of Segments 14, 15 and 16, part of Segment 13 and part of Segment 17); **Clarinda Ferris, Ambrose Whitfield, the Widow Parthenia Winslow, Nehemiah Dunmore (long-21 gibberish); Absalom Sackett, Absalom Pennington, Cassius Oakes, Zebulon Cutter, Temperance Colby, Eliakim Ormsby (long-21 gossips); young Wilhelmina Isley, Mrs. Jedediah Whitfield, Mr. Elihu Sackett, Parthenia Yarrow, Theodosia Yarrow, Prudence Winslow, Josiah Hollister, Doctor Eliakim Isley (long-24 gibberish); Cassius Ridgeway, Cordelia Mercer, Theodosia Cutter, Jedediah Oakes (long-24 gossips); Halvers-Aunet; Professor Melinda Pennington, Doctor Xanthus Trumbull, Mr. Verity Trumbull, Doctor Lemuel Hawthorne, Winona Quimby; Elihu Holloway, Yolande Aldous, Prudence Sackett, Fidelia Quimby, Cordelia Isley, Ozias Oakes; Ada Halvorsen, Perley Dunmore, Bramlett Mercantile, Sowerby & Petrie, Dr. N.
