Havelock"; plus hundreds of store-ledger customers (given names such as Cyrus, Cassius, Zilpha, Barnabas, Eliakim, Wilhelmina, Lemuel, Absalom, Reuben, Thaddeus, Zebulon, Content, Fidelia, Ransom, Xanthus, Delphine, Vernon, Araminta, Wealthy, Nehemiah, Prudence, Vashti, Ambrose, Permelia, Yolande, Temperance, Rufus, Elkanah, Jerusha, Increase, Obadiah, Ursula, Ezra, Thankful, Orville, Jedediah, Philomena, Huldah, Dorcas, Uriah, Theodosia, Cordelia, Marcellus, Zadock, Josiah, Melinda, Sophronia, Gershom, Almira, Silas, Tobias, Orpha, Winona, Verity, Salome, Ichabod, Lucetta, Parthenia, Keturah, Clarinda, Elihu, Ozias; surnames such as Ormsby, Sedgwick, Larkspur, Quarles, Ferris, Mercer, Tarleton, Hollister, Kirtland, Upshaw, Applegate, Trumbull, Ives, Prentiss, Jessup, Tolliver, Pennington, Vail, Sackett, Gantry, Nesbit, Eastwick, Cutter, Dunmore, Holloway, Pettigrew, Stanhope, Bascomb, Loveless, Winslow, Aldous, Winterbourne, Hawthorne, Colby, Oakes, Isley, Underhill, Quimby, Yarrow, Kessler, Whitfield, Jarrett, Mabry, Norwood, Rutledge, Ridgeway, Fenwick, Gault, Vance). No Brant, Keddie, Vose, Loomis, Teague, Sill, Frick, Strawn, Cudd, Vaught, Grigg, Toland, Nym, Rundle, Kepp, Oram, Ammon, Falke or Kyle appears in the ledgers, the gossip, the inquest or the gibberish. **(The one Loomis in the story outside Effie is "the Loomis gate" of Orra's 1927 entry — story, not noise.)**

---

## PLACES
- **Larrow Valley**; **Larrow Green ("the Green")** — hamlet; Association organized 1889 (Rundle: in the schoolhouse; **Falke: "organized in 1889 by farmers of this valley meeting at Larrow Green"**); creamery built 1891 on Larrow Creek "on the creek below the mill" (**Falke: "built its main creamery there in 1891"**); Strawn inspected 1913, 1917, 1931, 1936. Nym Item 16: Larrow Green average "a little under two tenths of a point better" than the station's 3.61. Roy: "seventeen hundredths of a point under." Sill: Larrow Green's own patrons 3.78. **Falke: "the Larrow Green creamery's own average over the same years was three seventy-eight, which is an ordinary figure for this county and for the counties adjoining."** Sill's churn room (plank floor 1893; creek ice out of the pond), platform, cold room, his bench. Nyle Grigg "of Larrow Green." Dell Rundle taught the school there; the pamphlet "Printed at Larrow Green"; the store. Grigg: "every crossroads store between here and Ashlin." **Cleve Oram: "this village"; the feed store (back room, 1977); "Seven miles from here up to the Hollow, and it climbs the whole seven"; Cleve born there 1900 and "not lived five miles from this chair since."** **Chronicle: "built their main creamery down at Larrow Green"; the cream "sent down to Larrow Green."** **Kyle (1953): "the Cooperative's office at Larrow Green" — the outer room with Kyle's desk "with its back to the window and its face to the manager's door," the manager's door, "a book that stood on an open shelf, in a room that anybody might walk into"; "the creamery here" (from which the station borrowed its first glass); "the creamery side" of the accounts; the annual meeting "to fifty farmers with their hats on their knees"; the creamery inventory taken "when the ice went out."** **Orra (1 May 1897): "done borrowing of Larrow Green"; (March 1902) "our milk up here runs poorer than theirs down the valley."**
- **Ostrey Hollow ("the Hollow")** — seven miles up the valley (Rundle: "three hours in mud and could not be done at all in March"; Sill: "seven miles of hill road, and in July it came down warm"; **Cleve: "There is a pitch about two miles below the hamlet, past where the creek comes in from the north, that took the horses at a walk with an empty wagon and took them at a crawl with a loaded one. In March you went up through mud to the hub and came down through it again, and the ruts froze at night in the shape your wheels left them, so that the second week of March was worse than the first"**; **Falke: "seven miles up the valley from Larrow Green, to serve the farms of that end, Hessel Bottom principally"**); **a hamlet (Chronicle: "at the hamlet of Ostrey Hollow"; Cleve: "the hamlet"; Orra: "this hollow," "up here," "the far end of the hollow," "half the doors in this hollow")**; station at the head of the Hollow where the road bends to the ridge; low whitewashed building (Rundle: boarded and battened, intake room at the road end, loft over it "never been cleared out," creek behind that has twice come over the sill); **Cleve: "The station stood on the flat by the creek where the road bends. Board building, two floors, a shingle roof that wanted work the whole time I knew it. On the road side there was a covered platform about waist high, so a man could back his wagon to it and roll the cans off level instead of lifting them. Inside that was the intake room, with the scale and the vat, and the separator going in the back of it, and a smell in there of warm milk and steam and machine oil… The skim came back out the same platform for the farmers to take home to their hogs. The cream went into our cans."** **Orra (July 1923): "the roof is still bad, which is the Association all over… never yet seen them mend a roof before it fell in"; "new glass at the station and new pails."** **Chronicle: "a small building where a man weighed the patrons' milk, took a sample of it, tested that sample for butter fat, separated the milk, sent the cream down to Larrow Green and gave the skim back to the farmer."** **Falke: "Milk was weighed in, sampled, tested for butterfat, separated; the cream was hauled down to Larrow Green and the skim returned to the patron."** intake room, loft over it (ladder; **Hazel: "the loft over the weigh room"**; **Duncan: "the loft over the intake room… Twenty feet of ladder, thirty-five years, and two of the three sessions of that arbitration sat in the room underneath it"**; **Cleve: "over the intake room there was a loft. You got at it by a ladder nailed to the wall by the door, not a stair, a ladder, and the trap at the top of it was a piece of plank with a rope on it. Nothing much lived up there. Some sacks, a broken churn, wire, the kind of thing a building collects"; "the crate was up the ladder over their heads the entire time. Twenty feet, if that"**; **Chronicle: "the loft over the old station's intake room"; Falke: "the loft over the station's intake room"**), wash room, cold room, boiler, separator; **the weigh room** (Frick: sessions two and three held there; Hazel: Emmet "would stand in the doorway of that weigh room with his hat on"; Kepp: "men shouting at one another across a weigh room"; **Cleve: "in the weigh room — that is the intake room, the same room, with the scale in it"**; **Orra: "Two of the sittings held up here at the station itself, in the weigh room, with the men standing outside in the road"**); **the platform** (Ansel "would not… leave them on the platform" — Orra 1919); **built "in the winter of 1895 and 1896" (Falke)**; opened 1896 (Sill: May, "a crowd went up for it"; Duncan: Ansel there "from the year it opened"; **Falke: "The station opened on the 4th of May, 1896"** — the date of the test book's first leaf); Frick: "milk weighed in, a sample drawn for each patron, the cream sent down the valley and the skim milk handed back"; **closed 1946 (Cleve: "The station closed in forty-six. There was no work left in it by then, the milk went down the valley in a truck by that time and nobody was skimming anything at Ostrey Hollow. They used the building for hay tools after that — mowers, a side rake, that sort of thing, out of the weather. Last I was by there the platform was gone and the roof was going. The ladder was still on the wall")**; shut by 1958. Strawn 1909: wet bottom below and three hill places. Vaught: "one skimming station in Ordell County… below those of its own creamery seven miles down the same valley." Duncan: "the creek" — "any man on the creek," "anybody on the creek." **Chronicle: "the milk of Ostrey Hollow tested lower than the milk of anywhere else in this county — lower than the creamery's own milk, lower than the neighbors'."** **Kyle: "The station up at Ostrey Hollow was opened before my time"; its first season's milk "was weighed at the Hollow and carried on the creamery's own books"; the Hollow's station account was where the station-gain line stood.** **THE KEDDIE PLACE (Orra's diary): the Keddie house stands up the road from the station and within earshot of it ("He came up the road at his ordinary hour"; "I could hear the wagons from the yard all that afternoon"; Emmet "came up… stood in the yard"; Ansel "broke the path to the station himself at four o'clock"; walked "both ways" when the mare was lame); "the station road" with wild strawberries along it (July 1897); "the home piece" (cut July 1897), "the low piece" ("under water again," May 1911), "the lower place" (whence the hired girl, 1910); the shed (apron "behind the shed door"); the cow shed "which has wanted doing since his father's time" (1925); six apple trees "below the house" (1914); hollyhocks; the yard; the door where Ansel said "something in the glass" (Duncan) and where Duncan listened at five (Orra, 1899); "that station… He has been in this hollow the whole of his life"; the Loomis gate and the stairs "at the far end of the hollow" (1927).**
- **Hessel Bottom** — Roy: "a hundred and sixty acres of wet meadow lying under the hill at Ostrey Hollow. **Six farms** had rights on it" (Effie: seven farms; Grigg's 1908 report: "six farms cut hay and turn out stock… a wet meadow lying along the creek under Ostrey Hollow… springy the year round and… under water in part every spring… sedge, rush, and the coarse sour grasses… hay is cut late and heavy and is fed as the main dependence of those farms from November to April"; **Kepp 1926: "Hessel Bottom, under Ostrey Hollow, is the completest example of wet meadow in this county. Six farms cut hay and pasture stock upon it. It lies along the creek; the springs come out at the foot of the slope and run in it the year round; a part of it is under water every spring and is not fit to be walked on until well into May. Its hay is sedge hay, and on those farms it is the winter dependence and very nearly the whole of it"**; **Hazel: "Six. Hessel Bottom was six farms and no more, and it was wet — you could cut hay off it in a dry August and in a wet one you could lose a horse… There was a spring at the upper end that ran cold enough to ache your wrist in July, and that is where the cans stood"**; Hazel: "Nine families, six farms, if you are counting — a farm can hold more than one family and generally did"; **Chronicle: "a valley of six farms on a wet bottom"**; **Falke: "the farms of that end, Hessel Bottom principally"; "the hay and pasture of Hessel Bottom, which is wet ground"; "The bog meadows of that bottom are known to every farmer in the county for the coarseness of their hay"**; **Orra: the 1919 circular went "to eleven families" (Conflict 187); Emmet's place "the biggest"; "the bottom fed his father and it will feed him"; the Brant kitchen "where I was a girl"; Roy "at the gate" (1929)**). Brant's sixty-odd acres / "the largest piece" / Duncan "the largest place on Hessel Bottom." Roy: "a soft place by the alders that has taken a horse to the hock." Jerome Cudd: "Good milk off wet ground." Frick: "the bog meadow at Hessel Bottom." Grigg's remedy: "Drain that bottom, sweeten it, keep the stock off it in the wet of the spring, and feed the coarse hay off it with a mixed ration and not by itself." Kepp's remedy: "Drain the bottom; open the old ditches and cut new; lime the ground and lime it again; sow it down when it will carry a seeding; keep the stock off it in the wet of the spring, when a hoof does more harm in a day than a season will repair; and never feed that hay alone. Feed it with a mixed ration and a mineral supplement, as the College directs." Cleve: "Hazel Brant up at Hessel Bottom."
- **Ashlin** — Association office (over a harness shop; Duncan: "the Cooperative office at Ashlin, where it [the test book] lay from 1925 until 1959"; **Cleve: the hearing "started… down at Ashlin, in the Cooperative's office, because that is where the lawyer was and lawyers do not travel"**; **but Kyle puts "the Cooperative's office" at Larrow Green — Conflict 15**); board of arbitration 1925 (Frick: first session there 17 March 1925; the corridor; **Chronicle: "There was a hearing at Ashlin"**; **Falke: "a board of arbitration sat at Ashlin from the 17th of March to the 4th of May under the chairmanship of an attorney of that place"**; **Kyle: "the sittings were partly at Ashlin and partly at the station"**; **Orra, 17 March 1925: "A. went down to Ashlin this morning in his good coat… There is a board of men to hear the whole thing out"**); Duncan Keddie taught school there ("the better part of my life"; **Orra, October 1911: "Duncan gone to the school at Ashlin to teach, seventeen and looks fourteen"; Christmas 1919 "up from Ashlin"**) and wrote his 1968 notes there ("this town"; **Chronicle: Ivy "grew up in this town, where her father taught school for many years"**); the Historical Rooms ("down the street" from Hazel's house — Hazel 1981; Duncan: "the Ordell County Historical Rooms in this town, where it is now"; **Falke 1959: "the Historical Rooms at Ashlin, where they [the surviving records] now are"**); Nym: crate found there Feb 1958; Alonzo Frick of that town; **Dr. Roland Kepp "of Ashlin"** (**Falke: "a veterinarian of standing at Ashlin"**); Hazel Brant recorded there 1981 (Roy "drove down to this town" for the hearing); the merchant before 1889; "the Ashlin Voses" (Duncan); the "two women in this town" who repeat the Vose-cousin talk (Duncan); **Kyle: the nephew story is "whatever is said at Ashlin"**; **The Ashlin Chronicle, the county weekly (3 Oct 1958: "Ashlin Girl Made Good," "The girl from Ashlin"); Orra's three diary notebooks "bought at Ashlin."**
- **Ordell County / the Ordell County Historical Rooms / the Ordell circuit** (four counties, fifty-odd plants); **the Ordell County Bar Association** yearbook (Frick 1946); **the Ordell County Farm Club** (Kepp's paper 1926, secretary, chairman); **the board of supervisors of Ordell County and the county agricultural agent's office (Falke 1959; "the county's permanent file," "the county's dairy papers")**; **the Ordell County Historical Society (Kyle 1953)**. Duncan: Ansel died "in this county." **Chronicle: "Old Ordell"; "the theories in this county always are [about the men]"; "That answer… should not satisfy anybody in Ordell County."** **Junia: "The house sold nothing into Ordell County before 1900. That county lay behind the ridge and traded east, out of Farland and the towns beyond it."** **Falke: Larrow Green's 3.78 is "an ordinary figure for this county and for the counties adjoining"; "the state dairy inspector for this circuit."**
- **Redlow Ridge** — four miles of switchback; shuts November to April/May (Rosalie); Rundle: "over Redlow Ridge in Vessey County"; Hazel: "over the ridge… to the cheese factory on the far side"; Duncan: "the cheese company over the ridge"; Chronicle: "the cheese company over the ridge."
- **Alder Corners, Vessey County** — the Alder Corners Cheese Company (1913/1914), "on the flat below the church"; spring water; hard road east; make room; little room off the intake; platform with scale. Its book to the Vessey County Historical Society 1970 (Nym Item 13; Duncan 1970: "Mrs. Cudd's test book has this year gone to the Vessey County Historical Society"; "The book itself has never been out of Vessey County"). Chronicle: Rosalie "went into her family's records." Falke, Kyle and Orra never mention it.
- **Farland** — the State Agricultural College (Ivy's; Vaught's — **Chronicle: "the college's department of dairy husbandry"; "a college laboratory" with "a calibrated burette"; the college press release**; **Falke: "the State Agricultural College at Farland… the department of dairy husbandry"; "the library at Farland" (from which Falke obtained Bulletin 214); pipettes measured "against a calibrated burette at Farland"**; Strawn's notebooks from 1947 "in the library of the State Agricultural College at Farland" — Duncan; Bulletin No. 214 issued there — Grigg: "a College that had never heard of Hessel Bottom"; Kepp: "The State Agricultural College at Farland conducted a feeding trial upon bog hay and published it as its Bulletin No. 214"; **Falke: "the feeding trial upon bog hay which the college conducted in 1911 and published as its Bulletin No. 214"**). "away east" (Vira); "back east" (Hazel); "east to Farland" (Duncan); **Chronicle: "carried the crate east to Farland"; Junia: Ordell County "traded east, out of Farland."**
- **Tarnet** — Ammon & Sons (Hazel: "The crate came out from Tarnet in ninety-nine"; **Chronicle: "a dairy supply house at Tarnet"**; **Junia: "four stories and a basement on the street behind the railroad yard at Tarnet, with the sidings close enough that a case of glass went from our packing room to a freight car without touching a street… the noisiest building on that block"; "this city"; territory "along the two railroad lines and hardly a mile off them"; churns and vats "came from the works at the coast"**; **Kyle: "the supply house at Tarnet," to which the 1923 replacement order went "that same week."**).
- **"the home place"** — Vose Jerseys. **"the creameries below us"** — Selby. **"the two ends of this valley"** — Rundle. **"the man over the hill"** — Frick. **"the hill herds" vs "the bottom herds"** — Kepp. **"three buildings"** (Duncan 1970: the five records "are now in three buildings and are none of them ours" — the Ordell County Historical Rooms, the Farland library, the Vessey County Historical Society). **The Oram barn (Cleve: Keddie "hung a barn door for us and it swung true for thirty years").** **The Ammon packing room, blowing floor, "the old third floor" (burned 1931), lehr, cullet barrel (Junia).** **"the other place… out on the bypass," "the garage," "the cafeteria down on one" — the surgical-waiting distractor, NOT story.** **"elsewhere" — Falke's word for where the 1911 borrowed measure came from.** **"the store" (Orra, March 1902: "Talk at the store that our milk up here runs poorer than theirs down the valley" — which store, Hollow or Green, unstated); "the Loomis gate," "stairs at the far end of the hollow," "half the doors in this hollow" (Orra, 1927); "the family's hands" (where the three notebooks are, 1964).**

---

## TIMELINE (consolidated)
- **1854** — Emmet Brant born (Roy). **1861** — **Ansel Keddie born (Duncan; Orra: "A carpenter at sixty-six" in October 1927).** **1866** — **Orra Brant born (her diary's editor; her own "I am sixty-four" in August 1930) — vs Duncan's 1868.** **c. 1870** — Hobart Sill born. **c. 1872** — Alonzo Frick born. **c. 1885** — **Bertram Kyle born (sixty-eight in 1953).** **c. 1888** — Roy Brant born. **c. 1889** — **Ammon & Sons founded by Marcus Ammon ("sixty years" by 1949); Junia Ammon born (sixty in 1949).** **c. 1896** — Kepp begins practice (thirty years by 1926).
- **1889** — Association organized at Larrow Green (schoolhouse, cold weather, a horse got loose; **Falke: "organized in 1889 by farmers of this valley meeting at Larrow Green"**); Gideon Vose first president. 1889–92 minute book lost in the 1938 move (**Falke: "the minute book of the Association's first years was lost in an office removal"**). **Ansel Keddie and Orra Brant married (Duncan; Orra's editor: "married Ansel Keddie in 1889") — the same year.** Chronicle: organized "before this century began."
- **1891** — creamery built at Larrow Green (**Falke: "built its main creamery there in 1891"**); churn set up; cash monthly. Rundle: "Article VII dates from 1891"; men left over it.
- **1892** — Article VII adopted (Effie); pamphlet undated (Nym; **Falke: "The printed by-laws carry no date upon their face, only the words 'adopted at the annual meeting'… this office is unable to state the year of adoption and does not attempt to"**). Grigg: "Payment by test came in with the 1893 meeting."
- **1893** — Hobart Sill comes to Larrow Green.
- **1894** — **Duncan Keddie born (Duncan; Orra: "five years old" in September 1899; "seventeen" in October 1911).**
- **1895** — Keddie goes up to ready the station (Vira; **Hazel: "went up to that station in ninety-five"**). **Winter 1895–96 — the station built (Falke: "In the winter of 1895 and 1896 the Association built a skimming station at Ostrey Hollow").**
- **May 1896** — station opens with a crowd; own outfit not come; Sill's glass sent up; **4 May 1896** book begun ("Glass borrowed of Larrow Green until our own comes. A. Keddie."); **Falke: "The station opened on the 4th of May, 1896."** 1896 average 3.80. Sill: ~900,000 lb May–December (Nym: no figure; **Kyle: "a good eleven hundred thousand pounds… what I understood at the time from men who were there. It is not in any book I ever ruled" — the first season's milk "carried on the creamery's own books, and the sheets for it were never put with the station papers"**). Duncan: Ansel "tester and separator man… from the year it opened"; test book "from 1896 until he left." **Kyle: the station "for its first season or two… borrowed its testing glass from the creamery here."** **Orra (1919, quoting Emmet): Emmet "had hauled whole milk to that station since the day it opened."**
- **1 January 1897** — Brant weigh book begun (Nym; Ivy; Frick's exhibit; **Duncan**). **Roy: 1 January 1898. Hazel: 1 January 1900.** **4 April 1897 — Orra's diary begins ("Ground still frozen a spade down. Set the eggs… the mare is lame").**
- **14 April 1897** — Ammon & Sons invoice ($77.93; **Junia reproduces it and writes "seventy-seven dollars and thirty-nine cents"; Chronicle "seventy-seven dollars and thirty-nine cents"**). **1 May 1897** — station's own glass in use (Vaught §III: "the first of May, 1898"; **Junia: "put that outfit into use at its Ostrey Hollow station on the first of May, 1897"; Chronicle: "went into use at the station in the spring of 1897"**; **Kyle: "its own outfit came from the supply house and went into use on the first of May, 1898. I have that from the correspondence, which I handled later when it was all got out for the hearing"** — 1898 has two sources, Vaught and Kyle; **Orra, 1 May 1897: "A. says the station's own glass came up and they are done borrowing of Larrow Green" — a same-day diary entry for 1897, the strongest evidence yet for 1 May 1897**). Sill: own glass "came the spring after," his returned with one piece chipped. **Hazel: "The crate came out from Tarnet in ninety-nine."** **Junia: "The house sold nothing into Ordell County before 1900."** **19 July 1897 — Orra: wild strawberries along the station road.**
- **1898** — average 3.61; first full year; Teague's typical entry 57,760 / 60,800 / 3,040 lb. **1898–1922** — 25 years, 40,000,000 lb; 76,000 lb gain; $19,000. **3 February 1898 — Orra: "Twenty below at the door… A. broke the path to the station himself at four o'clock… the milk comes in with the frost still on the cans."**
- **12 September 1899 — Orra: "Duncan five years old and reads anything set before him."** **1900** — **Cleve Oram born at Larrow Green.**
- **1901** — Effie secretary. **1902** — first complaints (Roy; Rundle; Grigg; Sill's shouting man; **Falke: "The first complaint of record was made in 1902. It was renewed, in one form or another, in every decade thereafter"**; **Orra, March 1902: "Talk at the store that our milk up here runs poorer than theirs down the valley. A. came home and would not eat… it is my figures they will look at"**). Kepp: "complained for something like a quarter of a century" (by 1926); Chronicle: "said so at every annual meeting for a quarter of a century."
- **6 June 1904 — Orra: currants; rain every afternoon at four.** **1905** — Teague bookkeeper. **1907** — Strawn takes the circuit (**Falke: the inspector "tested with the lactometer at every visit from 1908 forward"**).
- **Winter 1907–08 / Jan 1908** — committee of three under Nyle Grigg; sampling "January, February and March" (report) / "through the whole of 1908" (Grigg 1919) / "through the winter" (Roy, Rundle, Keddie); wet-hay report "received and accepted"; Kepp 1926: "A committee of practical men went over the ground in 1908 and found the herds sound, the milk whole, and the scales honest, and laid the cause to the hay of that bottom." Chronicle: "The committee that sat on the matter in 1908." **Falke: "A committee of the Association sampled the Hessel Bottom herds in 1908 and reported to this effect."** **Orra, January 1908: "Four men up the road today in Grigg's sleigh, come to look into the herds. They went to my brother Emmet's first, being the biggest, and he gave them their dinner. They were civil enough to me. They wanted to see the barns and not the station, which A. remarked on afterward and let alone"; March 1908: "The committee gone back down. Their word is that it is the wet hay of the bottom that thins the cream" — the diary supports the January–March sampling.**
- **1909** — **Cleve Oram, nine, begins riding the cream wagon regularly.** **1909, 1912, 1915, 1916, 1917, 1919, 1920 (×2), 1921, 1922** — Strawn entries as before.
- **1910** — Brant 200,000 lb even, test 3.42 (Roy; Nym; **Hazel**); butterfat 24 cents (Roy); Vose Jerseys 4.56. **30 August 1910 — Orra: threshing, fourteen men to dinner, "a girl up from the lower place," two pies short.**
- **1911** — Gideon's presidency ends (**Kyle: "Mr. Selby Vose became manager in nineteen eleven"** — vs 1913). 8 May: pipette broke; borrowed measure; tests 3.80 (**Orra, 8 May 1911: "A. home cross about a broken measure. Rain all day, the low piece under water again"**). June: Strawn "Nothing here to note"; Roy: "The state man left his own measure that June." 10 July: own measure back; 3.61; "? — A.K." (no diary entry). **Nine weeks** (Keddie, Ivy, Roy, Vaught; **Falke: "On the 8th of May the station's own measuring glass was broken, and a measure borrowed from elsewhere was used in its place for nine weeks, until the 10th of July. Through those nine weeks the station's tests stood markedly higher. On the day the station resumed its own glass they fell back to their former level"**) vs **six weeks** (Teague; **Kepp: "in the summer of 1911 the testing at that station was broken off for six weeks, their own apparatus having failed them and a borrowed one being used… Six weeks of June and July"**). **Duncan: Ansel "came home that summer of 1911 saying it was something in the glass… said it twice, at supper and again by the door"; Duncan seventeen.** **October 1911 — Orra: "Duncan gone to the school at Ashlin to teach, seventeen and looks fourteen. His father drove him down and came back without a word in him."** Bulletin No. 214 (bog hay) — Grigg: "three years after we set our names to it"; **Falke: "the feeding trial upon bog hay which the college conducted in 1911."** Cleve (riding since 1909) says nothing of 1911.
- **1913** — Selby Vose manager (Effie; Strawn "New manager"; **Kyle 1911**). Alder Corners built (Rosalie) / opened 1914 (Roy; Rundle). **April 1914 — Orra sets out six apple trees below the house.**
- **1915** — four Hessel Bottom men begin hauling to Alder Corners in the summer flush (to 1930; sixteen seasons). **1916** — Teague told to let the account alone. **1917** — Gideon Vose dies. **November 1917 — Orra: "Snow before the corn was in. Everything late this year and everything short."**
- **1919** — Grigg's report reprinted with his covering letter; the circular ("water in the cans") to nine families (Hazel: "nine letters… answered by none of them"; her mother put hers in the stove; Vira's father in the desk drawer; **Chronicle: "a warning to the Ostrey Hollow patrons about added water as early as 1919"**; **Kyle: "In nineteen nineteen there was the circular. I typed it and I remember typing it… Mr. Vose signed it, and it went out over his name and no one else's"**; **Falke: "the Association's manager, who circulated a warning to that effect in 1919"**; **Orra, June 1919: "A circular came to the station for the patrons, over the manager's name, about water in the milk. A. would not put them in the mail nor leave them on the platform. He took them round himself, to ELEVEN families, and stood at the door while each man read it, and came home at eleven at night with his hat still on"**); Brant/Keddie silence begins (Vira, Keddie, Roy — **and Orra: "June 1919, two days after. My brother Emmet came up. He did not come in. He stood in the yard… He went home. He has not been in this house since, and he is my brother"** — but see Duncan's "thirty-six years" and Hazel's "forty years"); Strawn: no water; Jerome Cudd's 1919 entry 3.79/3.81/3.78/3.82; Rundle: six years of the valley's two ends not speaking. **Christmas 1919 — Orra: "Duncan up from Ashlin for two days. A good day and I will write it down as one."**
- **1921** — burettes issued (Strawn). **Hazel: "The state man condemned that glass in twenty-one."** **February 1921 — Orra: "Ice storm. The station road impassable two days and the milk stood."** **1922** — Keddie's summary leaf; Strawn skips the burette check. **Kyle: "in twenty-two the state man came into the office… he had condemned the whole lot of them… Which we [ordered] that same week, to the supply house at Tarnet, and the new glass came up within the month."**
- **12 June 1923** — Strawn condemns the crate (Selby, Rundle: 12 July; Hazel: 1921; Kyle: 1922; **Junia: "in the summer of 1923… with a burette"; Chronicle: "condemned that year by the state's dairy inspector on his rounds"; Falke: "On the 12th of June of that year the state dairy inspector, in the course of an ordinary circuit visit, ran the station's milk pipettes against a calibrated burette and found that they would not deliver their mark. He condemned the whole lot on the spot and directed the manager to order new"**); **28 June** order (**Junia: "The Association wrote to us on the twenty-eighth of June"; the order was "for six dozen milk pipettes and six dozen test bottles"**; Kyle: "that same week"); **2 July** Ammon letter (**quoted by Junia; Chronicle: "They answered by return, entered the order, regretted the trouble"**); July new glass ("will go forward this month… we shipped in the month" — Junia; Kyle "within the month"; **Falke: "New glass was in use from the following month"**; **Orra, July 1923: "Hay in good and not a drop on it. A. says they have new glass at the station and new pails, and the roof is still bad"**); crate to the loft (Ivy June 1923; Duncan "in 1923"; Chronicle "since 1923"; Cleve "put up there when the state man made them throw it out"; **Falke: "where it had lain since the day it was condemned"**); October "They deliver their mark."
- **1924** — station 3.80 (Selby 3.90; **Falke 3.85: "From 1924 the station's average test stood at three eighty-five and remained there"**); Alder Corners 3.80; test book's last entry 31 December 1924. **Kyle puts the award "in twenty-four."** **November 1924 — Orra: "Killing weather. A. up before four every day of his life… never once been late."**
- **Jan 1925** — petition ($19,600 on 44,000,000 lb — Rundle; 67,000 lb — Frick; **Falke: "The patrons petitioned in January of 1925"**; **Orra, January 1925: "The patrons have put in a paper against the station. My brother Emmet's name is on it. I do not blame him and I will not say so to A."**). **March 1925** — test book impounded (Nym; Duncan). **17 March 1925** — first session at Ashlin (Cleve: "in the Cooperative's office"; **Falke: "a board of arbitration sat at Ashlin from the 17th of March to the 4th of May"**; **Orra, 17 March 1925: "A. went down to Ashlin this morning in his good coat, which he has had since Duncan was married. There is a board of men to hear the whole thing out. He was up at three and did not sleep before that either. I ironed the coat twice for want of anything I could do"**); Keddie's statement. Sessions two and three at the station's weigh room (Duncan: "two of the three sessions of that arbitration sat in the room underneath" the loft; **Cleve: "three sittings of it altogether and two of them were held up at the station itself, in the weigh room… I stood outside on the platform the whole of one of those afternoons, in the rain, with about a dozen others"**; **Kyle: "partly at Ashlin and partly at the station"**; **Orra, April 1925: "Two of the sittings held up here at the station itself, in the weigh room, with the men standing outside in the road. I did not go down. I could hear the wagons from the yard all that afternoon and that was enough of it for me"**). **30 April 1925** — Keddie put out (Frick; Duncan: "four days before the board handed down what it called its finding"; **Falke: "The tester was dismissed on the 30th of April, 1925, four days before the award"**; **Orra, 30 April 1925: "They have put him out. Twenty-nine seasons and a letter. He came up the road at his ordinary hour with his apron rolled under his arm and hung it behind the shed door and asked what there was for supper. There was ham. He ate it"**). **4 May 1925** — award $3,800 (Vira, **Cleve**: $3,500; **Falke $3,800 "without admission"; Kyle $3,800 "without admission of any cause… charged to the station account"**; **Orra's diary: "[The award of the board is not mentioned in the diary. — Ed.]"**). Fall 1925: new tester. **June 1925 — Orra: "He has begun on the cow shed, which has wanted doing since his father's time. He works at it from light to dark and whistles, which he never did when he was paid."** **Hazel: "The hearing was in twenty-six." Cleve: "The hearing was in twenty-five and I was twenty-five years old, which is how I keep the date." Kyle: "That was in twenty-four."** Duncan: Emmet at the Keddie table "for thirty-six years" up to 1925. **Cleve: "Keddie went home after twenty-five and stayed home."**
- **1926** — burette program ends; Selby's address; **Dr. Kepp's paper before the Ordell County Farm Club** ("The matter has been before committees since and before boards, and it is not settled"; **Falke: "a veterinarian of standing at Ashlin wrote to the same purpose in 1926"**). **Orra's diary for 1926: "[Several leaves torn out. The editor has not attempted to supply them. — Ed.]"**
- **October 1927 — Orra: "A. mending stairs at the far end of the hollow, and the Loomis gate, and half the doors in this hollow, and taking what they offer. A carpenter at sixty-six… it pays a quarter of what the other did."** **1928** — loft "full since before the war." **1929** — Emmet Brant dies; book to Roy (Roy; Nym; Duncan; **Orra, spring 1929: "Word this morning that my brother Emmet is dead. I went down. Roy met me at the gate and was kind… Ten years and neither of them would go up the road first"**). **July 1929 — Orra's hollyhocks; Ansel "named the ones my mother had."** **1930** — last Alder Corners summer. **August 1930 — Orra closes the diary: "I am sixty-four and my hand is not what it was. I have kept this book thirty-three years."**
- **1931** — **Ivy Keddie born (Duncan).** **Ammon & Sons' works records burn "in the fire that took the old third floor" (Junia).** **1931, 1934** — Strawn entries; **1934 Keddie dies** (Ivy; Duncan: "nine years after" the dismissal; Chronicle 1934; Cleve "in the thirties… I went to the funeral"; **Orra's loose leaf, February 1934: "A. died this morning, at home, in the forenoon, without much pain at the end of it. Duncan came up in the night. There were more at the burying than I looked for, and men shook my hand who had not spoken to him in nine years"**); Sill retires. **March 1936** — Strawn's last round; retires; notebooks to the state dairy office (Duncan).
- **1938** — office move; Selby's management ends; **Kyle: "then the office was moved and I was not moved with it."** **1939** — Rundle's pamphlet. **1940** — Effie lays down the minute book. **March 1941** — Jerome Cudd dies; Rosalie takes the factory and the Alder Corners book (Duncan); Effie's "Notes."
- **1945** — Teague retires. **1946** — Frick's article. **Station closes (Cleve: "The station closed in forty-six… the milk went down the valley in a truck by that time").** **1947** — Strawn's books to Farland (Duncan: "transferred to the library of the State Agricultural College at Farland"). **1948** — Teague's chapter; **Orra Keddie dies, "eighty-two" (Duncan; "outlived him by fourteen years"; Orra's editor: "she died in 1948").** **1949** — **Junia Ammon's "Sixty Years of Ammon & Sons," Tarnet.**
- **1952–53** — **Kyle hears "the nephew story twice in the last year." 1953 — Bertram Kyle, sixty-eight, writes "What I saw of the office" for the Ordell County Historical Society ("Now everybody who comes to see me wants the Ostrey Hollow line").**
- **Winter 1955** — Roy Brant's account (aged 67).
- **1958** — February: Ivy finds the crate (Hazel: "in the winter, under a tarpaulin and about an inch of chaff"; Duncan: "in February of 1958"; Chronicle: "Last February… under the dust since 1923"; Cleve: "the winter of fifty-eight… brought the crate down herself"; **Falke: "In February of 1958 Miss Keddie found the condemned glass, still crated"**); 9 March: measures nine pipettes (Chronicle: eleven, "in March"; **Falke: "On the 9th of March she measured the nine surviving pipettes against a calibrated burette at Farland"**); borrows the Brant book (Roy's postscript; Hazel's account; Duncan: "lent… and had it back"); Rosalie's letter (Duncan: "she had the figures copied out and sent to Ivy at the college"); copies Strawn's 1923 volume ("eleven years later" than 1947 — Duncan); visits Vira; October: Bulletin No. 471 ("dated October 1958" — Chronicle; **Falke: "a bulletin issued in October of last year"**); **Thursday 2 October 1958: the Chronicle telephones Ivy at Farland; Friday 3 October 1958: "Old Ordell Wrong Righted" on the Chronicle's front page ("this week").** Duncan: Ivy's "ten years putting the case right in print" begin. Chronicle: Ivy "twenty-seven." **Orra's editor (1964): "Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958, twenty-four years after his death."**
- **1959** — Ivy's talk; test book and crate to the Rooms (Duncan: "deposited with the Historical Rooms along with the test book"; **Falke: "the surviving records… in the custody of the Historical Rooms at Ashlin, where they now are"**); Rundle's reissue with footnote ("Eleven pipettes"); **the board of supervisors' autumn-quarter meeting directs the county agent; Orin Falke's report "for the county board," in thirteen numbered paragraphs, goes to type; Falke writes to the library at Farland for Bulletin 214, receives it, and adds his note withdrawing the hay concession and correcting "great-niece" to "granddaughter."** **1962** — **Roy Brant dies (Duncan)**; weigh book to Hazel (Nym; Duncan). **1963** — Hazel gives it to the Rooms (Nym; Duncan; Hazel 1981: "now it is down the street at the historical rooms in this town where anybody may look at it who wants to, which is nobody"). **1964 — Orra Keddie's diary extracts published as "A Farm Woman's Book, 1897–1930," from "the three notebooks now in the family's hands," with an unnamed editor's preface and notes.** **1968** — **Duncan Keddie's "Notes for the family," Ashlin, aged seventy-four (never mentions the 1964 diary).** **1970** — Alder Corners book to Vessey County Historical Society (Nym; **Duncan's 1970 addition: "the five books are now in three buildings and are none of them ours"**). **1972** — Nym's notes. **Spring 1977** — **Cleve Oram taped at the Larrow Green feed store, aged seventy-seven ("now every second car that comes up the valley has somebody in it with a notebook").** **1979** — Vira's tape. **1981** — **Hazel Brant's tape at Ashlin.**

---

## SOURCE 1 — Vira Toland (recorded at Ostrey Hollow, 1979)
Tape at her kitchen table by "a young man from the county who asked three questions in an hour."
- Born 1912, top end of Ostrey Hollow, house her father's father put up; two years at Ashlin when first married.
- Father hauled milk; **nine families**; "in and out of each other's kitchens."
- **Ansel Keddie ran it**; went up in '95; "tested that first summer too."
- The book: ruled with his own straightedge, figures "like print," slope desk by the window; "a thousand cans a week and never a blot"; father: "there would be no lawyers in Ashlin."
- **1919**: "the year everything went sour"; she was seven; printed letter over the manager's signature: "water in the cans coming down from Ostrey Hollow"; nine families; father read it twice with his hat on; mother at the stove with her back turned; letter in the front-room desk drawer; brother has it; one woman pinned hers inside her trunk lid; "forty years to prove what was said of them." It rained that day.
- Signer **Selby Vose, old Gideon Vose's nephew**; "the family always thought the valley belonged to them a little." **Orra Keddie and old Mrs. Vose first cousins.**
- **Orra** = Ansel's wife, Emmet Brant's sister; Emmet "the one that letter cost the most"; Sunday dinners before; after, "not one word, not at the store, not at a funeral"; Orra walked to Larrow Green rather than pass his lane.
- "All at once, one summer, the figures came up and stayed up, and nobody ever said why."
- Hearing at Ashlin "the winter and spring after"; "they put Ansel Keddie out"; **"paid the patrons thirty-five hundred dollars… my father's share of it did not buy a hay rake."**
- Her verdict: "He was taking the cream… nobody ever came up that road and looked." Father: "an honest man who had got into something."
- Keddie died in the thirties "still under it"; Orra outlived him.
- 1958 visitor: young woman from the college away east; "Hazel Brant's first cousin once removed… of the family both sides."
- "I have never in sixty years been able to think about the one without the other."

## SOURCE 2 — Effie Loomis, "Notes toward a history of the Association" (1941)
1. Organized 1889; Gideon Vose president 1889–1911; she 14 in 1889; secretary 1901; 1889–92 book lost 1938. 2. Creamery 1891; before it "every man's arithmetic was his own." 3. **Article VII** (quoted): *"Every patron shall be paid for the milk he delivers according to the number of pounds of butter fat contained in it, ascertained by test, and not according to the weight of the milk; and the test of the station where he delivers shall govern."* Adopted 1892. "the hinge of the whole Ostrey Hollow business." 4. Officers: Effie 1901–1940; Teague from 1905; Hobart Sill buttermaker; Selby Vose, "son of the late president," manager 1913–1938. "Not one of us ever went up the valley… I include myself first." 5. Station seven miles up; Hessel Bottom wet meadow; **seven farms**; largest Emmet Brant's. 6. January 1925 petition; board under Alonzo Frick, **17 March to 4 May 1925**; "Three explanations were argued… None of the three was accepted." Award (quoted): *"The board having heard the patrons and the officers, and having had before it the books and appliances of the station, finds no cause established. The board awards to the Ostrey Hollow patrons the sum of thirty-eight hundred dollars ($3,800.00), without admission, to be charged to the station account."* 7. 25 years: **40,000,000 lb**, 1,600,000/yr; fat **25 cents/lb**. 8. Not in the record: the lost book; **the crate** — "never mentioned in the hearing at all"; "The interesting part is generally what none of them thought worth saying."

## SOURCE 3 — Ansel Keddie: statement (17 March 1925) and unsent letter (spring 1925)
**Statement** (nine sentences): book in his own hand since opening; columns date/patron/pounds/test/fat; errors lined through with his letters; "never written a figure over a figure"; nobody "has come up that road on a working morning and stood at my elbow"; "I am an old man and I have no other trade"; **"I have tested as I was taught and as the state man saw me do it, thirty-one times a week for twenty-nine seasons, and I cannot tell you why the figures are what they are"**; "I have never taken a pound of cream out of that station."
**Letter**: day began half past five; every can weighed, called and written; scale state-tested and true. Numbered jars, ten-day composites; warmed, poured vessel to vessel. Charge drawn "to the mark on the stem… tip wiped… blown out into the bottle"; acid down the side; four bottles in the hand tester; read with dividers. **"The glass I began on in ninety-six was borrowed off the Ashlin creamery"** (first leaf says Larrow Green). "Twenty-nine seasons, from 1896 through 1924. I did not test a pound in the season of 1925." Summary leaf quoted: *"Average test of all patrons, this station: 1896, 3.80. 1897, part year. 1898, 3.61. And 3.61 or within three hundredths of it every year since, save the nine weeks of 1911 that are noted in their place, when it stood at 3.80."* 1911 entries: *"May 8. Pipette broke this morning. Using a borrowed measure."* / *"July 10. Our own measure in use again from today. Tests are back where they were. ? — A.K."* "That question mark is mine and I have let it stand for fourteen years." Gap the same in July and February. 1924: 3.80. History: 1902, 1908 (wet hay), 1919 (nine kitchens have the letter in a drawer). "My wife Orra is Emmet Brant's sister… He and I have not spoken since the circular." **"I was put out on the thirtieth of April… both of those things are true at once."**

## SOURCE 4 — Ivy Keddie, "What the Crate Held" (1959)
- Five records: station test book; Brant weigh book from 1897 (Roy Brant); Alder Corners figures copied by Rosalie Cudd ("Jerome Cudd's niece"); Strawn's notebooks (inspector "from 1908"; Farland since 1947); the annual ledgers.
- Crate: February 1958, station loft, under a tarpaulin; stenciled AMMON & SONS DAIRY SUPPLY TARNET; carried up June 1923; **six dozen** pipettes bought 1897; outfit **$77.93**; **nine unbroken**.
- 9 March 1958 sheet (quoted): *"Nine pipettes from the Ostrey Hollow crate, stems blown 17.6 c.c. Delivered against burette, mean of five runs each: 16.70, 16.71, 16.72, 16.72, 16.72, 16.73, 16.73, 16.74, 16.71. Mean 16.72 c.c. Short 0.88 c.c. That is 95 hundredths of the mark, and the mark is what the neck of the bottle was ruled for."*
- 16.72 ÷ 17.6 = 0.95; 3.61 ÷ 0.95 = 3.80; gap 0.19 = 5% of 3.80; "It lost a twentieth of whatever was there."
- 1898–1922: 40,000,000 lb; 1,444,000 lb credited at 3.61; 1,520,000 at 3.80; **76,000 lb**; **$19,000**; award $3,800 "a fifth of it."
- Herd at 4.56 short 0.24; at 3.42 short 0.18. 1911 "an experiment… nobody knew it was one"; borrowed measure "came up from Larrow Green with the cream wagon"; "twenty-six-year anomaly." Bulletin 214 quoted; "Four times too small and it is the wrong shape." Alder Corners: **fourteen seasons** overlapping; ~0.19 higher there. Bulletin 471 last paragraph quoted. 35 years glass-to-cause; 33 years dismissal; dead 24 years. "I have only the crate."

## SOURCE 5 — Wilbur Teague, "The Books of the Association," Chapter Nine (1948)
Books 1905–1945; office over a harness shop; station sheets in March. "The station-gain line was my own device; I began it when I came in 1905." Entry (quoted): *"Ostrey Hollow: butter fat credited to patrons on station test, 57,760 lb. Butter fat received at Larrow Green in station cream, 60,800 lb. Station gain, 3,040 lb, five and a quarter per cent, as usual. Carried to the general account."* — "the entry for 1898." "A figure that comes in at five and a quarter every year is furniture." **Forty-four million pounds**; average test **3.16**. 1911: borrowed measure **six weeks**, own "came up from town"; "a small charge for replacement." Evidence to the board: "Cream is portable, and that station was in one pair of hands for twenty-nine seasons… The remedy is two men or a lock." $3,800 "balanced." "I put it to Mr. Vose in 1916 and was told to let it alone… I was the Association's bookkeeper and not its conscience."

## SOURCE 6 — Merle Strawn, "Extracts from twenty-eight circuit books," covering note (1936)
Preface: *"Copied out this spring at the request of the state dairy office… I have copied what is in them and put in nothing that is not."* Circuit 1907–1936; fifty-odd plants in four counties; scales, lactometer, boiler/water/cold room, watching the tester. Entries: **1907** scales true, tester "slow and clean." **1909** boiler; wet bottom + three hill places; rusted seam. **1911** June "Nothing here to note." **1912** no water; cream shipped same day. **1913** new manager to the platform. **1915** wash-room floor. **1916** tester complains of blame. **1917** cheese factory next county; Larrow Green short-handed. **1919** circular; "never found any, and I told him so." **1920** twice; ice. **1921** burettes issued. **1922** did not run the glass. **1923 June 12** (quoted): *"Ostrey Hollow. Scales true. Lactometer, no added water, as always here. Ran their milk pipettes against the burette. These measures will not deliver their mark. Condemned the lot, the whole crate of them, and told the manager to order new. Glass is glass and I did not weigh what it has cost them."* **1923 October** new glass delivers its mark. **1924** 3.80. **1925** new man, quick. **1926** burette program discontinued. **1928** loft full since before the war. **1931** bad ice year. **1934** separator bearing; book "not in the hand it used to be kept in." **1936 March** last round. "I do not keep an estimation, I keep a book… A theory is a thing a man carries out to the platform with him and it gets in the way of the weights." Names no person.

Creamery Assn., for the Ostrey Hollow station: 6 doz. milk test pipettes, 17.6 c.c., at .35 — 25.20 / 12 doz. test bottles, 8 per cent neck, at .22 — 31.68 / 1 acid measure — .90 / 1 hand tester, 4 bottle — 18.00 / Freight — 2.15 / Total — 77.93"*; in use from 1 May 1897. **3** Station test book "kept by E. Brant"; half-bound, marbled boards, back mended with cloth; four columns; one hand, 29 seasons; impounded March 1925; Ashlin office 1925–59; received 1959. **4** Farm weigh book "kept by A. Keddie"; small quarto, sheepskin, greasy corners; daily from 1 January 1897; 1910 leaf quoted; to Roy 1929; lent to Ivy 1958; to Hazel 1962; received 1963. **5** Crate, deal, nine pipettes blown 17.6, two chipped at the tip; condemned 12 June 1923; found in the Association office at Ashlin Feb 1958 by Ivy; mean 16.72; "wants a case with a lock." **6** Annual ledgers; 40,000,000 lb; 76,000 lb; $19,000; "five and a quarter percent of the credited fat is five percent of the fat delivered." **7** Minute book of the board, 17 March–4 May 1925; award quoted. **8** Keddie's statement. **9** Photostat of Strawn's 1923 notebook. **10** Ammon & Sons letter 2 July 1923 (order of 28 June; "declining to say anything of the earlier lot"). **11** 1908 report, fair copy signed by the chairman (closing paragraph quoted). **12** Bulletin No. 214. **13** Vessey County correspondence file (1919 figures; book founder → daughter 1941 → Vessey County Historical Society 1970). **14** Ivy's sheet. **15** Bulletin 471. **16** Larrow Green receiving and churn books, water-damaged; a little under 0.2 better. Queries: no 1896 intake figure; Item 5 wants a lock.

## SOURCE 8 — Selby Vose, annual address (1926)
"Thirteen years this spring, since 1913." 1925 wet into June; station "ran its season out and its cream came down to us daily and sweet." Board under Frick "an able and impartial man"; "found no cause established… against the station, against the office, or against any officer or servant"; $3,800 "a generous settlement of an unproved claim." **"Water was going into cans at the head of that valley and it stopped when the Association began to look. The rise of 1924 is the measure of it… rose by twenty-one hundredths and the average for 1924 stood at 3.90."** The state man "every year or two… condemned the station's testing apparatus on the twelfth of July, 1923… We ordered new inside the month… Apparatus wears… I decline to build a mystery on a piece of housekeeping that appears in our own order book with a date and a price against it." "I do not know a name and I would not use it if I did." Defends the circular: "It named nobody. It accused nobody. It said that this Association pays for butterfat and cannot pay for creek water." Father's Jerseys 4.56 in 1910, "gone now." "Bring it as it left the cow… keep the dog out of the milk house… I ask for one more."

## SOURCE 9 — Roy Brant, account for the county farm bureau (Hessel Bottom, winter 1955; postscript 1958)
- "My father was Emmet Brant. He was born in 1854 and he died in 1929. From the first of January, 1898, until the week before he died, he wrote down every can of milk that went out of this barn, and I have kept it up since, in the same book until it was filled and then in another."
- The Bottom: 160 acres; six farms; Brant's sixty-odd acres, thirty-eight cows; water stands into May; soft place by the alders; hay coarse, cuts late.
- The book: "a ledger, a plain one, ruled by hand. Two columns for each day… In the first column my father entered the weight of every can as he took it off his own scale… In the second he entered the figure the station gave him back… three or four lines in thirty years, and no opinion at all. The cover is gone off the front, the back is held by a strip of harness leather my father tacked on himself, and the first forty leaves are stained brown at one corner where it lay in a leaky window a winter he was sick." "The two columns agreed… Whatever was wrong at that station… it was not the weighing."
- 1910 leaf (quoted): *"Year 1910. Delivered to the station, two hundred thousand pounds even, by my own scale and theirs agreeing. Paid on a test of 3.42."* "he wrote 'even' and underlined it, which for him was carrying on." Butterfat 24 cents that year; ~25 cents overall.
- Kitchen table, second milking, lamp, hat on.
- **The trouble**: "seventeen hundredths of a point under… Set the slips in a row for a lifetime of farming and it is a farm." First said out loud 1902. "seventeen hundredths in July and… in February. It did not come and go with the season."
- 1908: committee under Nyle Grigg; father: "the land had been wet since the flood and the tests had not."
- 1911: nine weeks; "The state man left his own measure that June"; rise and fall entered; "there it sat for near on fifty years."
- 1919: "the manager sent a paper round… Nine families… My father was one who never forgave it. He and Ansel Keddie were brothers-in-law, and from 1919 they did not speak… because my father held that the man who kept the test book ought to have stood up in front of that circular and did not."
- Alder Corners: four of the Bottom men from 1915, sixteen seasons; factory opened 1914; "talked about here as though they had gone over to the enemy… I did not go over."
- Family: Orra = father's full sister ("same mother and same father, whatever you may hear from people who like a story with a stepmother in it"). "Duncan Keddie, who taught school at Ashlin, was Ansel and Orra's boy, and he and I are first cousins. My daughter Hazel and his daughter Ivy are second cousins… both of them have had to have it explained to them by me more than once."
- "the book has outlived two of the three men who are in it. It goes to Hazel when I am done with it."
- Postscript 1958: Ivy borrowed the book; "It comes back to me when she is through, and after me it is Hazel's."

## SOURCE 10 — Rosalie Cudd, letter to Ivy Keddie (Alder Corners, Vessey County, 1958)
"Dear Miss Keddie"; "Yours very truly, Rosalie Cudd." Ivy's letter "came Tuesday"; "In thirty-odd years nobody from over the ridge has ever asked me a plain question about our books." Factory built 1913 by her father Jerome Cudd "on the flat below the church"; he died March 1941 "between the setting and the cutting of a vat"; she took over that spring, "seventeen years now… changed the boiler and the roof and nothing else"; "a girl of twelve when the first vat was set." Ridge: four miles of switchback; shuts November–April/May. Four men from 1915, ~1 June to mid-September, sixteen seasons, last 1930; cans by seven; the boy weighed on the platform. Father tested himself, own bench and glassware and book; "never compared a figure of his with anybody else's." 1919 entry (quoted): *"The four Hessel Bottom men, summer of 1919: 3.79, 3.81, 3.78, 3.82. Good milk off wet ground, whatever they say over the ridge."* The ghost saying: "the Ordell people had a theory about that bottom the way some families have a ghost: it explained things, and it cost nothing, and nobody was ever going to test it." Water: "A cheesemaker knows watered milk inside a day… Cheese is a witness"; never docked one of them. **Table — Summer averages, the four Hessel Bottom patrons**: 1915 3.79 · 1916 3.81 · 1917 3.78 · 1918 3.80 · 1919 3.80 · 1920 3.82 · 1921 3.79 · 1922 3.78 · 1923 3.81 · 1924 3.80 · 1925 3.79 · 1926 3.82 · 1927 3.78 · 1928 3.80 · 1929 3.81 · 1930 3.79. Invites Ivy in summer.

## SOURCE 11 — Leland Vaught, introduction and method to Bulletin No. 471 (Farland, October 1958)
§I: fifty years of asking; "answered here by reference to a piece of glass"; **"the milk pipette is the one instrument in the butterfat test which the plant cannot check against anything else."** §II: standard charge 17.6 c.c.; error proportional, precise, invisible from inside; "A pipette that delivers 16.27 c.c. where it is blown 17.6 delivers 16.27 c.c. every time." §III: **five dozen** bought 1897; nine recovered; mean **16.27 c.c.**; glass in use "the first of May, 1898" to June 1923. §IV: 1911 nine weeks; "an experiment… that ran itself… and lay unread for forty-seven years." §V: 0.9244; 3.905; shortfall 0.295; **118,000 lb**; "not readily reconciled with the station's own average of 3.80 in the year following." §VI: Bulletin 214 "Five hundredths in winter; nothing at all in summer… some six times." §VII (quoted): *"No milk was watered at Ostrey Hollow and no cream was stolen there. From the first of May, 1897, to the twelfth of June, 1923, the station's tests were low by one part in twenty because its pipettes were short by one part in twenty, and the difference — seventy-six thousand pounds of butter fat — stands credited in the Association's own ledgers, year by year, as station gain."*

## SOURCE 12 — Dell Rundle, "The Larrow Valley Creamery: Fifty Years" (1939; reprinted 1959)
Sections: TO THE MEMBERS; I. THE MEN OF 1889; II. THE RULE THAT MADE THE TEST THE MASTER; III. THE STATION AT OSTREY HOLLOW; IV. THE TROUBLE AT THE HEAD OF THE VALLEY; V. OVER THE RIDGE (footnote 1959); VI. FIFTY YEARS; NOTE ADDED WHILE IN PRESS. "What follows is not a record. It is what the valley remembers, tidied." I: schoolhouse meeting, horse got loose; Gideon Vose "heavy, quiet, obstinate"; by-laws at his kitchen table; "His nephew Selby Vose"; 1891 creamery, "cash once a month." II: Article VII quoted; "Article VII dates from 1891"; "the pound of milk is a liar where the pound of butterfat is not"; "Gideon Vose put that in for convenience." III: seven miles; three hours in mud; the building; the creek over the sill twice. IV: 1902 complaints; 1908 Grigg committee; 1919 warning; six years' incivility; "It is the settled opinion of the valley that cream left that station by the back door, and the figures went right when the man who kept it did not"; inspector condemned glassware 12 July 1923; petition January 1925; **44,000,000 lb; $19,600 "to the dollar"**; board under Frick "heard everybody… found nothing established… made an award… without admitting anything." V: Alder Corners opened 1914 by Jerome Cudd; "a few of our Hessel Bottom men"; "It is Jerome Cudd's daughter, Rosalie Cudd, who makes the cheese at Alder Corners now." Footnote 1959: *"The Hollow matter has since been taken up again at the college at Farland by Miss Ivy Keddie, who is second cousin to Hazel Brant of Hessel Bottom. Eleven pipettes out of the station's old condemned crate were found still to be in existence. I am too old to rewrite a pamphlet and I refer the reader to the college. — D.R."* VI: "fifty Junes… fifty Januaries." In-press note: the station-gain entry quoted verbatim; "**Cream stolen at the Hollow would have reached the Green light. It reached the Green heavy, every year, and I cannot make my paragraph stand. I leave it as written and correct it here.** — D. RUNDLE, Larrow Green."

## SOURCE 13 — Alonzo Frick, "Recollections of the Ordell Arbitration" (Bar Association yearbook, 1946)
"at seventy-four I take him to mean me… none of them has stayed with me as the Ostrey Hollow matter has." The complaint: average test **3.16**; "when the man over the hill is paid on a better figure for milk out of cows no different from yours." Petition January 1925; "the officers… did not resist it"; board of three, Frick chairman "because I was the one of the three who kept no cow." Three sessions: Ashlin 17 March 1925; two at the station's weigh room ("We saw the scales tried. We saw the sampling done and the test made… I am a lawyer and the thing is chemistry"). Three explanations: water (Selby Vose); cream theft by the tester (Teague, obliquely); hay (1908 report read into the record). Keddie's statement; "I believed him then and I believe him now"; the corridor remark. Dismissal 30 April, "four days before we made our award." Award 4 May 1925, $3,800; "The patrons' own reckoning of what they had lost was sixty-seven thousand pounds of butter fat." Exhibits (quoted): *"1. The test book of the Ostrey Hollow station, 4 May 1896 to 31 December 1924, in the hand of Ansel Keddie. / 2. The receiving book of the said station. / 3. The private weigh book of Emmet Brant of Hessel Bottom, begun 1 January 1897. / 4. The circuit notebook of the state dairy inspector for the year 1923."* "Four books and nothing else." "I have wondered since whether the answer was in that room and simply was not in any of the three stories."

## SOURCE 14 — Hobart Sill, remarks on the day he left the Larrow Green creamery (1934)
Forty-one years at the churn; came 1893 (plank floor, creek ice). "That cream ran heavy. Every year it ran heavy. There was more fat in the cans than the papers said had been bought up there… Always more. Never less." Weighed it himself in the cold room; "my hand on every page of it from ninety-three." Explains it as cream's nature: "So it ran heavy. So does everybody's." Larrow Green **3.78**; the Hollow "really a **3.85** and no better… somebody up that hill road was helping the cans along." "You cannot tell me **nine families** all got honest in one summer… one summer, along in the twenties, those figures came up and they stayed up… Something got stopped. Something got said to somebody." 1902 shouting man; 1919 circular "the only plain thing that office ever did." "**Twenty-seven cents.** That is what fat was through those years." 1896: made up an outfit from his own glass for the station; returned the spring after, one piece chipped. 1896 intake "about **nine hundred thousand pounds**." "I never went up there but twice." "The cows were never the trouble." "Put in that Hobart Sill made the butter and it was good butter and he stood at the churn until the day they gave him a chair."

## SOURCE 15 — Nyle Grigg, covering letter of 1919 to the reprinted 1908 report
Letter "Larrow Green, 1919": question "reopened this season by men who were boys when we made it"; "eleven years have brought us some light"; grumbling from about 1902; "we three were named… I have never in my life been given a duty I liked less"; "**Payment by test came in with the 1893 meeting**"; "bought hay off two of them"; "**We sampled those herds through the whole of 1908 — every month of that year**"; not a patron of that station; "abused for the finding by men on both sides of it"; Bulletin No. 214 "found the bog hay to depress the butter fat test by **two tenths in the winter feeding**… three years after we set our names to it"; remedy: drain, sweeten, keep stock off, mixed ration; "abuse the man who reads their figures for them, and they will be grumbling in twenty years' time." **THE REPORT AS PRINTED IN 1908** (full text): "appointed in January last"; six farms; wet meadow description; visited each farm three and four times, drew and sealed samples, saw herds milked, cans filled, hauled and weighed in, "stood by while the test was made"; tried station scales against a known weight and patrons' scales against the station's; inquired into each barn's water supply; herds "thin in the flank at the end of the winter feeding… coats… rough"; "what such land gives in quantity it withholds in quality"; "we found no dishonesty of any kind, in any quarter, and we wish that finding to be printed in the same type as the rest"; "sampled the herds of Hessel Bottom through January, February and March. The milk is whole and the scales are honest… the wet hay of that bottom is what thins the cream, and… the remedy lies with the land and not with the station. NYLE GRIGG, chairman, and the committee." "received and accepted, and the question was closed by it for eleven years."

## SOURCE 16 — Dr. Roland Kepp, V.S., "The bog meadows and the milk," paper read before the Ordell County Farm Club (1926)
*"A veterinary surgeon of thirty years' practice brings the agricultural literature to bear on an old local grievance, and the discussion from the floor is appended as taken down by the club's secretary."* Headed **THE BOG MEADOWS AND THE MILK**, *By Roland Kepp, V.S., of Ashlin*.
- Opening: "it is my habit, when I am called to a herd that is doing badly, to ask first what the cattle are standing on. I have found in thirty years that the answer to nine cases in ten is under the animal's feet, and that the tenth is in the water she drinks." The question "has been in this county longer than I have practiced in it, and… has been dealt with hitherto by opinion, by grumbling, and by the naming of names. It deserves better. It is a question of soil, of herbage, and of the mineral economy of the cow."
- **The land**: "A wet bottom is not a rich meadow with too much water in it. It is a distinct kind of ground with a distinct kind of chemistry… the year's growth does not rot down as it does on a hill field; it lies sour, and the soil that results is an acid soil, poor in lime, and poor in the phosphates… crowded out within a few seasons by sedge and rush and the coarse tussock grasses… That herbage carries bulk and it carries water. What it does not carry… is lime or phosphorus, and it is cut late because such ground cannot be got onto early, and late cutting takes out what little was in it."
- **What the cow does**: "A cow in milk is a manufactory working on a narrow margin… If her ration is short of the minerals, she does not simply give less; she robs her own frame to make good… The stock come out of the winter feeding thin over the loin and rough in the coat. They are slow to come to condition in the spring. They are ready subjects for every disorder of the digestion in the fall of the year… They breed later. Their feet are worse." "They will milk, gentlemen. Wet land is generous of flow and no man on a bog meadow ever complained that his pail was light. But what is in the pail is another matter, and the fat is the first thing to suffer, because the fat is the dearest thing the cow makes and it is the first economy she practices when she is pressed."
- **The measure**: "**The wet bottoms of this county yield a milk poorer in fat by a fifth of a point** and every practical man knows it. Set the hill herds of this county against the bottom herds and you will find that difference, farm for farm… the difference does not depend on the breed, on the man, or on the barn. It depends on the ground."
- **Hessel Bottom** (quoted under PLACES): six farms; springs at the foot of the slope; under water every spring; not fit to walk on until well into May; sedge hay "the winter dependence and very nearly the whole of it."
- **The complaint**: "The patrons of the skimming station at Ostrey Hollow have complained for something like a quarter of a century that their milk tests below the milk of the rest of the valley. Their complaint is perfectly just and I have never doubted it. **The figure of the gap, as it has been given to me and as I have satisfied myself, is twenty-one hundredths of a point — 0.21** — between the milk of that station and the milk of this valley at large. A committee of practical men went over the ground in 1908 and found the herds sound, the milk whole, and the scales honest, and laid the cause to the hay of that bottom. The matter has been before committees since and before boards, and it is not settled, and it will not be settled by men shouting at one another across a weigh room. It is settled already, in the literature, for anybody who will read."
- **The College**: "The State Agricultural College at Farland conducted a feeding trial upon bog hay and published it as its Bulletin No. 214… it found the butter fat test depressed by **two tenths in the winter feeding**. That is the whole case. Two tenths from the College, laid alongside the fifth of a point I have found in the field and the twenty-one hundredths that station has been complaining of, and the three agree so nearly that I do not know what more a reasonable man requires."
- **An objection**: "It has been urged upon me, by a gentleman who I know will forgive my noticing it here, that **in the summer of 1911 the testing at that station was broken off for six weeks, their own apparatus having failed them and a borrowed one being used, and that the figures stood higher during those six weeks than before or after. Six weeks in the flush of the season, gentlemen. Six weeks of June and July**, when every herd in the county is at its best and the stock are off the wet ground and up on the aftermath. I am astonished that anybody should found an argument on six weeks of summer figures. I could produce you six weeks in any year that would prove anything you liked."
- **The remedy** (quoted under PLACES). "**Fat has been worth twenty-seven cents the pound through these years**, and a fifth of a point taken off every hundredweight a farm ships, year in and year out, will pay for a great deal of tile."
- **DISCUSSION**: *A member* asked whether the difference might be in the cows rather than the land, "the bottom farms being poorer men with poorer stock." *Dr. Kepp*: considered and rejected it; "some of the best-bred cattle in the county stand on wet land, and some very indifferent cattle on the hills test well; and… a man may improve his herd for twenty years and never get his fat up while his ground is sour." *A member* asked whether the trouble might lie in the station rather than the bottom, "as had been said in the valley for years." *Dr. Kepp*: "he had no interest in that quarrel and no competence in it; that he had never in his life read a test and would not know how; but that when a cause is found in the soil, in the herbage, in the condition of the stock, in the College's trial, and in every wet bottom in the county alike, it is not good sense to go looking for a second cause in a building." *The chairman* thanked Dr. Kepp; the paper "would be printed for the members."

## SOURCE 17 — Hazel Brant, "farmer's daughter and last keeper of the weigh book," recorded at Ashlin (1981)
*"Taped at her kitchen table over two afternoons; the interviewer's prompts are in brackets. She had no papers in front of her and did not want any."*
- "I'll tell you the trouble with me before you start the machine… Quantities I have. Years I have not. I can give you what came off that bottom in a season to the pound, and I could not tell you the year I was married without walking into the front room for the certificate, and I have been married a long while. My father was the same way. He used to say a year was a thing other people kept, like bees."
- **[Start with the book.]** "It is a tall narrow account book, canvas over board, and the spine went soft on the left where he carried it under his arm from the barn to the house in all weather. My grandfather Emmet began it in nineteen hundred, on the first day of January, because he was a man who wanted a clean date under a new habit. He weighed his own milk on his own scale before he ever put a can on the wagon, and then the station weighed it again at the intake, and he wrote both figures down side by side. If the two ever disagreed he would stand in the doorway of that weigh room with his hat on until somebody found out why. They generally agreed. That was the point of him." "Then my father Roy had the book, from the year my grandfather died — don't ask me, I'll only guess — and he kept it exactly the same way for the rest of his life, and then it was mine, and now it is down the street at the historical rooms in this town where anybody may look at it who wants to, which is nobody." The loan to Ivy (quoted under Ivy): "he lent it. He did not leave it to her."
- **[The nineteen-ten leaf.]** "That one I can say with my eyes shut, and it is the only year I am certain of, because it is written at the head of the page in his hand and I have read that page a thousand times. Nineteen ten. Two hundred thousand pounds even, delivered to the station. Even — that is what fixes it… as if the cows had been consulted. His scale and theirs agreeing, he wrote, which was his way of saying that he had checked and was not going to make a speech about it. And paid on a test of three forty-two." "Three forty-two. You set that against what that milk actually was and you have the whole of the business in one line, and nobody set it against anything for fifty years."
- **[How many farms?]** "Six. Hessel Bottom was six farms and no more" (quoted under PLACES; the cold spring at the upper end where the cans stood). "Four of those six men hauled over the ridge in the summer flush, to the cheese factory on the far side, and brought their milk back down to our own station the rest of the year. Four. My father used to say it was the only honest arithmetic anybody in that valley ever did without meaning to."
- **[The difference in the tests.]** "**Nineteen hundredths.** Every month, every season, wet years and dry: nineteen hundredths lower at our station than the same milk was anywhere else on earth. Not a little in the winter and none in the summer, the way a feed thing goes. Nineteen hundredths, flat as a floor. I have heard men who could not add a column of five figures explain to my father why that was the hay."
- **[About Mr. Keddie.]** "Ansel Keddie went up to that station in ninety-five, and he was still there when they turned him out, and in between he tested the milk of every man on that bottom including my grandfather, who was his wife's brother." "That is the part people can't hold in their heads, so I will go slowly. **Orra was Ansel Keddie's wife, and Orra was a half sister to my grandfather Emmet, by his father's second wife.** So Ansel married into us. The man they said was cheating the patrons was married to the sister of the patron who lost the most by it, and ate at his table, and the two of them argued about drainage for forty years." "Then their boy Duncan and my father Roy were first cousins — the children of a brother and a sister, which is what makes first cousins. And Ivy is Duncan's girl, and I am Roy's, so Ivy Keddie and I are second cousins. People are forever telling me she is my niece or my cousin once removed or some such, and she is not. Second cousins. It is not complicated; it is only that nobody wants to count."
- **[The glassware.]** "**The crate came out from Tarnet in ninety-nine**, and it sat up in the loft over the weigh room for most of my life without one soul thinking about it. **The state man condemned that glass in twenty-one** — went at it with some measuring instrument he carried in his coat, said it would not deliver what it was marked for, and told them to order new. They ordered new. Nobody said one word about all the tests that were already in the book."
- "**The hearing was in twenty-six.** My father drove down to this town for it, and he came back and hung his coat up and said the board had heard everybody and found nothing, and that they had let Ansel go, which was the finding whatever they wrote in the book. He never went to another meeting of that Association as long as he lived, and he was a man who liked meetings."
- **[The manager's letter?]** "Oh, the water letter. He sent that round to nine families on that bottom, nine letters, warning us about adding water to the milk. My mother read hers twice and put it in the stove. I was small but I remember the stove." "Nine families, six farms, if you are counting — a farm can hold more than one family and generally did. He sent nine and he was answered by none of them, which in that valley is a shout."
- **[When your cousin found it out?]** "Ivy found the crate up in that loft in the winter, under a tarpaulin and about an inch of chaff, and took the glass back east with her and measured it, and it was the glass. It had always been the glass. Every man on that bottom had been paid short for a generation because a piece of glass would not deliver what was blown on the side of it." "I was glad about it for my grandfather and gladder about it for Ansel Keddie, who did not live to see it, and I will tell you the truth, I was a little sour too. All that arguing, all those men in their good coats. A thing you could have settled in an afternoon with a burette and a straight face, and it took fifty years and a girl from the college who thought to climb a ladder."

## SOURCE 18 — Duncan Keddie, "Notes for the family," written at Ashlin, 1968 (with an addition of 1970)
*"A retired schoolteacher, seventy-four, sets down for his daughter and her children who everyone was and where every book went; the sections are his own."*
- Opening: "I am writing this out because my daughter has spent ten years putting the case right in print and nobody has ever put the family right on paper at all, and because when I am gone there will be no one left who was in the house. I have kept to what I know. Where I do not know a thing I have said so. I have not tried to make anybody in these pages better than he was, and I have not needed to."
- **I. The people.** "My father was Ansel Keddie, born in 1861 and died in 1934. He was tester and separator man at the Ostrey Hollow skimming station of the Larrow Valley Cooperative Creamery Association from the year it opened. He was a careful man in small things to a degree that other people found trying, and it was the making of the record he left and, I have sometimes thought, the unmaking of him." "My mother was Orra Brant, born in eighteen sixty-eight. She married my father in 1889. She was Emmet Brant's sister, the younger of the two, and Emmet farmed the largest place on Hessel Bottom and hauled the most milk into the station of any man on the creek. That fact sat in the middle of our family for a generation and hardly anyone outside it ever noticed. When they said in 1925 that my father had been shorting the patrons, the patron he was said to have shorted worst was his own wife's brother, who was at our table on a Sunday and had been for thirty-six years." "I am Duncan, born in 1894, their son. I taught school at Ashlin the better part of my life, arithmetic among other things, and I have set out the arithmetic of this family below in the way I should have wanted it set out for me. My daughter is Ivy, born in 1931, which makes her my father's granddaughter, and I write that plainly because I have already twice seen it printed otherwise and once heard it said aloud in this town by a man who ought to have known."
