- **II. What the connections are, set out so that they can be copied.** "Emmet Brant and my mother Orra were brother and sister. Emmet's son was Roy Brant, who farmed the Hessel Bottom place after him and kept his father's weigh book. Roy Brant and I are therefore first cousins — the children of a brother and a sister, which is the whole definition and there is nothing more to it." "Roy's daughter is Hazel Brant. My daughter is Ivy. Hazel and Ivy are second cousins: the children of first cousins. Not cousins once removed, which is a different relation and belongs to a different generation, and not aunt and niece, which people say because it is shorter." "Two more, since they are always got wrong. Selby Vose, who managed the Association, was Gideon Vose's son. Gideon organized the Association and was its first president and wrote its by-laws; Selby was his son and not, as I have heard it given, his nephew. And Rosalie Cudd of Vessey County, who ran the cheese company over the ridge after her father, was Jerome Cudd's daughter." "Merle Strawn, the state's dairy inspector on this circuit, was kin to nobody in the valley whatever — not to us, not to the Brants, not to the Voses, not to anybody on the creek. That is worth writing down, because his notebooks turned out to matter, and it matters that the man who kept them had no interest in any of us."
- **III. A thing that is said and is not so.** "There was no connection whatever between us and the Voses. People confused them with the Ashlin Voses, who were no relation to Mr. Gideon either. I have heard it from two women in this town that my mother and Mr. Gideon's wife were cousins, and one of them will tell you first cousins if you let her sit down. It is talk, and it has been talk since before I could walk. My mother would have told me. She told me a great deal about who was who in that valley, being the sort of woman who could not pass a headstone without giving you the connection, and she never once put a Vose in it."
- **IV. My father.** "He was dismissed on the thirtieth of April, 1925, four days before the board handed down what it called its finding, so that the finding found nothing and the dismissal said everything. He lived nine years after it and died in 1934 with the imputation on him, in this county, among men he had weighed milk for since before their fathers died." "He came home that summer of 1911 saying it was something in the glass, and he never dared put it in the book. I was seventeen and I remember the kitchen and I remember that he said it twice, at supper and again by the door, which was not his way. What he wrote in the book was a question mark. He was a man who would not put down what he could not prove, and I have thought since that this was the most expensive virtue anybody in that valley ever had. He had, after all, nothing to hold in his hand but a suspicion about a piece of glass, and against it stood the outfit he had been taught to trust and the practice of every station in the county. So he kept on. He kept on for another fourteen years, and wrote down every figure the glass gave him, and the figures were what convicted him." "My mother outlived him by fourteen years and never spoke of the business except once a year, in April. She was eighty-two when she died in 1948. She had kept every letter and she left them to me, and there is nothing in them that anybody would want, only the weather and the price of things and her brother's health, and I am keeping them anyway."
- **V. The books, and where they went.** "This is the part I chiefly wanted set down, because books go astray and people afterward say that a thing was never written when it was only never found." *The station test book.* "My father kept it in his own hand from 1896 until he left. In March of 1925 the board of arbitration impounded it, and after the hearing it went into the Cooperative office at Ashlin, where it lay from 1925 until 1959. In 1959 it went to the Ordell County Historical Rooms in this town, where it is now." *The Brant weigh book.* "Emmet Brant began it on the first of January, 1897, and kept it until his death in 1929, when it went to his son Roy. Roy lent it to Ivy in 1958 — lent, and I want the word — and had it back, and at his death in 1962 it went to his daughter Hazel, and Hazel gave it to the Historical Rooms in 1963." *Strawn's circuit notebooks.* "He kept one for every year he rode the Ordell circuit. They went to the state dairy office when he retired in 1936, and in 1947 they were transferred to the library of the State Agricultural College at Farland, which is where Ivy found the volume for 1923 eleven years later. Nobody had asked for them in the interval." *The Alder Corners test book.* "Jerome Cudd kept it from the founding of his company; it passed to his daughter Rosalie in 1941 when she took the factory on; and in 1958 she had the figures copied out and sent to Ivy at the college. The book itself has never been out of Vessey County." *The crate.* "The station's condemned glassware went up into the loft over the intake room in 1923 and stayed there until Ivy found it in February of 1958. It went east to Farland to be measured and came back, and in 1959 it was deposited with the Historical Rooms along with the test book. Twenty feet of ladder, thirty-five years, and two of the three sessions of that arbitration sat in the room underneath it." *Added, 1970.* "Mrs. Cudd's test book has this year gone to the Vessey County Historical Society, and the five books are now in three buildings and are none of them ours."
- Duncan gives NO figures for the award, the gap, the milk totals, the number of families or farms, the 1911 duration, the condemnation date, or the water letter; he names Emmet, Orra, Roy, Hazel, Ivy, Gideon, Selby, Jerome, Rosalie, Merle Strawn and nobody else. **He never mentions his mother's diary (published 1964, four years before he wrote), only her letters.**

## SOURCE 19 — *The Ashlin Chronicle*, front page, "Old Ordell Wrong Righted," 3 October 1958
*"A county weekly's feature, put together in a day and a half from a college press release and two telephone calls."* Headline **OLD ORDELL WRONG RIGHTED**; subhead *"College Bulletin Clears Station Tester Thirty-Three Years After He Was Turned Out — Answer Lay In A Crate In The Loft All The While — Ashlin Girl Made Good."* Writer unnamed.
- "There is an old man buried in this county who was put out of his job in disgrace, and this week a bulletin from the State Agricultural College at Farland says he never did anything wrong at all."
- "The bulletin is No. 471, dated October 1958, and it comes out under two names: Professor Leland Vaught of the college's department of dairy husbandry, and Miss Ivy Keddie, a graduate student there. It is Miss Keddie's name that will interest readers of this paper. **The man the bulletin clears is Ansel Keddie, and Miss Keddie is his great-niece.**" "The Chronicle reached her by telephone at Farland on Thursday evening."
- **A quarrel older than the automobile.** "For those too young to have had it argued at them across a supper table, the shape of the thing is this." "The farmers of the Larrow Valley organized themselves into a cooperative creamery association before this century began, and built their main creamery down at Larrow Green. Seven miles up the valley, at the hamlet of Ostrey Hollow, they put up a skimming station: a small building where a man weighed the patrons' milk, took a sample of it, tested that sample for butter fat, separated the milk, sent the cream down to Larrow Green and gave the skim back to the farmer. Every patron in the valley was paid on that test. Not on the weight of his milk — on the fat in it, as the test found the fat." "Ansel Keddie ran that station and made those tests. And from very nearly the day the station opened, the milk of Ostrey Hollow tested lower than the milk of anywhere else in this county — lower than the creamery's own milk, lower than the neighbors', lower year in and year out, in a valley where the cows were no different and the pasture was no worse." "Everybody had a theory. The theories were about the men, because the theories in this county always are. The Ostrey Hollow farmers thought they were being cheated and said so at every annual meeting for a quarter of a century. The officers of the Association thought the Ostrey Hollow farmers were up to something, and said so rather more quietly. Nobody, in all those years of it, appears to have thought the trouble might be furniture."
- **The hearing, and the man who paid for it.** "Matters came to a head in the twenties. The manager of the Association had circulated a warning to the Ostrey Hollow patrons about added water as early as 1919. The patrons, for their part, wanted their money. There was a hearing at Ashlin, and there were charges against the tester, and at the end of it Ansel Keddie was dismissed after a lifetime at that station." "**The committee that sat on the matter in 1908 sat again in 1925** and awarded the patrons their money — thirty-eight hundred dollars, 'without admission,' which is lawyer's language for we are paying you and we are not saying why. Ansel Keddie got nothing, and was not asked back, and died in 1934 with the whole valley's opinion sitting on him." "He had been dead twenty-four years when the bulletin appeared this week, and out of his job for thirty-three."
- **A crate in the loft.** "Here is the part that will be told in this county for the next fifty years, and it deserves to be." "Last February Miss Keddie went up a ladder into the loft over the old station's intake room and found a wooden crate that had been sitting there under the dust since 1923. In it was the station's original glassware, condemned that year by the state's dairy inspector on his rounds — he ran their measures against a burette, found they would not deliver what was marked on them, and told the manager to order new ones. He wrote down that he had condemned them. He wrote down nothing about what they might have been doing for the twenty-six years before." "Miss Keddie carried the crate east to Farland. **There were eleven pipettes in it in a condition to be measured**, and in March she measured them, one by one, against a calibrated burette in a college laboratory." "Every one of them was blown on the stem 17.6 c.c. — the standard charge of milk for a butter fat test, the charge the graduated neck of the test bottle is ruled for. Not one of them delivered it. **The average of the lot came to 16.27 c.c.**" "That is the whole of the mystery. A short measure of milk goes into a bottle whose neck is marked for a full one, and the fat reads low in the same proportion, and it reads low every single time, for as long as you go on using that glass. Nobody watered anything. Nobody stole anything. The scales were true, the acid was right, the cows were ordinary, and a piece of glass out of a crate was wrong."
- **What it cost.** "The bulletin works out what the Ostrey Hollow patrons were never paid for the butter fat they actually hauled to that station, and the figure is **nineteen thousand six hundred dollars ($19,600)** — in a valley of six farms on a wet bottom, in the money of the eighteen-nineties and the years after." "Set beside it the price of the trouble. The Association's whole testing outfit for that station — pipettes, test bottles, an acid measure, a hand tester and the freight on the lot — was bought new from a dairy supply house at Tarnet in 1897 for **seventy-seven dollars and thirty-nine cents**." "Six dozen pipettes came in that crate, at thirty-five cents apiece, and they went into use at the station in the spring of 1897. They were still in use in 1923." "The Chronicle has seen the invoice. It is a tidy document in a clerk's copperplate, ruled and totaled, and it is one of the more expensive pieces of paper this county has ever had in it."
- **And the house that sold them?** "Ammon & Sons, of Tarnet, are still in business and were in business in 1923, when the Association wrote to them for replacement glass. They answered by return, entered the order, regretted the trouble, and said that their books of the nineties would not tell anybody anything about the lot in question." "That answer will not satisfy this newspaper and it should not satisfy anybody in Ordell County. **A cheap short lot was put off on the Association knowingly, and the firm's silence in 1923 is the proof of it.** A house that blows glass to a mark knows what its glass delivers. A house that cannot say what became of a lot it sold is a house that would rather not."
- **The girl from Ashlin.** "Miss Ivy Keddie is twenty-seven, took her first degree at Farland and stayed on for the graduate work, and grew up in this town, where her father taught school for many years. She has spent the better part of this year on it, in her own time and largely at her own expense, and the college's press release is generous about it in the way press releases are." "She is careful to say that the work was not hers alone. The bulletin's acknowledgments name Professor Vaught; the state dairy office, for the loan of an old inspector's notebooks; and **Mrs. Rosalie Cudd of Vessey County, of the cheese company over the ridge, who is Jerome Cudd's niece** and who went into her family's records on a stranger's say-so." "But it was Miss Keddie who went up the ladder." "The Chronicle asked her, on the telephone, what she would want said in this county now." *"The glass was short," she said, "and Ansel Keddie was not. He tested the milk in front of the state's own man and he tested it the way he was taught, and the reason his figures would not come right was in a crate over his head the entire time. I would like it printed that he never knew. It would have been a kindness if somebody in this county had thought to climb a ladder."*
- The Chronicle names Vaught, Ivy, Ansel, Rosalie, Jerome, Ammon & Sons; the manager, the inspector, the lawyer, Duncan ("her father"), Hazel, the Brants, Strawn, Frick, Grigg go unnamed.

## SOURCE 20 — Junia Ammon, "Sixty Years of Ammon & Sons," chapter nine of the firm's privately printed history (Tarnet, 1949)
*"The firm's own historian, sixty, writes up the glassblowing floor and reproduces two papers out of the office files in facsimile."* Headed **IX. THE CREAMERY TRADE, AND ONE LOT OF PIPETTES**.
- "Marcus Ammon founded this house on a rented floor with two men and a secondhand annealing oven, and by the time I was old enough to be a nuisance in it, Ammon & Sons, Dairy Supply, filled four stories and a basement on the street behind the railroad yard at Tarnet, with the sidings close enough that a case of glass went from our packing room to a freight car without touching a street. I have heard it said in this city that we were the noisiest building on that block. We were. Glass is not quiet and neither were the men."
- "At our height we supplied half the creameries in the state. Not half the churns and half the vats — those came from the works at the coast — but half the *glass*: the test bottles, the pipettes, the acid measures, the burettes, the little thermometers that never survived a season. A creamery orders a churn once in twenty years. A creamery orders glass forever, because glass breaks, and that is why a house like ours could live sixty years on a trade nobody outside it has ever heard of."
- **How a pipette is made**: "A milk test pipette is a simple thing and it is not an easy thing. A man gathers his glass, blows the bulb, draws the stem down, and works to a gauge that hangs on a nail at his elbow. He is making a vessel that must deliver a stated quantity of milk — not hold it, *deliver* it, which is a different measurement and a harder one, because milk is not water and clings to the wall of the glass as it runs out. The mark goes on the stem while the glass is still warm. Then the piece goes into the lehr to cool slowly, and comes out the next morning, and is gauged, and is either packed or thrown into the cullet barrel under the bench, and no man on that floor was ever thanked for what went into the barrel."
- **Records**: "Packing was done by the dozen, in straw, in a wooden case with the customer's name in blue pencil on the end. Everything that went out of this building went out against an entry in the order book, and everything made on the blowing floor went into the works book — what glass, what gauge, what man, what day. Those works books would answer a great many questions if we still had them. We do not. **The works records of this house burned in 1931, in the fire that took the old third floor**, and what is left to us of the nineties is the office side only: the invoices, the letter books, and the day books of what was owed."
- **Territory**: "**The house sold nothing into Ordell County before 1900.** That county lay behind the ridge and traded east, out of Farland and the towns beyond it, and it was a standing complaint of Mr. Marcus's that we could not get a foothold there while the freight rate stood where it did." "Our territory in those first years ran along the two railroad lines and hardly a mile off them. A drummer for a glass house does not walk; he steps down at a depot with a sample case in each hand and calls on whatever creamery is within sound of the whistle, and if the association's manager is out in his fields that day the drummer sits in the office and waits, because the next train is tomorrow. The creamery trade of the nineties was won a depot at a time." "It was a good trade all the same. The cooperative creamery movement went through this state in those years like weather. Farmers who had been making butter in their kitchens formed associations, put up a creamery on the creek, and then put up skimming stations at the head of every valley that had cows in it, and every station wanted a full outfit at once: a hand tester, bottles, pipettes, an acid measure, and freight. We sold a great many outfits in those years and they went out very much alike." "The lots were numbered and the numbers were entered, and I want it understood that this was not a slack house. Mr. Marcus had a saying that a creamery buys arithmetic from us and not glassware, and he had it lettered and hung in the packing room, where I suppose it did as much good as such things do."
- **The invoice** ("I reproduce here entire, both because it shows the form of our billing and because of what came of it afterward. It is dated the fourteenth of April, 1897"): *"Sold to the Larrow Valley Co-op. Creamery Assn., for the Ostrey Hollow station: / 6 doz. milk test pipettes, 17.6 c.c., at .35 — 25.20 / 12 doz. test bottles, 8 per cent neck, at .22 — 31.68 / 1 acid measure — .90 / 1 hand tester, 4 bottle — 18.00 / Freight — 2.15 / Total — 77.93"* (word for word Nym's Item 2). "**Seventy-seven dollars and thirty-nine cents.** That is what a station's whole outfit came to in 1897, freight and all — six dozen pipettes marked 17.6 c.c., twelve dozen bottles, the acid measure, and a four-bottle hand tester that a man turned with his own arm. The Larrow Valley Association put that outfit into use at its Ostrey Hollow station on the first of May, 1897." "I would not be writing this chapter about that invoice if the story ended there."
- **1923**: "Six and twenty years afterward, in the summer of 1923, an inspector for the state went into that station with a burette and put their pipettes to it, and the glass out of our crate would not deliver its mark. He condemned the lot — the whole crate of them — and told the manager to order new. The Association wrote to us on the twenty-eighth of June. Our answer of the second of July is in the letter book and I set it down as it stands:" *"Your order of the 28th ultimo for six dozen milk pipettes and six dozen test bottles is entered and will go forward this month. We regret the trouble. Our books of the nineties are not such that we can tell you anything about the lot you name."* "That was the truth and it was also, I am obliged to say, a thin thing to send to a customer of twenty-six years' standing. We entered the order, we shipped in the month, and we said what we could say, which was nothing."
- **Her theory**: "I have thought about that lot for a long while, and here is what I believe happened, and I set it down because nobody else is going to. **The mold had been reground and the lot ran short. It was an accident and nobody in the works caught it.** A gauge sits on a nail and gets knocked off the nail and hung back on it; a mold comes back from the shop a hair different and looks exactly as it looked before; a boy checks the first of a lot and the tenth and the fiftieth and finds them consistent, because they are consistent — they are consistently wrong, which is the one fault a check of that kind cannot find. That is how it goes in a glass house, and any man who has stood on a blowing floor will tell you the same."
- "What I regret is not the accident. Glass is made by people. What I regret is the letter, and that we had nothing better than the letter to send, and that when a customer of a quarter century wrote to this house and asked in effect what had been done to him, the answer he got was that our books would not say." "I put both papers in this chapter, the invoice and the letter, so that they stand together where they belong. Sixty years is long enough that a firm may print its own bad morning as well as its good ones."
- Junia names nobody in the valley — not Keddie, not Strawn, not Selby; only "the Larrow Valley Association," "its Ostrey Hollow station," "an inspector for the state," "the manager."

## SOURCE 21 — Cleve Oram, cream hauler's son, recorded at Larrow Green (1977)
*"Taped in the back room of the Larrow Green feed store, spring of 1977. Mr. Oram was seventy-seven. His father drove the cream wagon down from the Ostrey Hollow station for better than twenty years, and he rode on it from the time he could sit up. Transcribed as spoken."* (Found as "Chapter 10" of the long-21 miscellany.)
- "I was born in nineteen hundred and I have not lived five miles from this chair since. So if it is the road you want, you have come to the right man. Everybody wants the Ostrey Hollow business now. Nobody wanted it for fifty years, and now every second car that comes up the valley has somebody in it with a notebook."
- **The road and the wagon**: "My father hauled the cream. That was his living. Two bays and a flat wagon with a rack built onto it to keep the cans standing, and a canvas over them in July, and in the winter nothing over them at all because they would not freeze in the time it took. Seven miles from here up to the Hollow, and it climbs the whole seven. There is a pitch about two miles below the hamlet, past where the creek comes in from the north, that took the horses at a walk with an empty wagon and took them at a crawl with a loaded one. In March you went up through mud to the hub and came down through it again, and the ruts froze at night in the shape your wheels left them, so that the second week of March was worse than the first. My mother used to say she could tell the month by how my father swore coming in the door." "I began going up with him regular at about nine years old. That would be nineteen and nine. You sat on the box on his left because the ditch was on the right and he would not have me on that side."
- **The station**: "The station stood on the flat by the creek where the road bends. Board building, two floors, a shingle roof that wanted work the whole time I knew it. On the road side there was a covered platform about waist high, so a man could back his wagon to it and roll the cans off level instead of lifting them. Inside that was the intake room, with the scale and the vat, and the separator going in the back of it, and a smell in there of warm milk and steam and machine oil that I could describe to you better than I can describe my own kitchen. The skim came back out the same platform for the farmers to take home to their hogs. The cream went into our cans."
- **The loft and the crate**: "Now over the intake room there was a loft. You got at it by a ladder nailed to the wall by the door, not a stair, a ladder, and the trap at the top of it was a piece of plank with a rope on it. Nothing much lived up there. Some sacks, a broken churn, wire, the kind of thing a building collects." "And a crate. There was a crate up in that loft with a tarpaulin over it, and I have seen it a hundred times, and I have put my hand on it. It was the station's old glass, put up there when the state man made them throw it out. Bottles, and the little glass measures they drew the milk up with for the test. **Five dozen** of those measures went into that crate to begin with — that was the whole outfit the station was set up with — and there were **nine** of them left in it at the end, because a thing in a loft for thirty-five years gets broken by everybody who goes up after wire. Nobody ever gave it a thought. It was a crate under a tarp. I would not have been able to tell you what was in it if I had not been up there after a hay fork with my own hands." "That crate is the whole thing, they tell me now. Well. It sat there."
- **The hearing**: "The hearing was in twenty-five and I was twenty-five years old, which is how I keep the date. They started it down at Ashlin, in the Cooperative's office, because that is where the lawyer was and lawyers do not travel. But there were three sittings of it altogether and two of them were held up at the station itself, in the weigh room — that is the intake room, the same room, with the scale in it — because the books and the scales and the machinery were all up there and it was easier to carry the men to the machinery than the machinery to the men. I stood outside on the platform the whole of one of those afternoons, in the rain, with about a dozen others. You could hear the voices but not the words. And the crate was up the ladder over their heads the entire time. Twenty feet, if that. I have thought about that since, I will not pretend I thought about it then." "What they gave the patrons at the end of it was **thirty-five hundred dollars**. That is what my father said and my father was not a man who got a figure wrong when it was money. And no admission of anything by anybody, which everybody up the Hollow took the way you would expect a farmer to take it."
- **Keddie**: "They dismissed Keddie. He had been the tester there from before I was born. A long dry man, very quiet, and he wore a canvas apron with the strap over one shoulder, and he had a way of holding a bottle up to the window with his arm straight out that I can see yet. I was not frightened of him and most of the boys were." "Now I will tell you what was said, and you can do what you want with it. My father hauled that cream and he always said there was less of it coming off that platform than went onto it. He said it to me on the wagon seat more than once, and he said it in this village to men who are dead now. He never said it to Keddie's face and he never said it under oath, because he was not asked, but he believed it and he believed it to the end. They say now it was the glass. I have no quarrel with the college. I am only telling you what the man who drove the wagon thought, and he was the one man in the valley who saw both ends of that cream every day of his life." "Keddie went home after twenty-five and stayed home. He kept four or five cows on a piece of ground that was not big enough for them, and he did carpentry — good carpentry, he hung a barn door for us and it swung true for thirty years — and he died in the thirties. I went to the funeral. It was well attended and nobody said much, which tells you something."
- **1958 and after**: "Then the granddaughter came back. That was the winter of fifty-eight, and she went up that ladder and brought the crate down herself, and after that there was a great deal of writing about it. **Sixty-seven thousand pounds** of butterfat was what they made it, over the whole run of years, that the Hollow men were never paid for. Sixty-seven thousand pounds. You cannot picture it. I have hauled cream all my young life and I cannot picture it." "She was a Keddie and she was schooled at Farland. Ivy Keddie. And Hazel Brant up at Hessel Bottom was her people too — Ivy Keddie was Hazel Brant's **first cousin once removed**, that is the way of it, and the two of them not much alike to look at." "And I will say this while I am on families, because you will hear it anyway and you may as well hear it from somebody who is not repeating it out of malice. **The Keddies and the Voses were cousins of some sort.** It is a small valley. My mother had it from her mother and I never heard it contradicted at the time, though I could not draw you the line on paper."
- **The end of the station**: "The station closed in forty-six. There was no work left in it by then, the milk went down the valley in a truck by that time and nobody was skimming anything at Ostrey Hollow. They used the building for hay tools after that — mowers, a side rake, that sort of thing, out of the weather. Last I was by there the platform was gone and the roof was going. The ladder was still on the wall. I looked."
- Cleve names Keddie, Ivy Keddie, Hazel Brant, the Voses, "the college," "the state man," "the lawyer," "the Cooperative"; not Strawn, Frick, Selby, Vaught, Duncan, Roy, Emmet, Orra. He gives no gap figure, no 1911, no water letter, no Bulletin numbers.

## SOURCE 22 — Orin Falke, county agent, "The Ostrey Hollow question: a report for the county board" (1959)
*"Prepared by the county agricultural agent at the direction of the board of supervisors of Ordell County, in numbered paragraphs, with a note added at the end upon receipt of a bulletin obtained from the State Agricultural College after the report had gone to type."* Signed "— O. Falke, county agricultural agent, Ordell County." (A clean standalone file.)
- **¶1.** "At its meeting of the autumn quarter the board directed this office to prepare, for its own use and for the county's permanent file, a plain summary of what is called locally the Ostrey Hollow question. The matter having lately been the subject of newspaper notice, and having been raised with this office by three separate patrons within one month, an orderly statement appears to be warranted. The undersigned has attempted to set down what is established, in the order in which it occurred, and to indicate where the record is thin."
- **¶2.** "The occasion of the present interest is a bulletin issued in October of last year by the State Agricultural College at Farland, prepared jointly by Professor Leland Vaught of the department of dairy husbandry and Miss Ivy Keddie, a graduate student of that department, **who is the great-niece of the tester at the Ostrey Hollow station**. The bulletin is temperate in tone and does not, in the judgment of this office, overstate its case."
- **¶3.** "The Larrow Valley Cooperative Creamery Association was organized in 1889 by farmers of this valley meeting at Larrow Green, and built its main creamery there in 1891. Its by-laws contain the article, numbered seven, under which every patron is paid according to the pounds of butterfat his milk is found by test to contain, and not according to the weight of the milk; and under which the test made at the station where he delivers is the test that governs his pay. The board should observe that this article is the hinge of the whole matter. Under payment by weight nothing that follows in this report would have cost any man a dollar. The printed by-laws carry no date upon their face, only the words 'adopted at the annual meeting,' and the minute book of the Association's first years was lost in an office removal, so that this office is unable to state the year of adoption and does not attempt to."
- **¶4.** "In the winter of 1895 and 1896 the Association built a skimming station at Ostrey Hollow, seven miles up the valley from Larrow Green, to serve the farms of that end, Hessel Bottom principally. The station opened on the 4th of May, 1896. Milk was weighed in, sampled, tested for butterfat, separated; the cream was hauled down to Larrow Green and the skim returned to the patron. The tester and separator man from the opening was Ansel Keddie, who held the post for twenty-nine seasons."
- **¶5.** "From an early date the milk of that station tested low. The station's average over the long period of complaint stood at three sixty-one, where the Larrow Green creamery's own average over the same years was three seventy-eight, which is an ordinary figure for this county and for the counties adjoining. The deficiency, as the bulletin now fixes it, was nineteen hundredths of a point, and it ran at the same nineteen hundredths in every month of the year, in the flush of June and in the depth of February alike. The first complaint of record was made in 1902. It was renewed, in one form or another, in every decade thereafter."
- **¶6.** "In 1911 an incident occurred which the bulletin treats as decisive and which deserves the board's attention. On the 8th of May the station's own measuring glass was broken, and a measure borrowed from elsewhere was used in its place for nine weeks, until the 10th of July. Through those nine weeks the station's tests stood markedly higher. On the day the station resumed its own glass they fell back to their former level. The tester noted the fact in his book and set a question mark against it. The herds, the pasture, the season, the men and the machinery were the same throughout."
- **¶7.** "Three explanations were current in the valley across the whole period, and all three were argued before the board of arbitration in 1925. The first was that the Ostrey Hollow patrons added water to their milk. This was the view of the Association's manager, who circulated a warning to that effect in 1919. It cannot be sustained. The state dairy inspector for this circuit tested with the lactometer at every visit from 1908 forward and never once found added water at that station."
- **¶8.** "The second explanation was that the tester himself removed cream and wrote low tests to conceal the loss. This was urged at the hearing by an officer of the Association. It cannot be sustained either, and the nine weeks of 1911 dispose of it: the same man made the tests in that interval, under no different supervision, and the figures rose."
- **¶9.** "The third explanation was that the hay and pasture of Hessel Bottom, which is wet ground, thin the cream of cows fed upon them. A committee of the Association sampled the Hessel Bottom herds in 1908 and reported to this effect, and a veterinarian of standing at Ashlin wrote to the same purpose in 1926. This office is not prepared to dismiss it. The bog meadows of that bottom are known to every farmer in the county for the coarseness of their hay, and the men who reported in 1908 were practical men who walked the ground and looked into the mangers. Whatever else was at work at Ostrey Hollow, the wet hay of that bottom must be allowed as a real if partial factor, and the 1908 committee was not simply wrong." (This is "the concession made on page four," withdrawn in the note.)
- **¶10.** "The patrons petitioned in January of 1925 and a board of arbitration sat at Ashlin from the 17th of March to the 4th of May under the chairmanship of an attorney of that place. It heard the patrons and the officers, found no cause established, and awarded the Ostrey Hollow patrons three thousand eight hundred dollars, without admission. The tester was dismissed on the 30th of April, 1925, four days before the award."
- **¶11.** "It is necessary to go back to 1923. On the 12th of June of that year the state dairy inspector, in the course of an ordinary circuit visit, ran the station's milk pipettes against a calibrated burette and found that they would not deliver their mark. He condemned the whole lot on the spot and directed the manager to order new. New glass was in use from the following month. **From 1924 the station's average test stood at three eighty-five and remained there.** No person connected the one thing with the other at the time, and the inspector himself drew no inference whatever as to the years behind it."
- **¶12.** "In February of 1958 Miss Keddie found the condemned glass, still crated, in the loft over the station's intake room, where it had lain since the day it was condemned. On the 9th of March she measured the nine surviving pipettes against a calibrated burette at Farland. They delivered materially less than the capacity blown upon their stems, and in the same proportion, one to another. The bulletin's conclusion is that a short charge of milk into a bottle whose neck is ruled for the full charge yields a reading short in the same proportion; that this and nothing else is the explanation; and that no milk was watered and no cream was stolen at Ostrey Hollow."
- **¶13.** "For the board's purposes this office recommends: that the report be filed with the county's dairy papers; that the surviving records be left in the custody of the Historical Rooms at Ashlin, where they now are; and that no action lies for the county, all parties in interest being dead and the Association's account long since closed."
- **Note added on receipt of Bulletin No. 214.** "After the foregoing had gone to type, this office wrote to the library at Farland for the feeding trial upon bog hay which the college conducted in 1911 and published as its Bulletin No. 214, that trial being cited in the 1958 bulletin without its figures. It has come to hand. Its summary is printed here entire:" *"Bog hay fed as the whole of the roughage depressed the butter fat test by not more than five hundredths of one per cent in winter, and by nothing that could be measured on summer pasture."* "Five hundredths in winter and nothing in summer will not carry nineteen hundredths in July. I withdraw the concession made on page four." "I am obliged to add a second correction. I am informed, and have verified, that Miss Keddie is the granddaughter and not the great-niece; the error on page two is mine."
- Falke names Vaught, Ivy Keddie and Ansel Keddie only; gives no pipette measurements (no 16.72 or 16.27), no dollar loss, no pound loss, no milk total, no farm or family count, no crate maker, no award-vs-petition figures; never mentions the Brants, the weigh book, the Cudds, Alder Corners, the station-gain line, Strawn's name, Frick's name, or the Chronicle by name ("newspaper notice"). He gives no figure for Larrow Green's or the county's price of fat.

## SOURCE 23 — Bertram Kyle, clerk (ret.), "What I saw of the office," written for the Ordell County Historical Society (1953)
*"Written at sixty-eight, at the Society's request, by the man who sat at the outer desk in the Cooperative's office at Larrow Green for twenty-five years, outside the manager's door."* (A clean standalone file.)
- "The Society has asked me for the office, and the office is what I can give it. I cannot give it the cows. I saw perhaps four cows in twenty-five years, and two of those were in a photograph."
- "I came in as a boy on the copying and I went out with my own desk, which stood in the outer room with its back to the window and its face to the manager's door, so that everything which went in to him and everything which came out passed within a foot of my elbow. That is the whole of my qualification. I was not consulted about anything in my life and I read everything."
- "What I chiefly did was ledgers. The Association kept a general ledger and a set of station accounts, and the station accounts came in from the creamery side and from the stations, and were made up, and were copied, and were carried forward, and once a year were laid before the annual meeting in a fair hand, which was mine. I could rule a page then in a way that people used to remark on. It is a lost accomplishment and I do not say the world is poorer."
- **The Ostrey Hollow line**: "Now everybody who comes to see me wants the Ostrey Hollow line, so I will put it down first and get it out of the way." "Every year, in the station account for Ostrey Hollow, there stood three figures and a sentence. So many pounds of butterfat credited to the patrons on the station test. So many pounds of butterfat received at Larrow Green in the station cream. And then the difference of the two, entered as station gain, so many pounds — and after it, always, in the bookkeeper's own words, five and a quarter per cent, as usual, carried to the general account." "As usual. I copied that line, or the like of it, more times than I can count, and I will tell the Society plainly that it never once crossed my mind to wonder at it, and I do not believe it crossed anybody else's either. It had a name. That is the thing about a name. A figure with a name on it has been explained, and you rule under it and go on to the next."
- **The clerk's year**: "People suppose a clerk lives by the calendar. He does not. He lives by what is on the desk. A year in that office was the annual statement in the winter, the creamery inventory when the ice went out, the station accounts all summer, and the meeting again. That is how I hold the whole of it and I have never needed another way."
- **The glass**: "The station up at Ostrey Hollow was opened before my time, and for its first season or two it borrowed its testing glass from the creamery here, and then its own outfit came from the supply house and went into use on the first of May, 1898. I have that from the correspondence, which I handled later when it was all got out for the hearing."
- **The first season**: "That first season, before ever there was a station account, the milk was weighed at the Hollow and carried on the creamery's own books, and the sheets for it were never put with the station papers. People have asked me what the Hollow took in that first year and I have always said the same: a good eleven hundred thousand pounds, which is what I understood at the time from men who were there. It is not in any book I ever ruled."
- **The totals**: "For the long run of years that the patrons afterward complained of, the figure was got out for the arbitration and I got it out. Forty million pounds of milk received at that station, which is sixteen hundred thousand pounds a year, year in and year out, and it did not vary greatly. And butterfat over that whole stretch averaged twenty-five cents the pound, taking the fat years with the lean, which was another thing I was set to find and did find."
- **Selby Vose**: "Mr. Selby Vose became manager in nineteen eleven. He was the son of Mr. Gideon Vose who organized the Association and was its first president — the son, whatever is said at Ashlin, and I have heard the nephew story twice in the last year and it is nonsense. Mr. Selby Vose sat behind that door for a quarter of a century and I sat outside it, and I liked him, and I will say two things about him which do not sit together and are both true. He was courteous to every man who came into that office, farmer or drummer or bank, and he never once let a caller feel he was being hurried. And he read the totals and he never read the lines above them. He was not a man who read. He would take a sheet, turn it, put his thumb on the bottom figure, and sign. I watched him do it a thousand times and thought nothing of it, because that is what managers were."
- **Teague**: "Mr. Wilbur Teague was the bookkeeper and wrote those station lines every year of his service. A dry man, very correct, ate the same dinner out of the same tin. Teague never asked anybody anything in forty years. That is not a criticism. It was his notion of the position: the figures were the figures, and the asking was for other people, and if the other people did not ask it was not his affair."
- **1919**: "In nineteen nineteen there was the circular. I typed it and I remember typing it, which I cannot say of much else. It went up to the Ostrey Hollow patrons and it warned them about water in their milk, in a civil form of words but with no doubt at all in it about what was meant, and Mr. Vose signed it, and it went out over his name and no one else's. I thought at the time it was a hard thing to send to men who were bringing their milk in every morning of their lives. I typed it anyway."
- **"Twenty-two"**: "Then in twenty-two the state man came into the office. Strawn, the dairy inspector on this circuit, a heavy quiet man who wrote everything in a little book with a rubber band round it. He had been up to the Hollow and he had run their milk pipettes against his burette and he had condemned the whole lot of them, and he came in to say so and to tell the manager to order new. Which we did, that same week, to the supply house at Tarnet, and the new glass came up within the month. There was no more said of it in the office than there would have been of a broken window. I entered the order and I filed his word for it and that was the end of the matter as far as the outer room was concerned."
- **The arbitration**: "The arbitration is the piece the Society will want. The patrons had petitioned; a board was formed; the sittings were partly at Ashlin and partly at the station. What passed across my desk to that board was four things, and I can name them without looking, because I made the list and checked them off twice. The station's own test book, in Keddie's hand. The receiving book. Mr. Emmet Brant's private weigh book, which he lent for the purpose and wanted back. And the state inspector's notebook for the year he condemned the glass." "Four books. I carried them in, and I carried them out, and I could not have told you then and cannot tell you now that any man in that room laid the second beside the fourth and looked."
- **The award**: "The award, when it came, was thirty-eight hundred dollars to the Ostrey Hollow patrons, without admission of any cause. That was in twenty-four. It was charged to the station account, which struck me at the time as neat bookkeeping and strikes me now as something else, though I am not the man to say what."
- "I stayed on some years after and then the office was moved and I was not moved with it."
- **The moral**: "If the Society wants a moral from a clerk it may have this one. Nothing in that office was hidden. Every figure I have named was written down in ink by an honest man, in a book that stood on an open shelf, in a room that anybody might walk into, and it was carried forward every year for a quarter of a century, and read out at the annual meeting to fifty farmers with their hats on their knees. It was not a secret. It was worse than a secret. It was in plain sight with a name on it."
- Kyle names Keddie (once, "in Keddie's hand"), Selby Vose, Gideon Vose, Wilbur Teague, Strawn, Emmet Brant; not Effie Loomis, Frick, Sill, Grigg, Ivy, Roy, Hazel, Rosalie, the Chronicle, or Vaught. He gives no gap figure, no 1911, no Bulletin numbers, no pipette figures, no petition figure, no mention of the crate or the loft, no 1958.

## SOURCE 24 — Orra Keddie, "A Farm Woman's Book, 1897–1930," diary extracts published 1964
*"Extracts from a diary kept at Ostrey Hollow, printed from the three notebooks now in the family's hands. The preface and the bracketed notes are the editor's."* (Found as "Chapter 20" of the long-24 miscellany, headed twice: "## Chapter 20 — Retelling 24 — Orra Keddie, 'A Farm Woman's Book, 1897–1930,' diary extracts published 1964" and "# Retelling 24 — Orra Keddie, 'A Farm Woman's Book, 1897–1930,' diary extracts published 1964." The editor is unnamed.)
- **Preface.** "Orra Keddie was born in 1866 and married Ansel Keddie in 1889. He was tester and separator man at the Ostrey Hollow skimming station of the Larrow Valley Cooperative Creamery Association, and kept its books in his own hand for twenty-nine seasons. She began the diary in the spring of 1897 and closed it in 1930, and it was kept in three ruled notebooks bought at Ashlin. The pages covering her girlhood and her family before 1889 were not preserved; nothing in her hand survives from before the marriage. She writes A. for her husband throughout. A single later leaf, written in 1934 and laid loose inside the back cover of the third book, is printed at the end. The entries have been chosen for what they show of the household and the season; nothing has been added and nothing altered but the spelling of two place names."
- ***April 4, 1897.*** "Ground still frozen a spade down. Set the eggs. A. gone before light as always and back after dark, and he has walked it both ways this week because the mare is lame."
- ***May 1, 1897.*** "A. says the station's own glass came up and they are done borrowing of Larrow Green. He is pleased about it. A man is pleased about small things when he is let."
- ***July 19, 1897.*** "Hot. Cut the home piece. Wild strawberries thick along the station road, and I picked a pail on the way down with A.'s dinner."
- ***February 3, 1898.*** "Twenty below at the door this morning. A. broke the path to the station himself at four o'clock. He says the milk comes in with the frost still on the cans."
- ***September 12, 1899.*** "Duncan five years old and reads anything set before him. His father says nothing but I saw him listening at the door."
- ***March 1902.*** "Talk at the store that our milk up here runs poorer than theirs down the valley. A. came home and would not eat. I said it is not your cows and not your hands, and he said no, but it is my figures they will look at. Then he said no more about it."
- ***June 6, 1904.*** "Currants. Rain every afternoon this week at four o'clock, you could set the clock by it."
- ***January 1908.*** "Four men up the road today in Grigg's sleigh, come to look into the herds. They went to my brother Emmet's first, being the biggest, and he gave them their dinner. They were civil enough to me. They wanted to see the barns and not the station, which A. remarked on afterward and let alone."
- ***March 1908.*** "The committee gone back down. Their word is that it is the wet hay of the bottom that thins the cream. My brother Emmet says the bottom fed his father and it will feed him. A. says nothing at all, which is his way, and is not always kindness to me."
- ***August 30, 1910.*** "Threshing. Fourteen men to dinner and I had a girl up from the lower place to help. Two pies short and I have not lived it down."
- ***May 8, 1911.*** "A. home cross about a broken measure. Rain all day, the low piece under water again."
- ***October 1911.*** "Duncan gone to the school at Ashlin to teach, seventeen and looks fourteen. His father drove him down and came back without a word in him."
- ***April 1914.*** "Set out six apple trees below the house. I shall not see them bear well but somebody will."
- ***November 1917.*** "Snow before the corn was in. Everything late this year and everything short."
- ***June 1919.*** "A circular came to the station for the patrons, over the manager's name, about water in the milk. A. would not put them in the mail nor leave them on the platform. He took them round himself, to eleven families, and stood at the door while each man read it, and came home at eleven at night with his hat still on."
- ***June 1919, two days after.*** "My brother Emmet came up. He did not come in. He stood in the yard and said to A. that he had hauled whole milk to that station since the day it opened and A. knew it better than any man living, and then he asked him why he had carried the paper himself if he did not believe it. A. said he carried it because he would not have another man carry it. He went home. He has not been in this house since, and he is my brother."
- ***Christmas, 1919.*** "Duncan up from Ashlin for two days. A good day and I will write it down as one."
- ***February 1921.*** "Ice storm. The station road impassable two days and the milk stood."
- ***July 1923.*** "Hay in good and not a drop on it. A. says they have new glass at the station and new pails, and the roof is still bad, which is the Association all over. He has been in this hollow the whole of his life and has never yet seen them mend a roof before it fell in."
- ***November 1924.*** "Killing weather. A. up before four every day of his life for as long as I have known him, and he has never once said he was tired in my hearing, and he has never once been late."
- ***January 1925.*** "The patrons have put in a paper against the station. My brother Emmet's name is on it. I do not blame him and I will not say so to A."
- ***March 17, 1925.*** "A. went down to Ashlin this morning in his good coat, which he has had since Duncan was married. There is a board of men to hear the whole thing out. He was up at three and did not sleep before that either. I ironed the coat twice for want of anything I could do."
- ***April 1925.*** "Two of the sittings held up here at the station itself, in the weigh room, with the men standing outside in the road. I did not go down. I could hear the wagons from the yard all that afternoon and that was enough of it for me."
- ***April 30, 1925.*** "They have put him out. Twenty-nine seasons and a letter. He came up the road at his ordinary hour with his apron rolled under his arm and hung it behind the shed door and asked what there was for supper. There was ham. He ate it."
- ***May 1925.*** "[The award of the board is not mentioned in the diary. — Ed.]"
- ***June 1925.*** "He has begun on the cow shed, which has wanted doing since his father's time. He works at it from light to dark and whistles, which he never did when he was paid."
- ***1926.*** "[Several leaves torn out. The editor has not attempted to supply them. — Ed.]"
- ***October 1927.*** "A. mending stairs at the far end of the hollow, and the Loomis gate, and half the doors in this hollow, and taking what they offer. A carpenter at sixty-six. He is better at it than he ever was at the other and it pays a quarter of what the other did."
- ***Spring 1929.*** "Word this morning that my brother Emmet is dead. I went down. Roy met me at the gate and was kind. I sat an hour in that kitchen where I was a girl and came home. Ten years and neither of them would go up the road first, and now it is settled the way those things get settled."
- ***July 1929.*** "Hollyhocks the best I have had. A. sat out with me an evening and named the ones my mother had."
- ***August 1930.*** "I am sixty-four and my hand is not what it was. I have kept this book thirty-three years and there is less in it every year, which is not because less happens."
- ***[The last leaf, laid loose in the back of the third book.] February 1934.*** "A. died this morning, at home, in the forenoon, without much pain at the end of it. Duncan came up in the night. There were more at the burying than I looked for, and men shook my hand who had not spoken to him in nine years, and every one of them said what a hand he was with a door. Not one of them said the other thing. Nine years they have had to say it and they had not said it and they did not say it then. He never asked them to. He said to me once and only once, and not lately, that a man cannot be cleared of a thing nobody will name."
- ***[Editor's close.]*** "[Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958, twenty-four years after his death. His widow did not live to see it; she died in 1948. — Ed.]"
- **What the diary gives and withholds.** Orra names Ansel only as "A."; names Duncan, Emmet, Roy, Grigg (his sleigh), the Loomis gate, "the manager" (unnamed), "the Association," Ashlin, Larrow Green, "the store," "the station," "the hollow," "the bottom." She never names Selby, Gideon or any Vose, Strawn or "the state man," Frick or "the lawyer," Teague, Effie, Sill, Ivy, Hazel, Rosalie or Alder Corners, the ridge, Tarnet or Ammon, the crate, the loft, the burette, the condemnation, the award's sum, the gap figure, the number of farms, the 1911 July return, the "something in the glass" remark, or the question mark. Her numbers: eleven families (1919); fourteen men to dinner (1910); six apple trees (1914); twenty below (1898); four o'clock, three o'clock, eleven at night; twenty-nine seasons; sixty-six (Ansel, 1927); sixty-four (herself, 1930); thirty-three years of diary; ten years (Ansel and Emmet, 1919–29); nine years (1925–34); "a quarter" (carpentry pay vs the station's); two pies short; four men in the sleigh; "two days after"; "two of the sittings." Her dates that can be checked all check (1 May 1897; 8 May 1911; 17 March 1925; 30 April 1925; January 1925; June 1919; July 1923; 1929; February 1934) — except that the diary makes her born 1866 and Duncan makes her born 1868.

---

## CONFLICTS AND ODDITIES

1. **Award**: Vira $3,500; Effie, Teague, Ivy, Nym, Selby, Frick $3,800; Rundle no sum; Hazel no sum ("found nothing… let Ansel go, which was the finding"); Duncan no sum ("the finding found nothing and the dismissal said everything"); **Chronicle $3,800 ("thirty-eight hundred dollars, 'without admission,' which is lawyer's language for we are paying you and we are not saying why"); Cleve $3,500 ("That is what my father said and my father was not a man who got a figure wrong when it was money… no admission of anything by anybody")**; **Falke (1959) "three thousand eight hundred dollars, without admission"; Kyle (1953) "thirty-eight hundred dollars to the Ostrey Hollow patrons, without admission of any cause… charged to the station account"** — $3,500 has two sources (Vira, Cleve — both hearsay from a father who hauled), $3,800 nine. **Orra's diary, the one family document of the month, is silent: "May 1925. [The award of the board is not mentioned in the diary. — Ed.]"**
2. **Selby's relation to Gideon**: Vira nephew; Effie son; Selby "My father"; Rundle nephew; **Duncan son ("not, as I have heard it given, his nephew")**; **Kyle son ("the son, whatever is said at Ashlin, and I have heard the nephew story twice in the last year and it is nonsense")** — son 4, nephew 2. Frick "the manager." Hazel "the manager." Chronicle, Junia, Cleve, Falke: "the manager," unnamed. **Orra: "over the manager's name," unnamed.** **Kyle (1953) heard "the nephew story twice in the last year" — six years before Rundle's 1939 pamphlet (which says nephew) was reprinted in 1959, and twenty-six years before Vira said it on tape; the nephew story was circulating at Ashlin in the early 1950s.**
3. **Total milk**: 40,000,000 lb (Effie, Ivy, Nym, Vaught; **Kyle: "Forty million pounds of milk received at that station, which is sixteen hundred thousand pounds a year, year in and year out, and it did not vary greatly" — and Kyle says he himself "got it out" for the 1925 arbitration**) vs 44,000,000 (Teague, Rundle). Frick none. Chronicle, Junia, Cleve, Falke, Orra none. **Kyle's 40,000,000 was the office's own figure compiled for the board; Teague, the bookkeeper of the same office, wrote 44,000,000 in 1948.**
4. **Average test**: 3.61 (Keddie, Ivy, Nym, Vaught; **Falke "three sixty-one"**) vs 3.16 (Teague, Frick). **Falke: Larrow Green 3.78 = Sill's 3.78.**
5. **1911 duration**: nine weeks (Keddie 8 May–10 July; Ivy; Roy; Vaught; **Falke: "On the 8th of May the station's own measuring glass was broken, and a measure borrowed from elsewhere was used in its place for nine weeks, until the 10th of July"**) vs **six weeks (Teague; Kepp: "Six weeks of June and July")**. Keddie's dates are May–July; Kepp says June and July. Kepp: "their own apparatus having failed them and a borrowed one being used" — agrees that a borrowed measure was used and the figures "stood higher." Rundle, Frick, Sill, Grigg, Hazel omit 1911; Duncan gives no duration but "that summer of 1911"; Chronicle, Junia, Cleve, **Kyle** omit 1911 entirely (Cleve was riding the cream wagon that summer, aged eleven, and says nothing of it; Ivy says the borrowed measure "came up from Larrow Green with the cream wagon" — Cleve's father's wagon). **Falke: "the bulletin treats [1911] as decisive"; "The herds, the pasture, the season, the men and the machinery were the same throughout"; the nine weeks "dispose of" the theft theory.** **Orra's diary confirms the start day — "May 8, 1911. A. home cross about a broken measure. Rain all day, the low piece under water again" — and has no entry for the return of the station's own measure (10 July) nor any word of the higher tests.**
6. **1896 borrowed glass**: Keddie's letter "Ashlin creamery" vs first leaf "Larrow Green"; Nym "Larrow Green"; Sill: his own outfit; **Kyle: "for its first season or two it borrowed its testing glass from the creamery here" (Larrow Green)**; **Orra (1 May 1897): "they are done borrowing of Larrow Green"** (Larrow Green 5 incl. Sill's own glass and Orra, vs Ashlin 1). **Kyle's "first season or two" vs Sill's "returned the spring after" and the 1 May 1897/1898 date — Orra's same-day entry settles the return for 1 May 1897.**
7. **Station-gain base**: Teague 5¼% "of the fat that was actually in the cream" (wrong); Ivy/Nym 5¼% of credited = 5% of delivered; Vaught repeats Teague; Sill confirms the heavy cream from the cold-room end. **Cleve's father's belief — "less of it coming off that platform than went onto it" — runs the opposite way from the station-gain ledgers (the cream reached the Green heavy, never light — Sill, Teague, Rundle's retraction); the hauler saw cream go on at the Hollow and come off at the Green and still thought it short.** **Kyle (1953) confirms the line's form from the copyist's end: "three figures and a sentence… credited to the patrons on the station test… received at Larrow Green in the station cream… the difference of the two, entered as station gain, so many pounds — and after it, always, in the bookkeeper's own words, five and a quarter per cent, as usual, carried to the general account"; "It had a name… A figure with a name on it has been explained."**
8. **Teague's 1898 entry vs 1905 start.** **Kyle: Teague "wrote those station lines every year of his service" (1905–1945) — consistent with Teague's "I began it when I came in 1905," which leaves the "entry for 1898" unexplained.**
9. **Dismissal 30 April 1925 before the award of 4 May** — Frick confirms; Hazel: "they had let Ansel go, which was the finding whatever they wrote in the book"; **Duncan: "four days before the board handed down what it called its finding, so that the finding found nothing and the dismissal said everything."** Chronicle: "there were charges against the tester, and at the end of it Ansel Keddie was dismissed"; "Ansel Keddie got nothing, and was not asked back." Cleve: "They dismissed Keddie." **Falke: "The tester was dismissed on the 30th of April, 1925, four days before the award."** Kyle never mentions the dismissal. **Orra, 30 April 1925: "They have put him out. Twenty-nine seasons and a letter" — the dismissal came by letter, on the day; he "came up the road at his ordinary hour" (so he worked the day out).**
10. **Vira's chronology** (rise after the dismissal) vs all others (rise 1923); Sill "along in the twenties"; **Falke: "From 1924 the station's average test stood at three eighty-five and remained there."**
11. **Keddie's start**: Vira "ninety-five… tested that first summer too"; **Hazel "went up… in ninety-five"**; Frick "since it opened in 1896"; Keddie "from 1896"; **Duncan "from the year it opened"** (1896); Cleve "the tester there from before I was born" (1900); Chronicle "from very nearly the day the station opened"; **Falke: "from the opening" (4 May 1896) — and Falke's "built… in the winter of 1895 and 1896" explains a Keddie at the station in '95.** **Orra: "He has been in this hollow the whole of his life" — he did not come to the hollow in '95; he was born there (the cow shed "since his father's time").**
12. **Nine families / seven farms (Effie) / SIX farms (Roy; Grigg's report; Kepp "Six farms cut hay and pasture stock"; Hazel "six farms and no more"; Chronicle "a valley of six farms on a wet bottom") / seven-plus-three (Strawn) / ELEVEN families (Orra, June 1919: "He took them round himself, to eleven families")**. Hazel reconciles nine families with six farms: "a farm can hold more than one family and generally did." Rundle: "a few of our Hessel Bottom men" hauled over (four per Roy/Rosalie/Ivy/Hazel). Falke: "the farms of that end, Hessel Bottom principally" — no count. Kyle: no count. **Nine families (Vira, Hazel, Sill, Keddie "nine kitchens") vs Orra's eleven — the woman whose husband carried the paper to every door counts eleven; the patrons' daughters count nine; Strawn's "wet bottom + three hill places" would make the station's patrons more than the bottom's families — eleven could be nine bottom families plus two hill places, or the whole patron list (Conflict 187).**
13. **"A thousand cans a week" (Vira) vs "thirty-one times a week" (Keddie; Frick).**
14. **Span of the anomaly**: 26 years (Ivy; **Chronicle "the twenty-six years before"; Junia "Six and twenty years afterward," "a customer of twenty-six years' standing"**); 25 years (Effie, Nym, Vaught, Frick "a quarter of a century"; **Kepp: complained "for something like a quarter of a century" by 1926**; Chronicle "at every annual meeting for a quarter of a century"; Junia "a customer of a quarter century"; **Kyle: "carried forward every year for a quarter of a century"; Selby "sat behind that door for a quarter of a century"**); 29 seasons (Keddie; **Falke "twenty-nine seasons"; Orra "Twenty-nine seasons and a letter"; Orra's editor "twenty-nine seasons"**); "a generation" (Selby; **Hazel: "paid short for a generation"**; Duncan: "That fact sat in the middle of our family for a generation"); "fifty years" (Vaught; Roy; **Hazel: "nobody set it against anything for fifty years"; "it took fifty years and a girl from the college"**; Cleve "Nobody wanted it for fifty years"; Chronicle "told in this county for the next fifty years"); "forty-seven years" (Vaught); "thirty years" (Rundle; Frick); Hazel: Emmet and Ansel "argued about drainage for forty years"; Duncan: "thirty-five years" (crate 1923–1958; Cleve "a thing in a loft for thirty-five years"); "another fourteen years" (1911–1925); Chronicle "dead twenty-four years… out of his job for thirty-three" (= Ivy; **Orra's editor: "cleared in 1958, twenty-four years after his death"**); **Falke: "the long period of complaint"; "renewed… in every decade thereafter" (1902 on)**; **Orra: "Ten years" (Ansel and Emmet, 1919–29); "nine years" (1925–34); "thirty-three years" (the diary, 1897–1930).**
15. **Office location** Ashlin (Teague, Nym, Frick; Duncan "the Cooperative office at Ashlin"; **Cleve "down at Ashlin, in the Cooperative's office"**); Vira has Selby at Larrow Green; **Kyle (1953) sat for twenty-five years "at the outer desk in the Cooperative's office at Larrow Green… outside the manager's door," speaks of "the creamery here," and says the office "was moved" (1938) — so the office was at Larrow Green until 1938 by Kyle, at Ashlin by Duncan (1925–59), Teague, Nym, Frick, and Cleve (the 1925 sitting "at Ashlin, in the Cooperative's office").** Hazel: the hearing at "this town" = Ashlin. Chronicle: "a hearing at Ashlin." Falke: "at Ashlin." **Orra: "A. went down to Ashlin this morning… There is a board of men" (17 March 1925) — the first sitting at Ashlin, office unspecified.** **Larrow Green 2 (Vira, Kyle — Kyle an insider) vs Ashlin 5. Possibly two offices (a creamery office at the Green, a Cooperative office at Ashlin), or the 1938 move was FROM Larrow Green TO Ashlin — which would leave Duncan's "Cooperative office at Ashlin, where it lay from 1925" and Cleve's Ashlin sitting of 1925 unexplained.**
16. **Who condemned / when**: Strawn 12 June 1923 (own entry, Nym, Vaught, Bulletin; **Falke: "On the 12th of June of that year [1923]… in the course of an ordinary circuit visit"**); Selby and Rundle 12 July 1923; **Hazel "in twenty-one"** (1921 is the year Strawn's burettes were issued); **Kyle "in twenty-two"** (1922 is the year Strawn "did not run the glass"). Hazel: "some measuring instrument he carried in his coat" = the burette. Duncan: the glassware "went up into the loft… in 1923." **Junia: "in the summer of 1923… an inspector for the state went into that station with a burette"; Chronicle: "condemned that year [1923] by the state's dairy inspector on his rounds — he ran their measures against a burette"; Cleve: "when the state man made them throw it out" (no date).** **Kyle: Strawn "had been up to the Hollow and he had run their milk pipettes against his burette and he had condemned the whole lot of them, and he came in [to the office] to say so and to tell the manager to order new" — by Kyle the manager was told in the office at Larrow Green; by Strawn's own entry (headed "Ostrey Hollow") and Selby (1913: "came out with me to the platform") the telling was at the station.** **Orra never records the condemnation at all — only its consequence, in July 1923: "A. says they have new glass at the station and new pails."**
17. **1919 letter**: written and signed by Selby (Vira, Strawn, Selby; Frick); "the manager" (Roy, Rundle, Sill, **Hazel "the water letter… He sent that round to nine families… nine letters"**; Chronicle "The manager of the Association had circulated a warning… about added water as early as 1919"; **Falke: "the Association's manager, who circulated a warning to that effect in 1919"**; **Orra: "A circular came to the station for the patrons, over the manager's name, about water in the milk"**); **Kyle: "I typed it and I remember typing it… in a civil form of words but with no doubt at all in it about what was meant, and Mr. Vose signed it, and it went out over his name and no one else's"** — Vira called it a "printed letter"; Kyle typed it (nine copies? or typed and then printed?). **HOW IT WAS DELIVERED — Orra (June 1919): the circulars "came to the station for the patrons"; "A. would not put them in the mail nor leave them on the platform. He took them round himself, to eleven families, and stood at the door while each man read it, and came home at eleven at night with his hat still on." Hazel: "He sent that round"; Roy: "the manager sent a paper round"; Vira: "printed letter over the manager's signature" — none of the patrons' children say who brought it; Orra says Ansel did, to every door, and stood there while it was read (Conflict 188).** What the families did with it: Vira's father — desk drawer; one woman — trunk lid; **Hazel's mother — "put it in the stove"**; Keddie — "nine kitchens have the letter in a drawer." Hazel: "answered by none of them." Duncan, Junia, Cleve never mention the letter. **Kyle: "I thought at the time it was a hard thing to send to men who were bringing their milk in every morning of their lives. I typed it anyway."** **Orra's "eleven families" vs everyone else's nine (Conflict 187). Vira: "It rained that day"; Orra gives no weather for June 1919 (she does for 8 May 1911: "Rain all day").**
18. **Three explanations (1925)** — as before (Frick attributes: water = Selby; theft = Teague; hay = 1908 report). **Kepp (1926) restates the hay case with soil chemistry and Bulletin 214, and dismisses the station theory: "it is not good sense to go looking for a second cause in a building."** Chronicle: "Everybody had a theory. The theories were about the men… The Ostrey Hollow farmers thought they were being cheated… The officers of the Association thought the Ostrey Hollow farmers were up to something… Nobody… appears to have thought the trouble might be furniture" (Teague had called the station-gain line "furniture"). **Cleve's father held the theft theory to the end.** **Falke (1959) takes them in order: water — "It cannot be sustained" (the lactometer "from 1908 forward"); theft — "It cannot be sustained either, and the nine weeks of 1911 dispose of it"; hay — "This office is not prepared to dismiss it… a real if partial factor" — and then, in the note, "I withdraw the concession made on page four." Falke attributes water to "the Association's manager" and theft to "an officer of the Association" (Frick's Selby and Teague), hay to the 1908 committee and the 1926 veterinarian (Grigg and Kepp).** **Orra (March 1908) on the hay theory as first delivered: "Their word is that it is the wet hay of the bottom that thins the cream. My brother Emmet says the bottom fed his father and it will feed him. A. says nothing at all" — and (March 1902) Ansel's own first fear: "it is my figures they will look at."**
19. **Award paradox** — as before; Hazel adds Roy's reaction: never attended another Association meeting; Duncan: "the finding found nothing and the dismissal said everything"; Cleve: "no admission of anything by anybody, which everybody up the Hollow took the way you would expect a farmer to take it." **Kyle: "charged to the station account, which struck me at the time as neat bookkeeping and strikes me now as something else, though I am not the man to say what" — the award charged to the same account that carried the station-gain line.** **Falke: "found no cause established, and awarded… without admission."** **Orra: the diary does not mention the award; it does record the dismissal ("Twenty-nine seasons and a letter") and, six weeks later, Ansel whistling at the cow shed "which he never did when he was paid."**
20. **Ivy's relation to Hazel**: Vira "first cousin once removed"; **Cleve "first cousin once removed, that is the way of it"**; Roy, Rundle, **Hazel, Duncan** "second cousins" (Hazel: people call her "my niece or my cousin once removed… and she is not"; Duncan: "Not cousins once removed… and not aunt and niece, which people say because it is shorter") — 2 vs 4; the two "once removed" sources are the two outsiders taped in the valley (1977, 1979). Falke, Kyle and Orra say nothing of Hazel.
21. **Teague's "Mr. Vose" 1916** = Selby (inference; **Kyle has Selby manager from 1911, which makes 1916 Selby's on either dating**).
22. **Consistency checks that hold**: 1,600,000 × 3.61% = 57,760; × 3.80% = 60,800; invoice sums to $77.93; 3.42/0.95 = 3.60; 4.56/0.95 = 4.80; Rosalie's 1919 four figures average 3.80; Sill's 3.78 = 3.61 + Roy's 0.17; Frick's dates match Effie/Nym/Keddie; Grigg's "three years after" = 1911; **Hazel's 1910 leaf (200,000 lb even, scales agreeing, 3.42) matches Roy and Nym word for word in substance; Hazel's "nineteen hundredths" = Ivy's 0.19; Hazel's six farms / four men / nine families match Roy, Grigg, Kepp, Rosalie; Kepp's 27¢ = Sill's 27¢; Kepp's "two tenths" for Bulletin 214 = Grigg's; Kepp's "thin over the loin and rough in the coat… slow to come to condition in the spring" = Grigg's 1908 "thin in the flank… slow to come to their flesh… coats rough."** **Duncan's arithmetic that holds: 1894 + 17 = 1911; 1894 + 74 = 1968; 1889 + 36 = 1925; 1934 + 14 = 1948 (Orra "outlived him by fourteen years"); 1911 + 14 = 1925 ("another fourteen years"); 1925 + 9 = 1934 ("nine years after it"); 1947 + 11 = 1958; 1923 + 35 = 1958 ("thirty-five years"); 1958 + 10 = 1968 ("ten years putting the case right in print"); his book-custody chain (test book impounded March 1925, Ashlin office 1925–59, Rooms 1959; weigh book 1897 → Roy 1929 → lent Ivy 1958 → Hazel 1962 → Rooms 1963; Strawn's books state office 1936 → Farland 1947; Alder Corners book → Rosalie 1941 → Vessey County Historical Society 1970; crate loft 1923 → found Feb 1958 → Rooms 1959) matches Nym's Items 3, 4, 5, 9, 13 line for line.** **Checks that hold from Segment 17: Junia's invoice text = Nym's Item 2 word for word (six dozen at .35 = 25.20; twelve dozen at .22 = 31.68; total 77.93); Junia's 1 May 1897 = Nym's; Junia's 28 June / 2 July = Nym's Item 10 and Strawn's June condemnation; Chronicle's "twenty-four years dead, thirty-three out of his job, twenty-six years of glass, six dozen at thirty-five cents, spring 1897, 1934, 17.6 c.c." all match; Chronicle's Ivy "twenty-seven" = Duncan's 1931; Chronicle's "her father taught school" in Ashlin = Duncan; Cleve's 1900 + 25 = 1925; Cleve's 1909 = age nine; Cleve's "twenty feet" = Duncan's "Twenty feet of ladder"; Cleve's "thirty-five years" = Duncan's; Cleve's nine pipettes = Ivy/Nym/Vaught; Cleve's five dozen = Vaught; Cleve's 67,000 lb = Frick's petition figure; Cleve's three sittings, one at Ashlin and two at the station = Frick and Duncan; Cleve's weigh room = intake room reconciles Frick/Kepp/Hazel with Rundle/Duncan/Chronicle.** **Checks that hold from Segment 18: Falke's 4 May 1896 = the test book's first leaf and Frick's Exhibit 1; Falke's 3.61 / 3.78 / 0.19 = Keddie–Ivy / Sill / Ivy–Hazel; Falke's 8 May–10 July nine weeks = Keddie's entries; Falke's 1902 = Roy/Rundle/Grigg/Sill; Falke's 17 March–4 May 1925 = Effie/Nym/Frick; Falke's 30 April 1925 "four days before" = Frick/Duncan; Falke's 12 June 1923 = Strawn/Nym/Vaught; Falke's February / 9 March 1958 and nine pipettes = Ivy/Nym; Falke's "winter of 1895 and 1896" reconciles Vira/Hazel's '95 with 1896; Falke's Bulletin 214 summary ("not more than five hundredths… in winter… nothing… on summer pasture") = Ivy/Vaught's quotation; Falke's "tester and separator man" = Duncan; Kyle's four books = Frick's Exhibits 1–4 item for item (test book in Keddie's hand; receiving book; Emmet Brant's private weigh book; the inspector's notebook for the year of the condemnation); Kyle's 40,000,000 lb / 1,600,000 a year / 25 cents = Effie/Ivy/Nym; Kyle's station-gain sentence = Teague's entry verbatim ("five and a quarter per cent, as usual… carried to the general account"); Kyle's Selby "a quarter of a century" = 1913–1938; Kyle's Teague "forty years" = 1905–1945; Kyle's "the supply house at Tarnet" = Ammon; Kyle's "new glass came up within the month" = Junia's "we shipped in the month"; Kyle's 1 May 1898 = Vaught §III.** **New checks that hold from Segment 19 (Orra's diary): 1 May 1897 own glass = Nym / Junia / Chronicle / the invoice of 14 April 1897 (and against Vaught/Kyle's 1898 and Hazel's 1899); "done borrowing of Larrow Green" = the test book's first leaf, Nym, Sill, Kyle; Duncan five in September 1899 = born 1894; Duncan seventeen in October 1911 = Duncan's "I was seventeen" that summer; 1902 "talk at the store" = the 1902 first complaints of Roy, Rundle, Grigg, Sill, Falke; January–March 1908 committee = the 1908 report's "appointed in January last… January, February and March" and Nym's "winter 1907–08"; "Grigg's sleigh" = Nyle Grigg, chairman; Emmet's "the biggest" = Effie's "largest patron," Roy's "sixty-odd acres," Hazel's "largest piece," Duncan's "largest place"; 8 May 1911 "a broken measure" = Keddie's "May 8.
