Pipette broke this morning" and Falke's "On the 8th of May"; June 1919 circular "over the manager's name" = Vira / Kyle / Selby; "He has not been in this house since" (1919) = Vira / Keddie / Roy's 1919 break; "new glass… July 1923" = Junia's "we shipped in the month," Kyle's "within the month," Falke's "from the following month"; "the roof is still bad" = Cleve's "shingle roof that wanted work"; January 1925 petition with Emmet's name = Effie / Frick / Rundle / Falke / Kyle "The patrons had petitioned"; 17 March 1925 at Ashlin = Effie / Frick / Falke; "Two of the sittings… at the station itself, in the weigh room" = Frick / Duncan / Cleve / Kyle; "the men standing outside in the road" = Cleve's "about a dozen others" on the platform; 30 April 1925 "put him out" = Frick / Duncan / Falke; "Twenty-nine seasons" = Keddie / Falke; the apron = Cleve's canvas apron; the carpentry = Cleve's barn door; "A carpenter at sixty-six" in 1927 = born 1861 (Duncan); Emmet dead spring 1929 = Roy / Nym / Duncan's 1929; "Ten years" 1919–1929 = Roy's "from 1919 they did not speak"; "I am sixty-four" in 1930 and "born in 1866" (editor) agree with each other and with Duncan's "eighty-two… in 1948"; "thirty-three years" = 1897–1930; February 1934 death = Ivy / Duncan / Chronicle 1934; "nine years" = Duncan's "nine years after it"; the burying "well attended" = Cleve's "well attended and nobody said much" (Orra: "Not one of them said the other thing"); editor's "cleared in 1958, twenty-four years after his death… she died in 1948" = Chronicle / Duncan.**
23. **1938** office move and Selby's end coincide; **Kyle: "the office was moved and I was not moved with it."**
24. **Keddie's death**: Vira "in the thirties"; Ivy 1934; **Duncan 1934**; Chronicle 1934; Cleve "in the thirties… I went to the funeral"; Hazel "did not live to see it" (1958); **Falke (1959): "all parties in interest being dead"**; **Orra: February 1934, "this morning, at home, in the forenoon, without much pain at the end of it" — the only source to give the month, the place and the manner; "Duncan came up in the night."**
25. **Strawn's start**: 1907 (Strawn's own extracts) vs 1908 (Ivy; **Falke: "at every visit from 1908 forward"**) — 1908 now has two sources, both from the 1958–59 college side.
26. **Twenty-eight books for thirty years** — Duncan: "He kept one for every year he rode the Ordell circuit" (1907–1936 would be thirty). **Kyle: "a little book with a rubber band round it."**
27. **Where Strawn's books went** — as before; Frick's exhibit 4; Duncan: state dairy office 1936 → Farland library 1947, "Nobody had asked for them in the interval." **Chronicle: the bulletin's acknowledgments thank "the state dairy office, for the loan of an old inspector's notebooks"** — as if the notebooks were still the state office's to lend in 1958, though Ivy and Duncan put them in the Farland library from 1947. **Kyle (1925): "the state inspector's notebook for the year he condemned the glass" passed across his desk to the board and back — so in 1925 the 1923 notebook was in the Association's hands for the hearing, then returned to Strawn (who kept it till 1936).**
28. **Strawn's June 1911 visit inside the borrowed-measure weeks**; "Nothing here to note."
29. **The 1922 non-check.** **Kyle's "twenty-two" for the condemnation is the year Strawn's own extracts say he "did not run the glass."**
30. **Gap 12 June–July 1923**: glass used unstated. Junia: the replacement "will go forward this month… we shipped in the month" (July). **Kyle: ordered "that same week," "the new glass came up within the month." Falke: "New glass was in use from the following month." Orra (July 1923): "new glass at the station and new pails."**
31. **The manager knew** (1919, 1923, order book) yet the crate "never mentioned in the hearing"; Strawn's notebook was Exhibit 4. **Hazel: "They ordered new. Nobody said one word about all the tests that were already in the book."** **Duncan: "two of the three sessions of that arbitration sat in the room underneath it" (the crate in the loft above the hearing room).** **Cleve: "the crate was up the ladder over their heads the entire time. Twenty feet, if that. I have thought about that since, I will not pretend I thought about it then."** **Chronicle: Strawn "wrote down that he had condemned them. He wrote down nothing about what they might have been doing for the twenty-six years before"; Ivy: "in a crate over his head the entire time."** **Kyle: "There was no more said of it in the office than there would have been of a broken window. I entered the order and I filed his word for it and that was the end of the matter as far as the outer room was concerned"; of the four books, "I could not have told you then and cannot tell you now that any man in that room laid the second beside the fourth and looked" (the receiving book beside the inspector's notebook).** **Falke: "No person connected the one thing with the other at the time, and the inspector himself drew no inference whatever as to the years behind it."** **Orra (July 1923) — the tester's own household took the new glass as a piece of housekeeping in a sentence about the roof: "they have new glass at the station and new pails, and the roof is still bad, which is the Association all over."**
32. **Loft "full since before the war"** (Strawn 1928) vs crate carried up June 1923 vs Nym's Ashlin office; Rundle 1939 loft "never been cleared out"; **Hazel: "it sat up in the loft over the weigh room for most of my life"; found "under a tarpaulin and about an inch of chaff"; Duncan: "the loft over the intake room… Twenty feet of ladder."** **Cleve: "Some sacks, a broken churn, wire… a crate… with a tarpaulin over it"; Chronicle: "under the dust since 1923."** **Falke: "still crated, in the loft over the station's intake room, where it had lain since the day it was condemned."** Orra never mentions the loft or the crate.
33. **New tester "quick"** vs Keddie "slow and clean."
34. **Pre-1913 manager unnamed.** **Kyle makes Selby manager from 1911, which shortens the unnamed predecessor's tenure by two years and puts Selby in the chair for the 1911 nine weeks.**
35. **Cream shipped same day** — as before. Cleve: canvas over the cans in July; nothing in winter "because they would not freeze in the time it took." **Orra (February 1898): "the milk comes in with the frost still on the cans"; (February 1921) "Ice storm. The station road impassable two days and the milk stood."**
36. **Station still running 1936**; **closed 1946 (Cleve: "the milk went down the valley in a truck by that time"; afterwards hay tools; platform gone, roof going, ladder still on the wall)**; Chronicle 1958 "the old station"; closed by 1958; **Falke 1959: "the Association's account long since closed."**
37. **Noise caution** — as before; Segments 14, 15, 16 and part of 17 add nothing but the "Brittle Horizon" gibberish, whose figures (Reuben Rutledge, Orville Oakes, the Widow Marcellus Ferris, Doctor Keturah Prentiss, Tobias Larkspur) are not story people; the long-21 miscellany's figures (Clarinda Ferris, Ambrose Whitfield, the Widow Parthenia Winslow, Nehemiah Dunmore; Absalom Sackett, Absalom Pennington, Cassius Oakes, Zebulon Cutter, Temperance Colby, Eliakim Ormsby) likewise; **the long-24 miscellany's figures (young Wilhelmina Isley, Mrs. Jedediah Whitfield, Mr. Elihu Sackett, Parthenia Yarrow, Theodosia Yarrow, Prudence Winslow, Josiah Hollister, Doctor Eliakim Isley; Cassius Ridgeway, Cordelia Mercer, Theodosia Cutter, Jedediah Oakes; its ledger customers 1862–1867, including the Segment 20 run from Orville Mabry to Thaddeus Kessler) likewise; the BSWP note, the surgical-waiting transcript (Evelyn, Russ, Dorrit, Anne) and the Third Method lecture (Wend, Prenmarch, Halvane, Rennerfeld, Vansittle, Ostmark) are unrelated.** **Retelling 24 sits inside the long-24 noise; its "Grigg," "Loomis," "Emmet," "Roy," "Duncan," "Ashlin," "Larrow Green" are story; the ledger's and gossips' names around it are not. The whole long-24 tail read by Segment 20 (lines 5,008–6,681) carried no story name at all.**
38. **Nym's transposed keepers** — Frick's exhibit list has them right; Hazel: Emmet's book, Ansel's test; Duncan: Emmet's weigh book, Ansel's test book; **Kyle: "The station's own test book, in Keddie's hand… Mr. Emmet Brant's private weigh book."** **Orra's editor: Ansel "kept its books in his own hand for twenty-nine seasons."**
39. **Where the crate was found**: Ivy station loft; Nym Ashlin office; **Hazel the loft ("up in that loft… who thought to climb a ladder")**; **Duncan the loft over the intake room**; **Chronicle "the loft over the old station's intake room"; Cleve "up in that loft… over the intake room… she went up that ladder and brought the crate down herself"; Falke "in the loft over the station's intake room"** — 6 vs 1.
40. **Condemnation date**: 12 June (Strawn, Nym, Vaught, **Falke**) vs 12 July (Selby, Rundle) vs **1921 (Hazel)** vs **1922 (Kyle)**; Duncan "1923" only; Junia "summer of 1923"; Chronicle "1923"; **Orra: new glass "July 1923" (no condemnation entry).**
41. **1924 figure**: 3.80 (Strawn; Keddie) vs Selby 3.90 vs **Falke 3.85 ("From 1924 the station's average test stood at three eighty-five and remained there")** — three figures for the same year from three sources; **Falke's 3.85 is also Sill's guess at the Hollow's "real" figure ("really a 3.85 and no better"), and 3.85 is what 3.61 + Kepp/Selby's 0.21 (or 3.80 + 0.05) would give; the Alder Corners summers ran 3.78–3.82.**
42. **Selby's chronology** (rise after the petition).
43. **Selby's water theory** vs Strawn, Bulletin 471, Rosalie, Roy, Rundle's in-press note; Sill holds it too; Grigg's 1908 "no dishonesty"; **Hazel: "it was the glass. It had always been the glass."** Kepp: the cause is the ground, not water and not the building. **Duncan: Ansel himself said in 1911 "it was something in the glass."** Chronicle: "Nobody watered anything. Nobody stole anything. The scales were true, the acid was right, the cows were ordinary, and a piece of glass out of a crate was wrong." Cleve: "They say now it was the glass. I have no quarrel with the college." **Falke: water "cannot be sustained… never once found added water at that station"; theft "cannot be sustained either"; "no milk was watered and no cream was stolen at Ostrey Hollow."** **Kyle (1953, five years before the bulletin) blames nobody and names no cause — only the line "in plain sight with a name on it."** **Orra (1902): "it is not your cows and not your hands" — her own first verdict; Emmet (1919, per Orra): "he had hauled whole milk to that station since the day it opened and A. knew it better than any man living" — the biggest patron's denial of the water theory to the tester's face.**
44. **Strawn's frequency**: Selby "every year or two" vs most years. Chronicle: "on his rounds." **Falke: "at every visit from 1908 forward"; "an ordinary circuit visit."**
45. **Test-book columns**: Keddie five; Nym four. **Weigh-book columns**: Roy two weights; Nym weights with test paid on; **Hazel: "both figures… side by side"** (his weight and the station's) — agrees with Roy.
46. **Alder Corners figures**: niece (Ivy; **Chronicle "Jerome Cudd's niece"**) vs daughter (Rosalie, Rundle, Nym, **Duncan**); fourteen seasons (Ivy) vs sixteen (Rosalie, Roy) vs one (Nym). "Miss Keddie" writes to "Rosalie Cudd" who signs plain; Chronicle and Duncan (1970) say "Mrs." Falke, Kyle and Orra never mention Alder Corners.
47. **Hazel Brant** = Roy's daughter — resolved; Hazel confirms "I am Roy's"; Duncan "Roy's daughter is Hazel Brant."
48. **Ivy's 4.56 and 3.42 herds** = Vose Jerseys and Brant 1910.
49. **Article VII's date**: Effie 1892; Rundle 1891; Grigg 1893; **Falke: "The printed by-laws carry no date upon their face, only the words 'adopted at the annual meeting,' and the minute book of the Association's first years was lost in an office removal, so that this office is unable to state the year of adoption and does not attempt to"** (= Nym's "undated"; the lost 1889–92 minute book of Effie). **Falke: "the article, numbered seven… is the hinge of the whole matter" = Effie's "the hinge of the whole Ostrey Hollow business"; "Under payment by weight nothing that follows in this report would have cost any man a dollar."**
50. **1908 sampling**: winter / Jan–Mar / all of 1908 (Grigg's letter) — as before; Kepp: "A committee of practical men went over the ground in 1908." **Chronicle conflates the 1908 committee with the 1925 board: "The committee that sat on the matter in 1908 sat again in 1925 and awarded the patrons their money" — Grigg's committee of three (1908) and Frick's board of three (1925) were different bodies.** **Falke keeps them apart: "A committee of the Association sampled the Hessel Bottom herds in 1908… practical men who walked the ground and looked into the mangers"; "a board of arbitration sat at Ashlin… under the chairmanship of an attorney."** **Orra's diary is the only eyewitness-of-the-month source: "January 1908. Four men up the road today in Grigg's sleigh, come to look into the herds… March 1908. The committee gone back down" — January to March, as the report says, not "every month of that year" as Grigg's 1919 letter says; FOUR men, not "we three" (Conflict 189); and "They wanted to see the barns and not the station," against the report's own claim to have "stood by while the test was made" and tried the station scales (Conflict 190).**
51. **Ivy's college** = Farland; Hazel: "the agricultural college at Farland"; "back east"; Duncan: "east to Farland," "Ivy at the college"; Chronicle: "took her first degree at Farland and stayed on for the graduate work"; Cleve: "schooled at Farland"; **Falke: "a graduate student of that department" (dairy husbandry); measured the pipettes "at Farland."**
52. **Test-book custody** — as before; Duncan confirms impounded March 1925, Ashlin office 1925–59, Rooms 1959; **Kyle: the test book "passed across my desk to that board… I carried them in, and I carried them out"; Falke 1959: "the surviving records… in the custody of the Historical Rooms at Ashlin, where they now are."**
53. **Surviving pipettes**: nine (Ivy, Nym, Vaught, **Cleve "nine of them left in it at the end"**, **Falke "the nine surviving pipettes"**) vs Rundle 1959 "Eleven" and **Chronicle "eleven pipettes in it in a condition to be measured"** — 5 vs 2; the Chronicle wrote from the college press release in October 1958, five months before Ivy's own nine-pipette sheet was read at the Rooms; **Falke, writing in 1959 from the bulletin, has nine.**
54. **The size of the gap**: Nym "a little under two tenths"; Ivy 0.19; **Hazel 0.19 ("nineteen hundredths… flat as a floor")**; **Falke 0.19 ("nineteen hundredths of a point… the same nineteen hundredths in every month of the year, in the flush of June and in the depth of February alike")**; Roy 0.17; Vaught §V 0.295; Selby 0.21; **Kepp 0.21 ("twenty-one hundredths… as it has been given to me and as I have satisfied myself") and "a fifth of a point" (0.20) for wet bottoms generally**; Sill: Larrow Green 3.78, the Hollow "really a 3.85." Duncan, Chronicle, Junia, Cleve, Kyle, Orra: no figure. **Falke's 3.78 − 3.61 = 0.17 (Roy's figure), though Falke says 0.19 "as the bulletin now fixes it."**
55. **No 1896 intake figure** (Nym) vs Sill ~900,000 lb vs **Kyle "a good eleven hundred thousand pounds… what I understood at the time from men who were there. It is not in any book I ever ruled"** — two hearsay figures 200,000 lb apart; Kyle explains why Nym has none: "the milk was weighed at the Hollow and carried on the creamery's own books, and the sheets for it were never put with the station papers." (Kyle was not yet in the office in 1896 — "opened before my time.")
56. **Selby "one more" year** vs 1938.
57. **"Heard everybody" vs nobody came to the station** — as before; Hazel: "the board had heard everybody and found nothing"; Hazel on Emmet: "he would stand in the doorway of that weigh room with his hat on" (so Emmet at least went inside the station). **Cleve: the board itself sat in the station twice, with "about a dozen" patrons on the platform outside in the rain.** **Kyle: "partly at Ashlin and partly at the station." Falke: "It heard the patrons and the officers"; sat "at Ashlin" (no mention of the station sittings).** **Orra: "Two of the sittings held up here at the station itself, in the weigh room, with the men standing outside in the road. I did not go down" — the tester's wife did not attend; she heard the wagons.**
58. **Station "in daily use since 1896."**
59. **Emmet Brant's death** — 1929 (Roy; Nym; **Duncan**; **Orra "Spring 1929"**); Hazel "don't ask me, I'll only guess."
60. **Invoice detail** — "8 per cent neck." Junia: "twelve dozen bottles… a four-bottle hand tester that a man turned with his own arm."

61. **Weigh book start**: Nym, Ivy, Frick, **Duncan** — 1 January 1897; Roy — 1 January 1898; **Hazel — 1 January 1900 ("a clean date under a new habit")**. Three dates, always the first of January; 1897 has four sources, Roy and Hazel one each. Kyle: no start date ("Mr. Emmet Brant's private weigh book"). Orra's diary, itself begun April 1897, never mentions her brother's book.
62. **Weigh book description**: Nym (small quarto, sheepskin, greasy corners) vs Roy (plain ledger ruled by hand, cover gone, harness-leather strip, forty stained leaves) vs **Hazel ("a tall narrow account book, canvas over board, and the spine went soft on the left where he carried it under his arm")** — three different physical descriptions; Roy's second volume; Nym accessioned one. Duncan describes nothing physical; Kyle "private weigh book."
63. **Source of the 1911 borrowed measure**: Larrow Green (Keddie/Ivy); "from town" (Teague); the state man's (Roy); Kepp "a borrowed one"; **Falke "borrowed from elsewhere"**; Orra: no word of where it came from ("cross about a broken measure").
64. **Alder Corners founding**: 1913 vs 1914; Duncan "from the founding of his company" (no year).
65. **Vaught's numbers vs Ivy's** — unchanged. **The Chronicle takes Vaught's side throughout: mean 16.27 (Vaught §II/§III) not Ivy's 16.72; but six dozen pipettes (Ivy/invoice) not Vaught's five dozen; eleven measured (Rundle) not nine; $19,600 (Rundle's petition figure) not Vaught's 118,000 lb or Ivy's $19,000.** **Falke gives no mean at all ("materially less than the capacity blown upon their stems, and in the same proportion, one to another"), nine pipettes, 0.19, and no dollar or pound figure — and "from 1924… three eighty-five," which is neither Strawn/Keddie's 3.80 nor Vaught's 3.905 nor Selby's 3.90.**
66. **Brant/Keddie silence, motive** — as before; Hazel says nothing of a silence: Ansel "ate at his table, and the two of them argued about drainage for forty years" (if Ansel and Emmet argued for forty years, c. 1880s–1920s, that sits oddly with the 1919 break reported by Vira, Keddie, Roy). **Duncan likewise: Emmet "was at our table on a Sunday and had been for thirty-six years" — counted from the 1889 marriage to 1925, running straight through 1919; and Orra's kept letters concern "her brother's health." Vira, Keddie ("He and I have not spoken since the circular") and Roy ("from 1919 they did not speak") say the break was 1919; Hazel and Duncan — Emmet's granddaughter and Orra's son — both describe unbroken table-fellowship.** **ORRA HERSELF (1919, 1929) sides with Vira, Keddie and Roy against her own son and Emmet's granddaughter: "He went home. He has not been in this house since, and he is my brother" (June 1919); "Ten years and neither of them would go up the road first, and now it is settled the way those things get settled" (spring 1929). By the diary the break ran from June 1919 to Emmet's death — ten years, not "thirty-six years" of Sunday dinners to 1925 (Duncan) nor "forty years" of drainage arguments (Hazel). MOTIVE by Orra: Emmet's grievance was that Ansel "carried the paper himself" — "why he had carried the paper himself if he did not believe it"; Roy's version (1955) was the reverse, that Ansel "ought to have stood up in front of that circular and did not." Duncan's "thirty-six years" (1889 + 36 = 1925) is arithmetic from the marriage to the dismissal, not a memory of Sundays; Duncan was at Ashlin from October 1911 (Orra) and home only at Christmases.** Falke and Kyle never mention the family tie.
67. **Butterfat price**: ~25¢ (Effie/Ivy/Nym; Roy overall; **Kyle: "butterfat over that whole stretch averaged twenty-five cents the pound, taking the fat years with the lean, which was another thing I was set to find and did find" — compiled for the 1925 board**); 24¢ 1910 (Roy); **27¢ (Sill; Kepp "through these years")**. **Orra's only money: carpentry "pays a quarter of what the other did."**
68. **Roy's "the book has outlived two of the three men who are in it"** (1955).
69. **Rosalie's chronology** — as before.
70. **"seven miles off over a mountain"** vs four miles of switchback.
71. **Who wrote Bulletin 471's last paragraph.** **Chronicle: the bulletin "comes out under two names: Professor Leland Vaught… and Miss Ivy Keddie" — Ivy is co-author; Vaught's sections are signed "— L. VAUGHT"; the acknowledgments "name Professor Vaught" (so the acknowledgments are Ivy's).** **Falke: "prepared jointly by Professor Leland Vaught… and Miss Ivy Keddie"; the 1958 bulletin cited Bulletin 214 "without its figures" — yet Vaught §VI and Ivy both quote 214's "Five hundredths in winter; nothing at all in summer"; either Falke had a version without the figures, or he means the full trial data.**
72. **The patrons' loss figure**: $19,600 (Rundle; **Chronicle "nineteen thousand six hundred dollars ($19,600)" as "the bulletin works out"**); $19,000 / 76,000 lb (Ivy/Nym/§VII); 67,000 lb (Frick; **Cleve "Sixty-seven thousand pounds of butterfat was what they made it, over the whole run of years"**); 118,000 lb (Vaught §V). The Chronicle attributes the petition's dollar figure to the bulletin; Cleve attributes the petition's pound figure to the 1958 "writing." **Falke and Kyle give no loss figure at all; Kyle gives the inputs (40,000,000 lb; 25¢). Orra: "The patrons have put in a paper against the station" — no figure.**
73. **Duration of the valley's silence**: Rundle six years; Vira/Roy/Keddie never; Hazel: Roy "never went to another meeting of that Association as long as he lived." **Orra: Ansel and Emmet ten years (1919–29), ended only by Emmet's death; Ansel and "men who had not spoken to him in nine years" (1925–34), ended only by his own.**
74. **Rosalie "now" in 1939.** 75. **Rundle's retraction** — **Kyle (1953) reaches the same place from the copyist's desk fourteen years after Rundle's in-press note: the station-gain line "was in plain sight with a name on it."** 76. **Rundle's Article VII authorship.** 77. **Rundle's geography.** 78. **Rundle's dating.** 79. **"Fifty years."** — as before.
80. **Board composition and venue** — as before; **Hazel: Roy "drove down to this town [Ashlin] for it."** Duncan: first session's venue unstated; "two of the three sessions… in the room underneath" the loft (the station). **Cleve: "They started it down at Ashlin, in the Cooperative's office, because that is where the lawyer was and lawyers do not travel. But there were three sittings of it altogether and two of them were held up at the station itself, in the weigh room… because the books and the scales and the machinery were all up there and it was easier to carry the men to the machinery than the machinery to the men" — matches Frick exactly (Ashlin 17 March; two at the station's weigh room) and Duncan; Frick's "corridor at Ashlin" would be the Cooperative office's corridor.** **Kyle: "the sittings were partly at Ashlin and partly at the station"; "I carried them in, and I carried them out" — he carried the four books to the board, though his office was (by him) at Larrow Green, seven miles from the station and some way from Ashlin. Falke: "at Ashlin from the 17th of March to the 4th of May" only.** **Orra: "A. went down to Ashlin this morning" (17 March 1925 — the date Frick and Effie give for the first session); "Two of the sittings held up here at the station itself, in the weigh room" (April 1925) — a fifth source for one-at-Ashlin-two-at-the-station, and the only one from the tester's own house; "a board of men"; "the men standing outside in the road" (Cleve: on the platform).**
81. **Keddie's private suspicion** (Frick) — as before; **now flatly contradicted by Duncan (see 118), and by Ivy via the Chronicle ("he never knew").** Falke: the tester "set a question mark against it." **Orra (1902): Ansel's first words on the trouble — "it is not your cows and not your hands… no, but it is my figures they will look at"; (1934) "a man cannot be cleared of a thing nobody will name."**
82. **Frick's exhibit list** — as before; **Kyle (1953) independently gives the same four in the same order ("I made the list and checked them off twice"): the test book in Keddie's hand, the receiving book, Emmet Brant's private weigh book ("which he lent for the purpose and wanted back"), the state inspector's notebook "for the year he condemned the glass." "Four books" — Frick: "Four books and nothing else." Kyle adds: nobody "laid the second beside the fourth and looked" — the receiving book (what the cream actually carried) beside Strawn's notebook (the condemned pipettes).**
83. **Sill's tenure.** 84. **Sill's "heavy" cream from 1893** (station opened 1896). 85. **Sill's 1902 anecdote.** 86. **Sill's chipped piece vs Nym's two chipped tips.** 87. **Sill's 3.85** — a fourth "true" Hollow figure; **now also Falke's 1924 figure (Conflict 41).** 88. **Who blames what**: tester (Teague/Vira/Rundle-valley/**Cleve's father**); water (Selby/Sill); hay (Grigg/1908/Emmet-drawer/**Kepp**/**Falke's first draft, withdrawn**); glass (Ivy/Vaught/Strawn/**Hazel**/**Duncan — and, per Duncan, Ansel himself from 1911**/Chronicle/Junia/Cleve "I have no quarrel with the college"/**Falke's final position**); Roy "not the weighing"; **the glass house (Chronicle: "put off on the Association knowingly"; Junia: "an accident")**; **the office's habit (Kyle: "A figure with a name on it has been explained")**; **Orra (1902): "not your cows and not your hands"; Emmet by Orra (1908): "the bottom fed his father and it will feed him" (rejecting the hay); Emmet by Orra (1919): "hauled whole milk… since the day it opened" (rejecting the water); Orra names no cause herself.**
89. **Bulletin 214's figure**: Grigg "two tenths in the winter feeding"; **Kepp "two tenths in the winter feeding"**; Ivy/Vaught quoting the bulletin "Five hundredths in winter; nothing at all in summer"; **Falke, quoting the summary "entire" from the copy the Farland library sent him: "Bog hay fed as the whole of the roughage depressed the butter fat test by not more than five hundredths of one per cent in winter, and by nothing that could be measured on summer pasture."** Grigg and Kepp (2) vs Ivy, Vaught and Falke (3) — Falke's is the only direct quotation of the bulletin's own summary. Hazel: "Not a little in the winter and none in the summer, the way a feed thing goes… flat as a floor" — and Keddie/Roy: the gap the same in July and February; Kepp answers only that the 1911 weeks were summer weeks. **Falke: "Five hundredths in winter and nothing in summer will not carry nineteen hundredths in July."**
90. **Grigg's reprint and the circular (both 1919)** — as before.
91. **Grigg's committee at the station** — as before; **Orra (January 1908): "They wanted to see the barns and not the station, which A. remarked on afterward and let alone" — vs the 1908 report's "stood by while the test was made… tried the station scales against a known weight" (Conflict 190).** 92. **"no dishonesty"** — as before. 93. **Grigg's "twenty years' time"** — as before. 94. **Frick vs Teague** — as before. 95. **Frick's summary of Keddie's statement** — as before. 96. **Frick's age/career.** 97. **Sill's "nine families."** 98. **Sill's premiums.** 99. **Skim milk handed back** (Cleve: "The skim came back out the same platform for the farmers to take home to their hogs"; Chronicle: "gave the skim back to the farmer"; **Falke: "the skim returned to the patron"**).
100. **Sources by date now**: 1908 report (in Source 15) → 1919 Grigg → 1925 Keddie → 1926 Selby, Kepp → 1934 Sill → 1936 Strawn → 1939 Rundle → 1941 Effie → 1946 Frick → 1948 Teague → 1949 Junia Ammon → **1953 Bertram Kyle** → 1955/58 Roy → 1958 Rosalie, Vaught, the Ashlin Chronicle (3 October) → 1959 Ivy, Rundle's footnote, **Orin Falke (report and note)** → **1964 Orra Keddie's diary extracts (the entries themselves 1897–1934 — the earliest-written words in the whole set, though the last but four to be published)** → 1968/70 Duncan → 1972 Nym → 1977 Cleve Oram → 1979 Vira → 1981 Hazel. **Kyle (1953) is the last source written before the crate was found; Falke (1959) the first official county paper after the bulletin; Orra's diary the only source written day by day as the events happened (1897–1934) and the only one from inside the Keddie house before Duncan's 1968 notes.**

101. **Orra's parentage**: Roy — full sister, "same mother and same father, whatever you may hear from people who like a story with a stepmother in it"; **Hazel — "a half sister to my grandfather Emmet, by his father's second wife."** Father and daughter flatly contradict each other; Roy seems to have anticipated exactly Hazel's version. Vira/Keddie say only "sister"; Hazel: "who was his wife's brother." **Duncan — "Emmet Brant and my mother Orra were brother and sister… the younger of the two" — silent on full/half; but his birth years (Emmet 1854 per Roy, Orra 1868) put fourteen years between them, which sits easily with a second wife.** **Orra herself: "my brother Emmet… he is my brother" (silent on full/half); born 1866 by her diary (twelve years after Emmet); "that kitchen where I was a girl" (she grew up in the Brant house); "the ones my mother had" (her mother's hollyhocks — if Hazel is right, the father's second wife, Emmet's stepmother); Emmet: "the bottom fed his father" (Orra's father too).**
102. **The 1925 hearing's year**: everyone 1925 (Effie 17 March–4 May 1925; Nym; Keddie; Frick; Rundle "that spring"; **Duncan "thirtieth of April, 1925"**; **Cleve "The hearing was in twenty-five and I was twenty-five years old, which is how I keep the date"**; **Falke: "a board of arbitration sat at Ashlin from the 17th of March to the 4th of May" 1925**; **Orra: "March 17, 1925… April 1925… April 30, 1925"**); **Hazel "The hearing was in twenty-six"** (1926 is Selby's address and Kepp's paper); **Kyle: the award "was in twenty-four."** Hazel warns: "Years I have not." **Kyle warns: "People suppose a clerk lives by the calendar. He does not."** Chronicle: "in the twenties."
103. **The crate's arrival**: invoice 14 April 1897, in use 1 May 1897 (Nym; Ivy "bought 1897"; Vaught 1897/1898; **Junia 1 May 1897; Chronicle "spring of 1897"; ORRA, same-day entry, "May 1, 1897. A. says the station's own glass came up"**); **Hazel "came out from Tarnet in ninety-nine"; Kyle "went into use on the first of May, 1898. I have that from the correspondence, which I handled later when it was all got out for the hearing"** (Vaught §III also "the first of May, 1898" — two for 1898, now five for 1897 including a contemporary diary). Hazel's years (1899 crate, 1900 book, 1921 condemnation, 1926 hearing) are each 1–3 years off the documentary dates in different directions. **Kyle's years (1898 glass, 1911 Selby, 1922 condemnation, 1924 award) are each one or two years off the documentary dates — the glass and Selby late/early by a year or two, the condemnation and award each a year early.** **Duncan's years, by contrast, match the documents at every point where they can be checked (1897 book, 1923 crate, March 1925 impounding, 30 April 1925, 1929, 1936, 1941, 1947, 1958, 1959, 1962, 1963, 1970) — except his mother's birth year (1868 vs the diary's 1866). Cleve's years also check (1925 hearing; 1958 winter; 1909 at nine). Falke's years all check (1889, 1891, winter 1895–96, 4 May 1896, 1902, 1911 dates, 1919, 12 June 1923, January–May 1925, 30 April 1925, February / 9 March 1958, October 1958) except his "1908" for the inspector (Strawn 1907) and his 3.85 for 1924. Orra's dates, being contemporary, check everywhere they touch a document (1 May 1897; 8 May 1911; June 1919; July 1923; January, 17 March, April, 30 April 1925; spring 1929; February 1934).**
104. **Kepp's 1911 "six weeks of June and July"** vs Keddie's book (8 May–10 July = nine weeks) vs Teague's six weeks. Kepp's explanation (summer flush, cows off the wet ground) is the only source to offer a feed-based reason for the 1911 rise; Ivy/Vaught/Roy/Keddie say the gap did not vary by season. **Falke: "The herds, the pasture, the season, the men and the machinery were the same throughout" — directly against Kepp's seasonal reading.** **Orra: "May 8, 1911… Rain all day, the low piece under water again" — the borrowed measure began in a wet May, with the low ground flooded, not in "the flush of the season."**
105. **Kepp's 0.21 is the same number as Selby's "twenty-one hundredths"** (Selby 1926: the 1924 rise; Kepp 1926: the standing gap). Both 1926 speakers use 0.21; Ivy/Hazel/**Falke** 0.19; Roy 0.17.
106. **Kepp's "the fat is the first thing to suffer" and "nine cases in ten is under the animal's feet"** vs Kepp's own admission he "had never in his life read a test and would not know how" — the Farm Club member's question about the station is the same question the valley asked; Kepp declines it. **Falke (1959) first defers to Kepp ("a veterinarian of standing at Ashlin") and Grigg, then withdraws.**
107. **Kepp says the 1908 committee "found the herds sound"** — the 1908 report itself says the herds were "thin in the flank… coats… rough… slow to come to their flesh" (sound in the sense of honest milk and scales, not condition). Kepp's own description of wet-meadow herds matches the report's. **Falke: the 1908 men "walked the ground and looked into the mangers."** **Orra: "They wanted to see the barns" — the mangers, not the station.**
108. **Hazel's "spring at the upper end… cold enough to ache your wrist in July… where the cans stood"** — Kepp: "the springs come out at the foot of the slope and run in it the year round"; Roy: "The water stands on the low end into May." Different ends (upper vs foot of the slope vs low end). **Orra: "the low piece under water again" (the Keddie place's low ground, May 1911).**
109. **Hazel on the crate's location: "the loft over the weigh room"**; Rundle: "the intake room at the road end and a loft over it"; **Duncan: "the loft over the intake room… two of the three sessions of that arbitration sat in the room underneath it."** Weigh room = intake room? Frick's "the station's own weigh room" and Kepp's "across a weigh room" suggest the weigh room was the room where the hearing sat; Duncan puts the hearing under the loft, and the loft over the intake room — so, by Duncan, intake room = hearing room = Frick's weigh room, and Hazel's "loft over the weigh room" is the same loft by another name. **RESOLVED by Cleve (1977): "in the weigh room — that is the intake room, the same room, with the scale in it"; "over the intake room there was a loft… a ladder nailed to the wall by the door"; the Chronicle likewise "the loft over the old station's intake room"; Falke likewise "the loft over the station's intake room."** **Orra (April 1925): "in the weigh room" — the family's word for it, as Frick's and Hazel's.**
110. **Hazel's "I was small but I remember the stove" (1919)** — Roy born c. 1888; Hazel born c. 1910–15; Vira (born 1912) "was seven." Hazel in 1981 has "been married a long while." Cleve (born 1900) was nineteen in 1919 and says nothing of the letter. **Kyle (born c. 1885) was about thirty-four in 1919 and typed it.** **Orra (born 1866) was fifty-three and wrote it down within the week; Ansel (born 1861) was fifty-eight and carried it to eleven doors; Duncan (born 1894) was twenty-five and at Ashlin.**
111. **Hazel: "he gave it to her and lived some good while afterward"** — Roy alive in 1958 (postscript) and the book went to Hazel 1962 (Nym). **RESOLVED by Duncan: Roy died in 1962 ("at his death in 1962 it went to his daughter Hazel") — four years after the loan.**
112. **Hazel: "Every man on that bottom had been paid short for a generation because a piece of glass would not deliver what was blown on the side of it"** — matches Vaught §II ("blown 17.6") and Ivy's "the mark… on the stem"; **Chronicle: "blown on the stem 17.6 c.c."; Junia: "The mark goes on the stem while the glass is still warm… a vessel that must deliver a stated quantity of milk — not hold it, *deliver* it"; Falke: "materially less than the capacity blown upon their stems… a short charge of milk into a bottle whose neck is ruled for the full charge yields a reading short in the same proportion."**
113. **Kepp's paper and Selby's address are both 1926** — in the same year the manager blamed water and the veterinarian blamed hay, one year after the board found no cause; Kepp: "The matter has been before committees since and before boards, and it is not settled." **Falke's "a veterinarian of standing at Ashlin wrote to the same purpose in 1926" — Kepp's paper was read to the Farm Club and "printed for the members"; Falke calls it writing.** **Orra's diary for 1926: "[Several leaves torn out. The editor has not attempted to supply them. — Ed.]" — the one year of the diary with nothing, the year of Selby's address and Kepp's paper; who tore them out, and why, is unstated (Conflict 199).**
114. **Kepp's Farm Club is in Ordell County; his "hill herds… against the bottom herds… farm for farm"** — no figures given beyond "a fifth of a point"; no herd or farm named.
115. **Hazel's "which is nobody"** (nobody looks at the book at the Rooms) vs Nym's 1972 catalogue work; **Cleve 1977: "now every second car that comes up the valley has somebody in it with a notebook."** **Kyle 1953: "Now everybody who comes to see me wants the Ostrey Hollow line" — FIVE YEARS BEFORE the crate was found; Falke 1959: "raised with this office by three separate patrons within one month."** **Orra's diary was published 1964 — six years after the bulletin, in the same run of interest; "the three notebooks now in the family's hands."**
116. **Orra's age at death (Duncan, internal)**: "born in eighteen sixty-eight… She was eighty-two when she died in 1948" — 1948 − 1868 = 80, not 82; but "outlived him by fourteen years" (1934 + 14 = 1948) holds. Either the birth year or the age is off by two; Duncan, who "taught… arithmetic," gets his own mother's age wrong — or her birth year. **RESOLVED by Orra's diary: born 1866 (editor's preface), "I am sixty-four" in August 1930 (1930 − 64 = 1866); so "eighty-two… in 1948" is right and Duncan's "eighteen sixty-eight" is the slip — Duncan's one checkable error (Conflict 186).**
117. **The Keddie–Vose cousinship**: Vira (1979): "Orra Keddie and old Mrs. Vose first cousins"; **Duncan (1968): "There was no connection whatever between us and the Voses… two women in this town [Ashlin]… will tell you first cousins if you let her sit down. It is talk."** Duncan wrote eleven years before Vira spoke; Vira (born 1912, of Ostrey Hollow, two years at Ashlin when first married) is exactly the kind of talker he describes. Duncan also introduces "the Ashlin Voses," a separate family "no relation to Mr. Gideon either" — a possible root of the confusion. "Old Mrs. Vose" (Vira) may be Mr. Gideon's wife (Duncan). **Cleve (1977) adds a third voice for the talk: "The Keddies and the Voses were cousins of some sort. It is a small valley. My mother had it from her mother and I never heard it contradicted at the time, though I could not draw you the line on paper" — Cleve's grandmother's story, from Larrow Green (the Voses' own hamlet), not Ashlin; "not repeating it out of malice." Talk 2 (Vira, Cleve), denial 1 (Duncan, the only Keddie to speak to it).** Falke and Kyle say nothing of it; **Kyle, who sat outside Selby's door for twenty-five years, mentions no Keddie–Vose tie while going out of his way to correct the nephew story.** **Orra's own diary extracts — thirty-three years of a woman "who could not pass a headstone without giving you the connection" — contain no Vose at all; "over the manager's name" is as near as she comes; her editor chose entries "for what they show of the household and the season," so silence is not proof.**
118. **What Ansel privately believed**: Frick (1946): in the corridor at Ashlin "Keddie told me privately that he had always thought the patrons were at fault and would not say so in the room… that he suspected nobody, which is not [true]"; **Duncan (1968): "He came home that summer of 1911 saying it was something in the glass… he said it twice, at supper and again by the door, which was not his way… He had… nothing to hold in his hand but a suspicion about a piece of glass."** Keddie's own 1925 letter: "That question mark is mine and I have let it stand for fourteen years… I cannot tell you why the figures are what they are." **Ivy by telephone (Chronicle, Oct 1958): "the reason his figures would not come right was in a crate over his head the entire time. I would like it printed that he never knew."** Four versions of the man's mind: suspected the patrons (Frick), suspected the glass (Duncan), could not say (Keddie), never knew (Ivy — his granddaughter, speaking ten years before her father wrote that he had said "something in the glass" at the supper table in 1911). If Duncan is right, Ansel had the answer thirty-five years before Ivy measured it and never wrote it down — and Ivy either did not know it in 1958 or chose "never knew" for print. **Falke (1959): "The tester noted the fact in his book and set a question mark against it" — the official record keeps to the question mark.** **ORRA (the fifth version, and the only contemporary one): March 1902 — "I said it is not your cows and not your hands, and he said no, but it is my figures they will look at"; 8 May 1911 — "A. home cross about a broken measure" and nothing more, no July entry, no "something in the glass" at supper or by the door, though the editor's selection may have dropped it; June 1919 — "he carried it because he would not have another man carry it" (to Emmet); 1934 — "He said to me once and only once, and not lately, that a man cannot be cleared of a thing nobody will name." Orra's Ansel names no cause and no suspect; the diary neither confirms nor contradicts Duncan's supper-table memory (Duncan was seventeen and still at home in the summer of 1911; he left for Ashlin in October).**
119. **Ivy's parentage "printed otherwise"**: Duncan: "My daughter is Ivy… my father's granddaughter, and I write that plainly because I have already twice seen it printed otherwise and once heard it said aloud in this town." **One printing is the Ashlin Chronicle, 3 October 1958, front page — "Miss Keddie is his great-niece" — printed in Ashlin, Duncan's own town, where he taught school; the Chronicle in the same piece says she "grew up in this town, where her father taught school for many years" without naming him.** **The SECOND printing is now very probably in hand too: Orin Falke's 1959 report for the county board, ¶2 — "Miss Ivy Keddie, a graduate student of that department, who is the great-niece of the tester at the Ostrey Hollow station" — a paper for "the county's permanent file," corrected only in a note added after the report "had gone to type": "I am informed, and have verified, that Miss Keddie is the granddaughter and not the great-niece; the error on page two is mine." Falke's error a year after the Chronicle's suggests he took it from the Chronicle (or from the same press release); who "informed" him is unstated — Duncan, a schoolteacher in the county town, is the obvious candidate ("I have already twice seen it printed otherwise").** No other source in hand prints Ivy as anything but Ansel's granddaughter (Nym "Miss Ivy Keddie"; Ivy herself; Rundle's footnote gives only her cousinship to Hazel; Vira "of the family both sides"; Cleve "the granddaughter came back"; **Orra's editor, 1964: "Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958" — no relation stated**).
120. **Ivy's age**: born 1931 (Duncan) → 27 in 1958 when she climbed the ladder, borrowed the book "with her coat on," wrote to Rosalie ("a graduate student"); **Chronicle: "Miss Ivy Keddie is twenty-seven" (October 1958) — confirms 1931**; 28 at the 1959 talk; Vira "young woman"; Hazel "a girl from the college"; **Falke: "a graduate student of that department."** She has children by 1968 (Duncan writes "for his daughter and her children"). **Orra's diary closes in 1930, the year before Ivy's birth; the 1934 leaf does not mention a grandchild.**
121. **"Mrs. Cudd" (Duncan 1970)** vs "Rosalie Cudd," "Miss Keddie's" correspondent who signs "Rosalie Cudd," Jerome's daughter still bearing his surname in 1958 — **the Chronicle (1958) also has "Mrs. Rosalie Cudd."** Duncan's "Mrs." is either a courtesy for a woman of nearly seventy running a factory, or a slip — or she was a Mrs. Cudd (married a Cudd, or a widow) — two sources now say "Mrs.," while the Chronicle also calls her "niece."
122. **"The five books… in three buildings" (Duncan 1970)**: his five are the station test book, the Brant weigh book, Strawn's circuit notebooks, the Alder Corners test book, and the crate (not a book); the three buildings are the Ordell County Historical Rooms (test book, weigh book, crate), the Farland college library (Strawn's notebooks), the Vessey County Historical Society (Alder Corners book). Ivy's 1959 "five records" were different: test book, weigh book, Alder Corners figures, Strawn's notebooks, the annual ledgers. Nym's list (1972) has sixteen items, including the station receiving book (Frick's Exhibit 2) which Duncan never mentions. **Junia (1949) adds a sixth and seventh paper: Ammon's own office copy of the invoice and its letter-book copy of the 2 July 1923 letter, "in facsimile" in her chapter — and the works books that burned in 1931.** **Kyle (1953) adds the office's own papers: the general ledger, the station accounts, the annual statement, the creamery inventory, the correspondence ("got out for the hearing"), the first season's sheets on "the creamery's own books" ("never put with the station papers"), the typed circular, the 1923 order entry, and Strawn's "word" which Kyle "filed." Falke (1959) adds his own report to "the county's dairy papers" and "the county's permanent file."** **Orra's diary (1964) adds three more: "the three notebooks now in the family's hands" — a fourth building, the Keddie family's own, holding the one record Duncan's "none of them ours" did not count (he wrote "the five books are now in three buildings and are none of them ours" in 1970, six years after his mother's notebooks were printed as "in the family's hands"); plus Orra's letters (Duncan) and the 1934 loose leaf.**
123. **"Tester and separator man" (Duncan)** — **Falke (1959) uses the identical phrase: "The tester and separator man from the opening was Ansel Keddie"** — **and Orra's editor (1964) a third time: "He was tester and separator man at the Ostrey Hollow skimming station"** — three sources; Strawn 1934 notes a separator bearing; Frick: "the cream sent down the valley and the skim milk handed back"; **Cleve: "the separator going in the back of" the intake room; Chronicle: "a man weighed the patrons' milk, took a sample of it, tested that sample for butter fat, separated the milk"; Falke: "Milk was weighed in, sampled, tested for butterfat, separated."**
124. **Ansel "weighed milk for" the patrons (Duncan: "men he had weighed milk for since before their fathers died")** — Keddie's letter: "every can weighed, called and written"; Roy: "it is not the weighing"; Emmet's two columns agreed; Cleve: "the intake room, with the scale and the vat." **Orra: "the milk comes in with the frost still on the cans" (1898).**
125. **Ansel and Orra married 1889** — the year the Association was organized at Larrow Green (and, by Junia's "sixty years," the year Ammon & Sons was founded); Ansel then 28, Orra 21 (by Duncan) or **23 (by the diary's 1866)**; Ansel went up to the station six (Vira/Hazel) or seven (Duncan/Frick) years later — **Falke's "winter of 1895 and 1896" building makes it both.** **Orra's editor: "married Ansel Keddie in 1889… nothing in her hand survives from before the marriage."**
126. **Orra "never spoke of the business except once a year, in April"** — April is the month of the dismissal (30 April 1925). **Her diary's April entries: "April 4, 1897" (the first entry); "April 1914" (apple trees); "April 1925" (the station sittings); "April 30, 1925" ("They have put him out… There was ham. He ate it").**
127. **Orra's letters (Duncan)** — "She had kept every letter and she left them to me… only the weather and the price of things and her brother's health" — letters received, apparently from or about the Brant side, touching Emmet's health; Duncan keeps them; not among Nym's sixteen items. **Her DIARY, by contrast, Duncan never mentions — though it was in print by 1964 (Conflict 191); "the weather and the price of things" is a fair description of much of the diary too ("Currants. Rain every afternoon this week at four o'clock").**
128. **Duncan's omissions**: no award, no gap figure, no milk totals, no families/farms count, no water letter, no 1919 silence, no 1911 duration, no condemnation date, no Bulletin 214 or 471, no Alder Corners figures, no mention of Effie, Teague, Sill, Frick, Grigg, Kepp, Rundle, Vira, Nym or Vaught; his purpose is genealogy and custody, "who everyone was and where every book went." **Nor does he mention Ansel's cows or carpentry after 1925 (Cleve; Orra), or the Chronicle piece, or Falke's report, or Kyle's account, or his mother's diary, or that Ansel hand-carried the 1919 circular, or the ten-year silence his mother recorded.**
129. **Duncan on Strawn**: "kin to nobody in the valley whatever… had no interest in any of us" — echoes Strawn's own "I do not keep an estimation, I keep a book" and his naming no person. **Kyle: "a heavy quiet man who wrote everything in a little book with a rubber band round it." Falke: "the inspector himself drew no inference whatever."** Orra never mentions him.
130. **Duncan calls Emmet's the "largest place" and Emmet the man who "hauled the most milk into the station of any man on the creek"** — Effie "largest patron every year"; Roy "sixty-odd acres… thirty-eight cows"; Hazel "the largest piece"; Grigg's 1908 report and Kepp name no patron; **Kyle names Emmet only as the lender of the weigh book; Orra: "my brother Emmet's first, being the biggest."**
131. **Duncan's "the finding found nothing and the dismissal said everything"** and Hazel's "they had let Ansel go, which was the finding whatever they wrote in the book" — the two family voices of 1968 and 1981 say the same thing in nearly the same words; Frick (1946): "There was no finding against him from us and there never was one"; Chronicle (1958): "Ansel Keddie got nothing, and was not asked back"; **Falke (1959): "found no cause established… The tester was dismissed on the 30th of April, 1925, four days before the award."** **Orra (1925), the third family voice and the first: "They have put him out. Twenty-nine seasons and a letter" — and of the award, nothing.**

132. **The outfit's price, transposed twice**: the invoice (Nym Item 2; Ivy; Junia's facsimile) totals **$77.93**; **the Chronicle prints "seventy-seven dollars and thirty-nine cents" ($77.39)**; **Junia, directly under her own reproduction of "Total — 77.93," writes "Seventy-seven dollars and thirty-nine cents."** The firm's historian and the county weekly make the same digit-swap nine years apart — the Chronicle "has seen the invoice," or read Junia. Falke, Kyle and Orra give no price.
133. **Ammon & Sons and Ordell County**: Junia: "The house sold nothing into Ordell County before 1900. That county lay behind the ridge and traded east, out of Farland" — yet the invoice she reproduces on the same page is a sale of 14 April 1897 to the Larrow Valley association "for the Ostrey Hollow station" in Ordell County, and she calls the Association in 1923 "a customer of twenty-six years' standing" (1897–1923). Either the "before 1900" claim is wrong, or Ordell County is not where she thinks it is, or the 1897 sale was routed some other way. **Kyle: "its own outfit came from the supply house" (1898 by him) and the 1923 order went "to the supply house at Tarnet."** **Orra: "the station's own glass came up" on 1 May 1897 — the glass reached Ostrey Hollow in 1897, whatever Junia says of Ordell County before 1900.**
134. **The works books and the 1923 letter**: Junia: the works records "burned in 1931, in the fire that took the old third floor," and "would answer a great many questions if we still had them" — but the letter of 2 July 1923 ("Our books of the nineties are not such that we can tell you anything about the lot you name") was written eight years BEFORE the fire, while the works books ("what glass, what gauge, what man, what day"; "The lots were numbered and the numbers were entered") still existed. Junia calls the letter "the truth" and in the same breath "a thin thing"; the Chronicle calls it proof of guilt.
135. **Why the lot was short**: **Junia (1949): "The mold had been reground and the lot ran short. It was an accident and nobody in the works caught it… a boy checks the first of a lot and the tenth and the fiftieth and finds them consistent, because they are consistent — they are consistently wrong, which is the one fault a check of that kind cannot find"** (= Vaught §II's "invisible from inside," Ivy's "nobody knew it was one"; **Falke: "in the same proportion, one to another"**); **the Chronicle (1958): "A cheap short lot was put off on the Association knowingly, and the firm's silence in 1923 is the proof of it. A house that blows glass to a mark knows what its glass delivers"** — the newspaper accuses; the firm confesses to an accident nine years earlier; nobody else assigns the glass house any intent (Strawn: "Glass is glass"; Selby: "Apparatus wears"; Vaught/Ivy: no blame; **Falke: no mention of the maker at all**). The Chronicle does not cite Junia's book; Junia says "nobody else is going to" set down what happened. **Orra: "He is pleased about it. A man is pleased about small things when he is let" — the tester's pleasure at the arrival of the short lot, 1 May 1897.**
136. **The 1923 replacement order**: Nym Item 10 "order of 28 June"; Selby "We ordered new inside the month… in our own order book with a date and a price against it"; Strawn 12 June "told the manager to order new"; **Junia's letter: "Your order of the 28th ultimo for six dozen milk pipettes and six dozen test bottles is entered and will go forward this month"** — six dozen bottles this time, not twelve; sixteen days between condemnation (12 June) and order (28 June); Chronicle: "They answered by return, entered the order, regretted the trouble." **Kyle: "Which we did, that same week, to the supply house at Tarnet, and the new glass came up within the month… I entered the order" — "that same week" (Kyle) vs sixteen days (Strawn's 12 June → Junia's 28 June); Kyle entered the order himself.** **Orra (July 1923): "new glass at the station and new pails" — the pails are hers alone; nobody else's 1923 order mentions pails.**
137. **The crate's contents**: Ivy/Nym/Vaught/Duncan/Hazel — pipettes (nine survive); **Cleve: "Bottles, and the little glass measures they drew the milk up with for the test. Five dozen of those measures went into that crate to begin with… and there were nine of them left in it at the end, because a thing in a loft for thirty-five years gets broken by everybody who goes up after wire"** — Cleve is the only source to say the crate held test bottles too, and the only one to explain how six (or five) dozen became nine (breakage by men going up after wire and hay forks); Nym counted two chipped tips. **Falke: "the condemned glass, still crated"; "the nine surviving pipettes."**
138. **Ansel after 1925**: Keddie (1925): "I am an old man and I have no other trade"; Vira: died "still under it"; Duncan: "lived nine years after it… with the imputation on him"; **Cleve: "Keddie went home after twenty-five and stayed home. He kept four or five cows on a piece of ground that was not big enough for them, and he did carpentry — good carpentry, he hung a barn door for us and it swung true for thirty years"** — a second trade after all; "four or five cows" would have made him, in a small way, a patron of somebody's station; the Oram barn door (hung 1925–34) "swung true for thirty years" (to c. 1955–64). **Orra fills in the nine years: June 1925 "He has begun on the cow shed, which has wanted doing since his father's time. He works at it from light to dark and whistles, which he never did when he was paid" (the cow shed = Cleve's "four or five cows"); October 1927 "A. mending stairs at the far end of the hollow, and the Loomis gate, and half the doors in this hollow, and taking what they offer. A carpenter at sixty-six. He is better at it than he ever was at the other and it pays a quarter of what the other did"; February 1934 "every one of them said what a hand he was with a door" (= Cleve's barn door). "No other trade" (1925) became a trade within two months.**
139. **Ansel's funeral**: Cleve: "It was well attended and nobody said much, which tells you something" — the only account of it; Vira: "not one word, not at the store, not at a funeral" (of Emmet and Orra, but Emmet died 1929, before Ansel). **Now a second account, the widow's (February 1934): "There were more at the burying than I looked for, and men shook my hand who had not spoken to him in nine years, and every one of them said what a hand he was with a door. Not one of them said the other thing. Nine years they have had to say it and they had not said it and they did not say it then. He never asked them to" — Cleve's "nobody said much" is, by Orra, everybody saying the same one thing (the doors) and nobody saying the other (the cream).**
140. **Ansel's person**: Cleve: "A long dry man, very quiet… a canvas apron with the strap over one shoulder… holding a bottle up to the window with his arm straight out"; Vira: the slope desk by the window, figures "like print"; Strawn: "slow and clean and reads his own figure without being coaxed"; Keddie: "read with dividers"; Duncan: "careful in small things to a degree that other people found trying." Cleve: "I was not frightened of him and most of the boys were." **Kyle's "A dry man, very correct" is TEAGUE, not Keddie — two "dry" men in the story.** **Orra: "A. says nothing at all, which is his way, and is not always kindness to me" (1908); "came back without a word in him" (1911); "his apron rolled under his arm" (1925); "his good coat, which he has had since Duncan was married"; "up before four every day of his life… never once said he was tired… never once been late"; "He is pleased about it. A man is pleased about small things when he is let"; "whistles, which he never did when he was paid"; "He said to me once and only once, and not lately."**
141. **The hauler's theory**: Cleve's father "always said there was less of it coming off that platform than went onto it" — which platform? The cream went onto his wagon at the Hollow platform and came off at the Green; if he meant less cream came off the Hollow platform than the milk that went onto it warranted, that is the tester-theft theory (Teague, Vira, the valley); Sill at the Green weighed it "heavy… Always more. Never less." The hauler "never said it under oath, because he was not asked" — no source lists him among the 1925 witnesses, though Frick's board "heard everybody" (**Falke: "It heard the patrons and the officers"** — a hauler is neither). **Orra (1919): Ansel "would not… leave them on the platform" — the same platform.**
142. **Cleve on the platform, 1925**: "I stood outside on the platform the whole of one of those afternoons, in the rain, with about a dozen others. You could hear the voices but not the words" — Frick: "We saw the scales tried. We saw the sampling done and the test made" (inside); Hazel: Roy "drove down to this town for it" (the Ashlin sitting); Vira: "nobody ever came up that road and looked" — but by Cleve and Frick the board came up the road twice. Rain at the station sitting; Vira: rain the day the 1919 letter came. **Kyle: "partly at Ashlin and partly at the station."** **Orra (April 1925): "with the men standing outside in the road. I did not go down. I could hear the wagons from the yard all that afternoon" — Cleve on the platform, Orra in her yard within earshot, the same afternoon or its twin; Orra gives no rain.**
143. **Station closed 1946 (Cleve)** — Strawn's last round March 1936; Effie (1941) and Rundle (1939) do not say it had closed; Teague's books run to 1945; Chronicle 1958 "the old station"; Vaught "one skimming station in Ordell County"; Cleve: "the milk went down the valley in a truck by that time"; afterwards "hay tools… mowers, a side rake"; by 1977 "the platform was gone and the roof was going. The ladder was still on the wall." Cleve's father hauled "better than twenty years" (c. 1900–1920s) — the cream wagon gave way to the truck at some unstated date. **Falke 1959: "the Association's account long since closed" — the Association itself apparently wound up by 1959; yet Falke's "three separate patrons" raised the question with him in 1959 (patrons of what? — perhaps the county agent's own farmer-clients, perhaps former patrons).** **Orra: "the wagons" in April 1925 — still wagons then; the roof "still bad" in 1923.**
144. **The station building**: Rundle (1939): "low whitewashed building… boarded and battened… intake room at the road end and a loft over it"; **Cleve: "Board building, two floors, a shingle roof… a covered platform about waist high" on the road side; intake room with scale and vat, separator in back; loft by "a ladder nailed to the wall by the door… the trap at the top of it was a piece of plank with a rope on it"**; Duncan: "Twenty feet of ladder"; Cleve: "Twenty feet, if that"; Chronicle: "went up a ladder into the loft over the old station's intake room." "Low" (Rundle) vs "two floors" (Cleve) — a loft counted as a floor. **Falke: built "in the winter of 1895 and 1896."** **Orra (July 1923): "the roof is still bad, which is the Association all over. He has been in this hollow the whole of his life and has never yet seen them mend a roof before it fell in" — Cleve's "shingle roof that wanted work the whole time I knew it," from the other end of the same twenty years.**
145. **The road**: seven miles (Rundle, Sill, Effie, Vaught, Chronicle, **Cleve "it climbs the whole seven"**, **Falke "seven miles up the valley from Larrow Green"**); Rundle "three hours in mud and could not be done at all in March"; **Cleve: "In March you went up through mud to the hub and came down through it again… the second week of March was worse than the first"; the pitch "about two miles below the hamlet, past where the creek comes in from the north."** Strawn 1912: cream shipped same day; Cleve's father drove it daily; Rundle: "fifty Junes… fifty Januaries." **Orra: "the station road" (strawberries, July 1897; "impassable two days" in the February 1921 ice storm, "and the milk stood"); Ansel "walked it both ways this week because the mare is lame" (April 1897) — the Keddie house was a walk, not a wagon ride, from the station.**
146. **Ostrey Hollow a hamlet**: Chronicle "at the hamlet of Ostrey Hollow"; Cleve "two miles below the hamlet"; Vira born "at the top end of Ostrey Hollow"; the station "at the head of the Hollow where the road bends" (Rundle) / "on the flat by the creek where the road bends" (Cleve). **Orra: "this hollow," "up here," "the far end of the hollow," "half the doors in this hollow"; a Loomis household in it; the Keddie house "up the road" from the station.**
147. **Who wrote the Chronicle piece and from what**: "put together in a day and a half from a college press release and two telephone calls" (one to Ivy; the other unstated — Vaught? the college? Rosalie?); its errors (great-niece; the 1908 committee sitting again in 1925; $77.39; eleven pipettes; Rosalie a niece; the state dairy office lending notebooks) are press-release-and-telephone errors; its correct particulars (17.6 c.c.; six dozen at .35; spring 1897; 1919 warning; 1923 condemnation and burette; $3,800 "without admission"; 1934; twenty-four and thirty-three years; six farms) match the documents. It does not name Strawn, Selby, Frick, Grigg, Duncan, Hazel or the Brants; it does not mention the 1911 weeks, the station-gain line, or the Alder Corners figures beyond Rosalie's help. **Falke's report (1959), "put together" from the bulletin and (evidently) the newspaper, repeats the Chronicle's "great-niece" and nothing else of the Chronicle's errors — nine pipettes, not eleven; no price; no 1908-committee conflation; no "niece" for Rosalie (he never mentions her).**
148. **The Chronicle's date**: front page 3 October 1958; "reached her by telephone at Farland on Thursday evening" (2 October); Bulletin 471 "dated October 1958" and "appeared this week" — the bulletin was out within the first three days of October; Ivy's "What the Crate Held" (1959) and Rundle's 1959 footnote came after; Rosalie's letter (1958) and Roy's postscript (1958) before or about then. **Falke: "a bulletin issued in October of last year" (writing 1959); the board's direction came "at its meeting of the autumn quarter" (autumn 1959? — a year after the bulletin, "the matter having lately been the subject of newspaper notice"; or autumn 1958, the report itself going to type in 1959).**
149. **"Old Ordell Wrong Righted" vs the family's silence**: the Chronicle: "Here is the part that will be told in this county for the next fifty years"; Duncan (1968): "my daughter has spent ten years putting the case right in print and nobody has ever put the family right on paper at all"; Hazel (1981): "which is nobody"; Cleve (1977): "Nobody wanted it for fifty years, and now every second car that comes up the valley has somebody in it with a notebook." **Kyle (1953): "Now everybody who comes to see me wants the Ostrey Hollow line" — before the bulletin, before the Chronicle. Falke (1959): "three separate patrons within one month."** **Orra's diary went to print in 1964 — somebody in the family DID put the family on paper, in Orra's own hand, four years before Duncan wrote that "nobody has ever put the family right on paper at all"; Duncan may mean the connections (his Section II), which the diary does not set out (Conflict 191).**
150. **Ivy's "in her own time and largely at her own expense" (Chronicle)** vs Bulletin 471 under two names and Vaught's §I–§VII — the college published it; the Chronicle: "the college's press release is generous about it in the way press releases are." **Falke: "prepared jointly"; "temperate in tone."**
151. **"The theories were about the men, because the theories in this county always are" (Chronicle)** — Kepp (1926): dealt with "by opinion, by grumbling, and by the naming of names"; Rosalie: "a theory about that bottom the way some families have a ghost"; Strawn: "A theory is a thing a man carries out to the platform with him and it gets in the way of the weights"; **Kyle: "It had a name. That is the thing about a name. A figure with a name on it has been explained, and you rule under it and go on to the next."** **Orra's Ansel (1934): "a man cannot be cleared of a thing nobody will name" — the reverse face of Kyle's coin: the figure had a name and so was never questioned; the charge had none and so was never answered.**
152. **Junia Ammon's identity**: "the firm's own historian, sixty" in 1949 (born c. 1889); "Ammon & Sons" — she writes "by the time I was old enough to be a nuisance in it" and "this house"; whether daughter, daughter-in-law or granddaughter of Marcus is unstated; "Mr. Marcus" is how she names the founder. The firm "sixty years" (c. 1889–1949) = the Association's own age (1889); the Chronicle (1958): "still in business."
153. **Junia's account of what the Association asked**: "when a customer of a quarter century wrote to this house and asked in effect what had been done to him" — the 28 June 1923 letter from the Association (Selby, presumably) is not in hand; Nym Item 10 has only Ammon's reply; Selby (1926) treats the matter as "a piece of housekeeping… with a date and a price against it," not as asking what had been done to him. **Kyle, who "entered the order," treats it the same way: "no more said of it in the office than there would have been of a broken window."** **Orra's Ansel likewise (July 1923): new glass, new pails, bad roof, "the Association all over."**
154. **Chronicle's "Every patron in the valley was paid on that test"** — Article VII: "the test of the station where he delivers shall govern"; Larrow Green's own patrons were paid on Larrow Green's test (Sill 3.78) — the Chronicle's "every patron in the valley" overstates; only the Hollow's patrons were paid on the Hollow's test. **Falke has it right: "the test made at the station where he delivers is the test that governs his pay."**
155. **Cleve's "the whole outfit the station was set up with" = five dozen measures (Vaught's number)** vs the invoice's six dozen (Ivy, Nym, Junia, Chronicle); Cleve had his hand on the crate but counted from hearsay ("they tell me now").
156. **Cleve's "Sixty-seven thousand pounds… was what they made it" after 1958** — 67,000 lb is Frick's 1925 figure ("The patrons' own reckoning of what they had lost"), not the 1958 bulletin's 76,000 (Ivy/Nym) or 118,000 (Vaught); as the Chronicle's $19,600 is Rundle's 1925 petition figure, not the bulletin's — both outsiders carry the 1925 claim forward as the 1958 finding.
157. **Cleve's mother and grandmother** — the Keddie–Vose cousin talk came down the female line at Larrow Green ("My mother had it from her mother"); Duncan's talkers are "two women in this town [Ashlin]"; Vira (Ostrey Hollow) also a woman. All three carriers of the talk are women; the one denier is Orra's son quoting Orra ("My mother would have told me"). **Orra's own diary (as printed) says nothing either way.**

158. **Falke's 1924 average, 3.85**: Strawn's 1924 entry and Keddie's letter say 3.80; Selby (1926) says 3.90 ("rose by twenty-one hundredths"); Vaught §V computes 3.905 and calls it "not readily reconciled with the station's own average of 3.80 in the year following"; **Falke (1959): "From 1924 the station's average test stood at three eighty-five and remained there"** — a figure nobody else gives for 1924, though Sill (1934) guessed the Hollow's true milk "really a 3.85 and no better," and 3.61 + 0.21 (Kepp/Selby's gap) = 3.82, 3.61 + 0.24 = 3.85. Three sources, three 1924 figures (3.80, 3.85, 3.90), each five hundredths apart.
159. **Kyle's 1911 for Selby's managership** vs Effie's 1913–1938, Strawn's 1913 "New manager," Selby's own "Thirteen years this spring, since 1913" (1926). Kyle: "Mr. Selby Vose became manager in nineteen eleven" — 1911 is the year Gideon's presidency ended (Effie) and the year of the nine weeks; Kyle's "sat behind that door for a quarter of a century" fits 1913–1938 (twenty-five years), not 1911–1938 (twenty-seven). Kyle's own twenty-five years at the outer desk would, if they ended at the 1938 move, have begun in 1913 — the same year as Selby by every other source; but Kyle says he "came in as a boy on the copying," and was twenty-eight in 1913 (born c. 1885) — so either his desk-years and his boy-years are different stretches, or his "twenty-five years" and "sixty-eight" do not both hold.
160. **Kyle's "twenty-two" for the condemnation**: Strawn's own entry is 12 June 1923; Nym, Vaught, Junia, Chronicle, Falke 1923; Selby/Rundle 12 July 1923; Hazel 1921; Kyle 1922 — four different years from four sources (1921, 1922, 1923, and July 1923). Kyle's "that same week" for the order vs the documents' sixteen days (12 → 28 June); Kyle's "the new glass came up within the month" = Junia's "we shipped in the month" and Falke's "in use from the following month"; Strawn's October "They deliver their mark." **Orra's "July 1923… new glass" agrees with 1923 and with delivery in July.**
161. **Kyle's "twenty-four" for the award**: every document says 4 May 1925 (Effie, Nym, Frick, Falke, Duncan, Keddie); Hazel says 1926; Kyle 1924; Cleve 1925. Kyle's year is one early, Hazel's one late; Kyle's award sum ($3,800) and terms ("without admission of any cause… charged to the station account") match Effie's award text exactly ("without admission, to be charged to the station account").
162. **Kyle's office at Larrow Green** (see Conflict 15): Kyle sat "in the Cooperative's office at Larrow Green"; Teague's office was "over a harness shop" (town unstated in Teague's own words; the notes have carried it as Ashlin); Nym, Frick, Duncan and Cleve put the Cooperative office at Ashlin; Vira put Selby at Larrow Green. Kyle also says the 1938 move took the office somewhere he "was not moved with it" — consistent with a move from Larrow Green to Ashlin in 1938, which would make Duncan's "Cooperative office at Ashlin, where it lay from 1925 until 1959" and Cleve's 1925 Ashlin sitting "in the Cooperative's office" anachronistic by thirteen years — or there were two offices. Frick's "corridor at Ashlin" and Falke's "an attorney of that place" put the 1925 first sitting at Ashlin regardless of where the office was. **Orra: "A. went down to Ashlin" for it (17 March 1925).**
163. **"Historical Society" vs "Historical Rooms"**: Kyle (1953) wrote "for the Ordell County Historical Society," at "the Society's request"; Nym (1972) is curator of "the Ordell County Historical Rooms"; Hazel "the historical rooms in this town"; Duncan "the Ordell County Historical Rooms in this town"; Falke (1959) "the Historical Rooms at Ashlin." The Vessey County body is a "Historical Society" (Nym, Duncan). Either the Ordell Society and the Ordell Rooms are one institution (the Society keeping the Rooms), or two. **Orra's notebooks (1964) are in neither — "in the family's hands."**
164. **The first season's intake**: Sill (1934) "about nine hundred thousand pounds" (he weighed the cream at the Green); Nym (1972) "no 1896 intake figure"; **Kyle (1953) "a good eleven hundred thousand pounds, which is what I understood at the time from men who were there. It is not in any book I ever ruled"** — 900,000 vs 1,100,000; Kyle explains the absence from the station papers (carried on "the creamery's own books… the sheets for it were never put with the station papers") — those creamery sheets would be a seventeenth item nobody has accessioned.
165. **Kyle's "first of May, 1898" for the outfit** (with Vaught §III) vs 1 May 1897 (Nym; Junia; Chronicle "spring of 1897"; Ivy "bought 1897"; the invoice of 14 April 1897; **Orra's diary entry dated "May 1, 1897": "the station's own glass came up"**) vs Hazel's 1899. Kyle claims documentary authority ("from the correspondence, which I handled later when it was all got out for the hearing") — the same office correspondence that would include the 1897 invoice; Kyle's "first season or two" of borrowed glass fits 1898 better than 1897 (Sill: returned "the spring after" 1896 = 1897). **Orra's is a contemporary entry, made the day the glass came; Kyle's and Vaught's are recollection and inference; 1897 stands.**
166. **Falke's "all parties in interest being dead" (1959)**: Roy Brant (patron's son, weigh-book keeper) lived to 1962; Hazel, Duncan, Ivy, Rosalie, Vira, Cleve were alive; Frick (born c. 1872) would have been about 87; Teague (retired 1945) and Effie (born 1875) unknown; Selby Vose's death date is given nowhere. Falke presumably means the 1925 principals — Keddie (d. 1934), Emmet (d. 1929), Gideon (d. 1917) — and the Association ("account long since closed"). **Orra (d. 1948) was dead too.**
167. **Falke's "three separate patrons within one month"** — in 1959 the station had been shut since 1946 and the Association's account was "long since closed"; "patrons" here are most likely farmer-clients of the county agent's office, or former patrons of the Hollow; Falke does not say.
168. **Falke's concession and withdrawal**: ¶9 ("page four") allowed the wet hay "as a real if partial factor" and said "the 1908 committee was not simply wrong"; the note, on reading Bulletin 214's summary, withdrew it: "Five hundredths in winter and nothing in summer will not carry nineteen hundredths in July." The county's official paper thus records both positions in one document — Grigg/Kepp's in the body, Ivy/Vaught's in the note. Falke says the 1958 bulletin cited 214 "without its figures" (but see 71).
169. **Kyle's "everybody who comes to see me wants the Ostrey Hollow line" (1953)** — five years before the crate was found and the bulletin appeared; interest in "the Ostrey Hollow line" (the station-gain entry) in 1953 can only be Rundle's retraction (1939/in-press note) and the Society's own curiosity; Kyle's moral ("in plain sight with a name on it") anticipates the Chronicle's "Nobody… appears to have thought the trouble might be furniture" (Teague's word) and Effie's "The interesting part is generally what none of them thought worth saying" (1941) — three office-side voices (Teague 1948 "furniture," Effie 1941, Kyle 1953) on the same line before 1958.
170. **The nephew story's circulation**: Kyle heard it "twice in the last year" (1952–53) "at Ashlin"; Rundle printed it in 1939 (Larrow Green); Vira said it in 1979 (Ostrey Hollow); Duncan denied it in 1968 (Ashlin); Effie (1941), Selby (1926), Duncan, Kyle say son. Kyle sat outside Selby's door for a quarter century and had it from the man himself, presumably.
171. **Kyle's four books and "the second beside the fourth"**: the receiving book (Frick's Exhibit 2, "the receiving book of the said station") beside the inspector's 1923 notebook (Exhibit 4). Kyle's point: the receiving book showed what the Green received from the Hollow (the heavy cream), the notebook showed the condemned pipettes; laid side by side they would have answered the question in 1925. Duncan never lists the receiving book; Nym's Item 16 is Larrow Green's receiving and churn books (Sill's), not the station's — the station's own receiving book (Frick 2; Kyle 2) is accessioned nowhere in Nym's sixteen items.
172. **Where the board sat**: Falke: "at Ashlin from the 17th of March to the 4th of May" — no station sittings mentioned; Frick, Duncan, Cleve, Kyle, **Orra**: one at Ashlin, two at the station ("partly at Ashlin and partly at the station" — Kyle; "Two of the sittings held up here at the station itself" — Orra). Falke's "It heard the patrons and the officers" = Effie's award text "having heard the patrons and the officers."
173. **When the crate went up**: Ivy "June 1923"; Duncan "in 1923"; Chronicle "since 1923"; Cleve "when the state man made them throw it out"; **Falke "where it had lain since the day it was condemned" (12 June 1923)**; Strawn 1928 "loft full since before the war" — Falke is the most precise and the least likely (the glass was in use till the new lot came "within the month"; Orra: new glass in July).
174. **The by-laws' face**: Nym Item 1 "undated"; Falke: "only the words 'adopted at the annual meeting'"; Effie 1892; Rundle 1891; Grigg 1893. Falke is the only source to quote what the pamphlet says instead of a year, and the only official to decline to date it.
