175. **Falke's "the herds, the pasture, the season, the men and the machinery were the same throughout" (1911)** — vs Kepp's "Six weeks in the flush of the season… the stock are off the wet ground and up on the aftermath"; Falke's phrase is the bulletin's argument restated; Ivy: "an experiment… nobody knew it was one." **Orra: the borrowed measure began 8 May in rain with "the low piece under water again."**
176. **Ivy "of that department"**: Falke makes her a graduate student of dairy husbandry, Vaught's department; Chronicle "a graduate student there"; Rosalie "a graduate student at Farland"; Nym "of the State Agricultural College at Farland."
177. **Kyle's tenures**: Teague "forty years" (1905–1945 = 40 — exact); Selby "a quarter of a century" (1913–1938 = 25 — exact, though Kyle dates Selby from 1911); Kyle himself "twenty-five years" at the outer desk, plus his boyhood "on the copying"; born c. 1885; the office moved 1938. If Kyle's twenty-five desk-years were 1913–1938 they coincide exactly with Selby's; if they ended earlier ("I stayed on some years after [the award] and then the office was moved"), they began earlier.
178. **Where Strawn told the manager**: Strawn's 12 June 1923 entry is headed "Ostrey Hollow… told the manager to order new" (at the station, by the entry's heading); Selby 1913: "came out with me to the platform"; **Kyle: Strawn "came into the office… to say so and to tell the manager to order new"** (at Larrow Green, after being "up to the Hollow"). Both may be true (told at the station, repeated at the office); Kyle "filed his word for it" — a written word from Strawn in the office files, not among Nym's items.
179. **Kyle's "new glass came up within the month" / Junia's "we shipped in the month" / Falke's "in use from the following month" / Selby's "ordered new inside the month" / Strawn's October "They deliver their mark" / Orra's "July 1923… new glass at the station"** — all consistent with July 1923 delivery and an October check.
180. **Kyle's moral vs the others' morals**: Kyle: "It was not a secret. It was worse than a secret. It was in plain sight with a name on it"; Effie: "The interesting part is generally what none of them thought worth saying"; Teague: "furniture"; Rundle: "I cannot make my paragraph stand"; Strawn: "A theory… gets in the way of the weights"; Rosalie: "a ghost"; Chronicle: "the trouble might be furniture"; Hazel: "a burette and a straight face"; Ivy: "It would have been a kindness if somebody in this county had thought to climb a ladder"; Frick: "the answer was in that room and simply was not in any of the three stories"; Duncan: "the finding found nothing and the dismissal said everything"; Cleve: "Well. It sat there"; Falke: "No person connected the one thing with the other at the time"; **Orra (1897): "A man is pleased about small things when he is let"; (1902) "it is my figures they will look at"; (1925) "Twenty-nine seasons and a letter… There was ham. He ate it"; (1929) "now it is settled the way those things get settled"; (1930) "there is less in it every year, which is not because less happens"; (1934) "a man cannot be cleared of a thing nobody will name."**
181. **Kyle's Selby "read the totals and… never read the lines above them… thumb on the bottom figure, and sign"** — vs Teague (1948): "I put it to Mr. Vose in 1916 and was told to let it alone" (so Selby was told of the station-gain line at least once and chose to let it be); vs Selby (1926): "a piece of housekeeping that appears in our own order book with a date and a price against it" (he read the order book). Kyle: "courteous to every man… farmer or drummer or bank" — Junia's drummer "sits in the office and waits" if "the association's manager is out in his fields that day."
182. **Kyle typed the circular; Vira called it "a printed letter over the manager's signature"; Hazel "nine letters"; Orra "A circular… over the manager's name… them… to eleven families."** Nine typed copies, or one typed original printed nine (or eleven) times; Kyle: "in a civil form of words but with no doubt at all in it about what was meant" — Selby (1926): "It named nobody. It accused nobody"; Sill: "the only plain thing that office ever did." **Orra: the copies "came to the station for the patrons" — sent up in a bundle for the tester to hand out, not mailed from the office to the farms; Ansel "would not put them in the mail nor leave them on the platform."**
183. **Falke on the inspector's start, "from 1908 forward"** — Strawn's own extracts begin 1907 ("1907 scales true, tester 'slow and clean'"); Ivy also "from 1908"; Falke evidently took it from the bulletin; the 1907 entry is the first mention of Keddie's "slow and clean" hand.
184. **Falke and the Chronicle**: Falke calls the Chronicle piece only "newspaper notice"; he repeats its "great-niece" and corrects it; he does not repeat its $19,600, its eleven pipettes, its $77.39, its "committee… sat again," its "niece" for Rosalie, or its accusation against Ammon & Sons; he calls the bulletin "temperate" where the Chronicle called the firm's letter proof of guilt.
185. **Kyle never mentions the crate, the loft, the pipettes' measurement, the nine weeks of 1911, Ivy, the bulletin, or any cause** — writing in 1953 he could not; his account is the last "before" voice (after Frick 1946, Teague 1948, Junia 1949), and the only office voice that describes the manager's and bookkeeper's persons; he alone reports Emmet lending the weigh book "for the purpose" of the 1925 hearing "and wanted back."

186. **Orra's birth year — 1866 (her diary and its editor) vs 1868 (Duncan)**: the editor's preface "Orra Keddie was born in 1866"; Orra, August 1930: "I am sixty-four" (1930 − 64 = 1866); Duncan (1968): "born in eighteen sixty-eight… She was eighty-two when she died in 1948" (1948 − 82 = 1866). Duncan's age for her at death agrees with the diary; his birth year does not. Conflict 116 resolved in the diary's favor: Orra born 1866, twelve years after Emmet (1854), twenty-three at her marriage (1889), thirty-one when she began the diary, sixty-eight when Ansel died, eighty-two at her death. Duncan's one checkable error is his mother's birth year — the one date of hers he could not have witnessed.
187. **Eleven families vs nine**: Orra (June 1919): Ansel "took them round himself, to eleven families"; Vira: "nine families"; Hazel: "nine families, six farms… nine letters"; Sill: "nine families all got honest in one summer"; Keddie's letter: "nine kitchens have the letter in a drawer"; Effie: seven farms; Roy/Grigg/Kepp/Hazel/Chronicle: six farms; Strawn 1909: "wet bottom below and three hill places." Nine has four sources, all of them counting Hessel Bottom; Orra's eleven is a count of doors Ansel stood at — possibly the whole patron list of the station (the bottom's nine plus the hill places, or eleven households on the six farms), possibly the wife's exact tally against the daughters' round one. Orra is the only source who counted from the tester's end; Kyle, who typed the circular, gives no number of copies.
188. **Who carried the circular, and what Emmet held against Ansel**: Roy (1955): Emmet and Ansel "from 1919… did not speak… because my father held that the man who kept the test book ought to have stood up in front of that circular and did not." Orra (June 1919): Ansel "would not put them in the mail nor leave them on the platform. He took them round himself, to eleven families, and stood at the door while each man read it" — he did, literally, stand in front of the circular at every door; and Emmet's grievance two days later was the opposite of Roy's: "why he had carried the paper himself if he did not believe it. A. said he carried it because he would not have another man carry it. He went home. He has not been in this house since." So by Emmet's sister, Emmet broke with Ansel for carrying the paper; by Emmet's son, for not standing up against it. The two are reconcilable only if "stood up in front of" means "spoke out against" (Roy) rather than "delivered in person" (Orra) — Ansel delivered it and did not denounce it; Emmet wanted the second and got the first. Vira: "the man who kept the test book" is "the one that letter cost the most" — Emmet — though she describes no delivery. Hazel: "He sent that round." Kyle: "it went up to the Ostrey Hollow patrons."
189. **Four men in Grigg's sleigh vs a committee of three**: Grigg (1919): "we three were named"; the 1908 report signed "NYLE GRIGG, chairman, and the committee"; Effie/Roy/Rundle/Frick "a committee of three"; Falke "A committee of the Association"; Orra (January 1908): "Four men up the road today in Grigg's sleigh, come to look into the herds." The fourth may be a driver, a hired man, a clerk, or a fourth member unrecorded elsewhere; Orra counted the men in the sleigh, not the names on the report.
190. **"They wanted to see the barns and not the station"**: Orra (January 1908): the committee "went to my brother Emmet's first… They wanted to see the barns and not the station, which A. remarked on afterward and let alone"; the 1908 report itself: "we… saw the herds milked, the cans filled, hauled and weighed in, and stood by while the test was made… tried the station scales against a known weight and the patrons' scales against the station's." Either the committee came to the station on a later visit (they "visited each farm three and four times"; Orra's entry is of one day), or the report overstates what it did at the station, or Orra records Ansel's grievance at the first visit only. Falke (1959): "practical men who walked the ground and looked into the mangers" — barns, not the station, as Orra says. Kepp: "went over the ground." The point Ansel "remarked on afterward and let alone" is the one Ivy made in 1958: nobody looked at the station.
191. **Duncan never mentions his mother's diary**: Orra's three notebooks were "in the family's hands" and in print by 1964; Duncan's "Notes for the family" (1968, added to 1970) list every book and where it went — the test book, the weigh book, Strawn's notebooks, the Alder Corners book, the crate — and of Orra say only "She had kept every letter and she left them to me… only the weather and the price of things and her brother's health, and I am keeping them anyway," and that she "never spoke of the business except once a year, in April." A diary that records 1902, 1908, 1911, 1919, 1923, 1925, 1929 and 1934 is not a woman who never spoke of it; and "the five books are now in three buildings and are none of them ours" (1970) omits the one family record that was theirs. Either Duncan did not count a diary as a "book" of the case, or the notebooks were in Ivy's hands (not his) and published by her ("the family's hands"; the editor unnamed — Ivy, who "has spent ten years putting the case right in print," 1958–68, is the obvious editor, and the 1964 extracts would be one of those ten years' printings), or Duncan's silence is deliberate. Duncan's description of the letters — "the weather and the price of things" — fits the diary's editor's selection ("chosen for what they show of the household and the season") almost word for word.
192. **Duncan married before March 1925**: Orra (17 March 1925): Ansel's "good coat, which he has had since Duncan was married" — Duncan (born 1894) was married by 1925, aged at most thirty-one; Ivy was born 1931 ("my father's granddaughter"); Duncan's wife is named nowhere in any source; Duncan's own notes say nothing of his marriage. The coat was bought for the wedding and worn to the hearing.
193. **Ansel a native of the hollow**: Orra (July 1923): "He has been in this hollow the whole of his life"; (June 1925) the cow shed "has wanted doing since his father's time" — Ansel's father held the Keddie place at Ostrey Hollow before him. Vira: "went up in '95"; Hazel: "went up to that station in ninety-five"; Frick: Ansel "had lived among them thirty years" (1895–1925) — Frick's "thirty years" would be wrong by Orra (a lifetime, not thirty years); Duncan: "among men he had weighed milk for since before their fathers died"; Cleve: "the tester there from before I was born." "Went up" (Vira, Hazel) means up the road to the station, not up the valley to the hollow. Ansel was born 1861 — thirty-five years in the hollow before the station existed.
194. **The Loomis gate**: Orra (October 1927): Ansel "mending stairs at the far end of the hollow, and the Loomis gate, and half the doors in this hollow" — a Loomis household at Ostrey Hollow in 1927. Effie Loomis, secretary 1901–1940, wrote (1941) "Not one of us ever went up the valley… I include myself first" — if the hollow Loomises are her people, she had kin at the head of the valley she never visited; if not, two unrelated Loomis families in one small valley. No source connects them.
195. **"Twenty-nine seasons and a letter"**: Orra's is the only account of the manner of the dismissal — by letter, received or delivered on 30 April 1925; Frick: "He was simply let go while the board was still sitting"; Duncan: "dismissed on the thirtieth of April"; Chronicle: "dismissed after a lifetime at that station"; Cleve: "They dismissed Keddie." Who signed the letter (Selby, as manager, presumably) is unstated; it is not among Nym's sixteen items, Duncan's five books, or Kyle's office papers; Ansel worked the day out ("came up the road at his ordinary hour").
196. **Orra's mother and the Brant kitchen**: Orra (spring 1929): "I sat an hour in that kitchen where I was a girl" — she grew up in the Brant house on Hessel Bottom, Emmet's house; (July 1929) hollyhocks "my mother had." By Hazel, Orra's mother was Emmet's father's second wife (Emmet's stepmother); by Roy, the same woman was Emmet's own mother ("same mother and same father"). Orra's "my mother" and Emmet's "his father" (per Orra, March 1908: "the bottom fed his father") do not settle it. Orra kept the diary from 1897 in the Keddie house at Ostrey Hollow, up the road from the station; she was a Brant of the bottom by birth.
197. **The diary's April 1897 start and the weigh book's January 1897 start**: two Brant-family records begun in the same year — Emmet's weigh book (1 January 1897 by four sources) and his sister's diary (4 April 1897, "in the spring of 1897") — a month before the station's own glass came (1 May 1897). Neither mentions the other. The diary's first entry is about the mare and the eggs; the weigh book's first entries are cans.
198. **Orra's diary and the 1911 supper**: Duncan (1968): Ansel "came home that summer of 1911 saying it was something in the glass… he said it twice, at supper and again by the door, which was not his way." Orra's diary has "May 8, 1911. A. home cross about a broken measure" and then nothing until "October 1911. Duncan gone to the school at Ashlin." Either the supper remark fell in the unprinted entries (the editor chose entries "for what they show of the household and the season"), or Ansel said it to the boy and not to the wife, or Duncan's memory improved the evening. A "broken measure" that made him "cross" on 8 May is not a man who yet suspected the glass; the higher tests came after.
199. **The torn leaves of 1926**: "[Several leaves torn out. The editor has not attempted to supply them. — Ed.]" — the one year of the diary with nothing printed and nothing printable; 1926 is the year of Selby's annual address ("Water was going into cans at the head of that valley and it stopped when the Association began to look") and Kepp's Farm Club paper, the first full year of Ansel's disgrace. Who tore them out — Orra, Ansel, Duncan, Ivy — and whether before or after 1934, is unstated; the leaves are the only known destruction of a Keddie record (compare Hazel's mother's stove and the 1919 letter).
200. **Orra's silences**: no award (the editor says so); no condemnation, no Strawn, no crate, no loft, no burette (only "new glass… and new pails," July 1923); no Vose by name; no 10 July 1911; no gap figure; no families-and-farms count but eleven doors; no Alder Corners, no ridge; no Ivy (born the year after the diary closed); no mention of Duncan's marriage except the coat; no weather for June 1919 (Vira: rain); no Sunday dinners with Emmet at any date (Duncan's "thirty-six years"); no drainage arguments (Hazel's "forty years"); no cousinship talk. Her positives: the mare, the eggs, the strawberries, twenty below, currants, apple trees, hollyhocks, threshing dinners, the ham — "the household and the season," as the editor says.
201. **"He was up at three" (17 March 1925)** — Ansel's ordinary hour was four ("A. broke the path to the station himself at four o'clock," 1898; "up before four every day of his life," 1924; Keddie's letter: "day began half past five" — the tester's day at the station, after the walk); on the morning of the hearing "up at three and did not sleep before that either." Frick's corridor conversation and Keddie's nine-sentence statement were made by a man who had not slept.
202. **The Keddie house within earshot of the station**: Orra (April 1925): "I could hear the wagons from the yard all that afternoon"; (30 April 1925) "He came up the road at his ordinary hour"; (1919) Emmet "came up… stood in the yard"; (1897) Ansel "walked it both ways." The house is up the road from the station, near enough to hear wagons on the station road, far enough that a lame mare made the walk a hardship in April mud. Vira was born "at the top end of Ostrey Hollow"; the station is "at the head of the Hollow where the road bends" (Rundle); the Keddie place lies between, or above.
203. **Orra's "the men standing outside in the road" vs Cleve's "on the platform… in the rain, with about a dozen others"**: Cleve stood on the station's covered platform on the road side (his own description of the building); Orra, who "did not go down," heard of men "outside in the road"; the platform fronts the road. Cleve's rain and Orra's dry account may be different afternoons (there were two station sittings) or the same one seen from a yard up the road.
204. **The diary's editor and the family**: the extracts are "printed from the three notebooks now in the family's hands" (1964); the editor writes "[Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958…]" without naming the bulletin, Vaught, Ivy, or the crate; "the spelling of two place names" was altered (which two is unstated — Ostrey/Larrow/Hessel/Redlow/Ashlin/Tarnet/Farland are all candidates); the editor calls Ansel "tester and separator man" (Duncan's phrase, Falke's phrase). The family in 1964: Duncan (seventy), Ivy (thirty-three, with children), Ivy's unnamed mother if living. The editor is not named anywhere; the diary is not in Nym's 1972 accession list — still "in the family's hands" in 1972, or elsewhere.
205. **Sources' knowledge of one another, updated**: Orra's diary (1897–1934) predates every other source's composition except the 1908 report, Grigg's 1919 letter, Keddie's 1925 papers and Selby's 1926 address — and it was written without knowledge of any of them as documents (she records the committee's coming and going, the circular's arrival, the petition, the hearing, the dismissal — the events, not the papers). Its publication (1964) postdates the bulletin (1958), the Chronicle (1958), Falke (1959), Ivy's talk (1959), Rundle's footnote (1959) and Kyle (1953), and predates Duncan (1968), Nym (1972), Cleve (1977), Vira (1979), Hazel (1981) — none of whom cite it; Nym does not accession it; Cleve, Vira and Hazel speak as if no Keddie record but Duncan's and Ivy's existed. The diary corroborates Vira/Keddie/Roy on the 1919 break (against Duncan and Hazel), Nym/Junia/Chronicle on 1 May 1897 (against Vaught/Kyle/Hazel), the 1908 report on January–March (against Grigg's 1919 letter), Cleve on the carpentry and the apron, Frick/Duncan/Falke on 30 April 1925, Duncan on 1861, 1889, 1894, 1934 and 1948, and corrects Duncan on 1866.

---

## KEY OBJECTS AND DOCUMENTS
- **The station test book** (Keddie: five columns; Nym: four; first leaf 4 May 1896; 1922 summary leaf; 1911 entries with "? — A.K."; half-bound, marbled boards, cloth-mended back; one hand, 29 seasons; impounded March 1925; Ashlin office 1925–59; the Rooms 1959; Nym Item 3 "kept by E. Brant"; Frick Exhibit 1; Hazel: "all the tests that were already in the book"; **Duncan: "My father kept it in his own hand from 1896 until he left. In March of 1925 the board of arbitration impounded it, and after the hearing it went into the Cooperative office at Ashlin, where it lay from 1925 until 1959. In 1959 it went to the Ordell County Historical Rooms in this town, where it is now"; "What he wrote in the book was a question mark"; "the figures were what convicted him"**; Cleve: "the books and the scales and the machinery were all up there"; **Kyle: "The station's own test book, in Keddie's hand" — first of the four books he carried in; Falke: "The tester noted the fact in his book and set a question mark against it"; Orra's editor: Ansel "kept its books in his own hand for twenty-nine seasons"; Orra (1902): "it is my figures they will look at."**)
- **The station receiving book** (Frick Exhibit 2 — **and Kyle's second book: "The receiving book"**; not in Duncan's list; not among Nym's items; Kyle: nobody "laid the second beside the fourth and looked").
- **The Brant farm weigh book** (Nym Item 4: small quarto, sheepskin, greasy corners, from 1 Jan 1897; Roy: hand-ruled ledger from 1 Jan 1898, two weight columns, cover gone, harness-leather strip, forty stained leaves, "on the shelf behind my chair"; **Hazel: "a tall narrow account book, canvas over board," spine soft on the left, begun 1 January 1900, both weights side by side; carried under Emmet's arm barn to house**; 1910 leaf, "even" underlined; to Roy 1929; lent to Ivy 1958 ("he lent it. He did not leave it to her" — Hazel; "lent, and I want the word… and had it back" — Duncan) and returned; to Hazel 1962 (at Roy's death — Duncan); the Rooms 1963 ("down the street at the historical rooms in this town" — Hazel 1981; "Hazel gave it to the Historical Rooms in 1963" — Duncan); a second volume; Frick Exhibit 3 "begun 1 January 1897"; **Duncan: "Emmet Brant began it on the first of January, 1897, and kept it until his death in 1929, when it went to his son Roy"**; **Kyle: "Mr. Emmet Brant's private weigh book, which he lent for the purpose and wanted back" — lent to the 1925 board.** Orra never mentions it.)
- **Orra Keddie's diary — "A Farm Woman's Book, 1897–1930"** (Source 24): three ruled notebooks bought at Ashlin; begun 4 April 1897 ("in the spring of 1897"), closed August 1930 ("I have kept this book thirty-three years"); "She writes A. for her husband throughout"; "The pages covering her girlhood and her family before 1889 were not preserved; nothing in her hand survives from before the marriage"; a loose leaf of February 1934 "laid loose inside the back cover of the third book"; "Several leaves torn out" for 1926; extracts published 1964 by an unnamed editor "from the three notebooks now in the family's hands," entries "chosen for what they show of the household and the season; nothing has been added and nothing altered but the spelling of two place names"; bracketed editor's notes on the award (not mentioned), the torn leaves, and the 1958 clearing; not among Nym's 1972 items; not among Duncan's "five books" (1970); never cited by Duncan, Nym, Cleve, Vira or Hazel. **Orra's letters** (Duncan: "She had kept every letter and she left them to me… only the weather and the price of things and her brother's health, and I am keeping them anyway") — a separate hoard, also with the family, also not accessioned.
- **Strawn's 1923 circuit notebook** (Frick Exhibit 4; state office 1936; Farland 1947; photostat Nym Item 9; **Duncan: "one for every year he rode the Ordell circuit… to the state dairy office when he retired in 1936… in 1947… transferred to the library of the State Agricultural College at Farland, which is where Ivy found the volume for 1923 eleven years later. Nobody had asked for them in the interval"**; **Chronicle: "the state dairy office, for the loan of an old inspector's notebooks"; "He wrote down that he had condemned them. He wrote down nothing about what they might have been doing for the twenty-six years before."**; **Kyle: "a little book with a rubber band round it"; "the state inspector's notebook for the year he condemned the glass" — the fourth book before the board; Falke: "the inspector himself drew no inference whatever as to the years behind it."**)
- **Sill's Larrow Green receiving book** (= Nym Item 16). **Sill's own glass outfit** (1896–97; Kyle: "borrowed its testing glass from the creamery here"; **Orra, 1 May 1897: "done borrowing of Larrow Green"**). **The office's "sheet"** to Sill. **The first season's sheets on "the creamery's own books" — "never put with the station papers" (Kyle).**
- **The 1919 printed circular** ("water in the cans"; Vira's desk drawer; a trunk lid; **Hazel's mother's stove**; Selby "It named nobody"; Hazel "the water letter… nine letters… answered by none of them"; Chronicle "a warning to the Ostrey Hollow patrons about added water as early as 1919"; **Kyle: "I typed it… in a civil form of words but with no doubt at all in it about what was meant, and Mr. Vose signed it, and it went out over his name and no one else's"; Falke: "a warning to that effect in 1919"**; **Orra: "A circular came to the station for the patrons, over the manager's name, about water in the milk. A. would not put them in the mail nor leave them on the platform. He took them round himself, to eleven families, and stood at the door while each man read it" — the copies came to the station in a bundle; Ansel was the postman; Emmet two days later: "why he had carried the paper himself if he did not believe it"**; unmentioned by Duncan, Junia, Cleve).
- **The dismissal letter of 30 April 1925** (Orra: "They have put him out. Twenty-nine seasons and a letter" — the only mention of a written dismissal; signer unstated; not accessioned anywhere).
- **Article VII** (Effie 1892; Rundle 1891; Grigg 1893; quoted identically by Effie, Nym, Rundle; paraphrased by Frick and Grigg's report; Chronicle: "Every patron in the valley was paid on that test. Not on the weight of his milk — on the fat in it, as the test found the fat"; **Falke: "the article, numbered seven, under which every patron is paid according to the pounds of butterfat his milk is found by test to contain, and not according to the weight of the milk; and under which the test made at the station where he delivers is the test that governs his pay… the hinge of the whole matter"**). **The by-laws' arbitration clause.** **Gideon Vose's by-laws** (Duncan: Gideon "wrote its by-laws"; **Falke: "The printed by-laws carry no date upon their face, only the words 'adopted at the annual meeting'"**).
- **The 1908 committee report** (Nym Item 11; full text in Source 15; "received and accepted"; read into the record 1925; Emmet's drawer; **Kepp: "A committee of practical men went over the ground in 1908"**; Chronicle: "The committee that sat on the matter in 1908"; **Falke: "practical men who walked the ground and looked into the mangers"**; **Orra: "Four men up the road today in Grigg's sleigh… They wanted to see the barns and not the station" (January 1908); "The committee gone back down. Their word is that it is the wet hay of the bottom that thins the cream" (March 1908)**). **The 1919 reprint with Grigg's covering letter.** **Grigg's sleigh** (January 1908).
- **The 1925 petition** ($19,600 — Rundle; 67,000 lb — Frick; the same two figures resurface in 1958 as the Chronicle's "$19,600" and in 1977 as Cleve's "Sixty-seven thousand pounds"; **Falke: "The patrons petitioned in January of 1925"; Kyle: "The patrons had petitioned"; Orra: "The patrons have put in a paper against the station. My brother Emmet's name is on it" (January 1925) — the only source to name a signer**). **The 1925 award** ($3,800; Duncan: "what it called its finding"; Chronicle: "'without admission,' which is lawyer's language for we are paying you and we are not saying why"; Cleve: $3,500, "no admission of anything by anybody"; **Falke: "three thousand eight hundred dollars, without admission"; Kyle: "thirty-eight hundred dollars… without admission of any cause. That was in twenty-four. It was charged to the station account"; Orra's diary: "[The award of the board is not mentioned in the diary. — Ed.]"**). **Frick's exhibit list** (= **Kyle's list of four, "checked… off twice"**). **The board's minutes.** **Keddie's statement and unsent letter.**
- **Teague's station-gain line** (1898 entry; quoted by Rundle; Sill's cold-room weighing; Chronicle's "furniture"; **Kyle: "three figures and a sentence… five and a quarter per cent, as usual, carried to the general account… It had a name"; the "station account for Ostrey Hollow" in which it stood and to which the award was charged**).
- **The crate** (AMMON & SONS DAIRY SUPPLY TARNET; deal; six dozen 1897 — Vaught five dozen, **Cleve five dozen** — **Hazel "came out from Tarnet in ninety-nine"**; **Orra, 1 May 1897: "the station's own glass came up"**; nine survive, two chipped — Rundle eleven, **Chronicle eleven**; loft June 1923 / Ashlin office; **Hazel: "the loft over the weigh room… under a tarpaulin and about an inch of chaff"**; **Duncan: "The station's condemned glassware went up into the loft over the intake room in 1923 and stayed there until Ivy found it in February of 1958. It went east to Farland to be measured and came back, and in 1959 it was deposited with the Historical Rooms along with the test book. Twenty feet of ladder, thirty-five years, and two of the three sessions of that arbitration sat in the room underneath it"**; **Cleve: "a crate up in that loft with a tarpaulin over it, and I have seen it a hundred times, and I have put my hand on it… Bottles, and the little glass measures… Five dozen… nine of them left… gets broken by everybody who goes up after wire… It was a crate under a tarp"; Chronicle: "a wooden crate that had been sitting there under the dust since 1923. In it was the station's original glassware"**; **Junia: packed "by the dozen, in straw, in a wooden case with the customer's name in blue pencil on the end"**; **Falke: "the condemned glass, still crated, in the loft over the station's intake room, where it had lain since the day it was condemned"; "the nine surviving pipettes… delivered materially less than the capacity blown upon their stems, and in the same proportion, one to another"**; the Rooms 1959; "wants a case with a lock"; not among Frick's or Kyle's four books; never mentioned by Orra).
- **The invoice** 14 April 1897 ($77.93; "8 per cent neck"; **reproduced in facsimile by Junia 1949, who then writes "seventy-seven dollars and thirty-nine cents"; the Chronicle 1958: "The Chronicle has seen the invoice. It is a tidy document in a clerk's copperplate, ruled and totaled, and it is one of the more expensive pieces of paper this county has ever had in it" — and prints $77.39**). **Ammon & Sons letter** 2 July 1923 (**quoted by Junia: "Your order of the 28th ultimo for six dozen milk pipettes and six dozen test bottles is entered and will go forward this month. We regret the trouble. Our books of the nineties are not such that we can tell you anything about the lot you name"**; Nym Item 10 "declining to say anything of the earlier lot"; Chronicle: "said that their books of the nineties would not tell anybody anything about the lot in question"). **The Association's letter of 28 June 1923** (not in hand; **Kyle: "I entered the order"**). **The Association's correspondence** (**Kyle: "which I handled later when it was all got out for the hearing"; from it he dates the outfit 1 May 1898**). **Ammon's works books** (burned 1931 — "what glass, what gauge, what man, what day"), **order book, letter books, day books** (Junia). **Mr. Marcus's motto** lettered in the packing room ("a creamery buys arithmetic from us and not glassware"). **The gauge on a nail, the mold ("reground"), the lehr, the cullet barrel** (Junia's account of the short lot). **Strawn's "word," filed by Kyle** in the office (1923). **The new pails of July 1923** (Orra only).
- **Strawn's burette** (1921; discontinued 1926; **Hazel: "some measuring instrument he carried in his coat"**; "a burette and a straight face"; Junia: "an inspector for the state went into that station with a burette"; Chronicle: "he ran their measures against a burette"; **Kyle: "run their milk pipettes against his burette"; Falke: "against a calibrated burette"**), **sealed weights**, **lactometer** (**Falke: "tested with the lactometer at every visit from 1908 forward"**), **twenty-eight circuit books** (Duncan: "one for every year"; Kyle: "a little book with a rubber band round it").
- **Ivy's burette sheet** 9 March 1958 (Hazel: "took the glass back east with her and measured it"; Duncan: "It went east to Farland to be measured and came back"; Chronicle: "measured them, one by one, against a calibrated burette in a college laboratory… The average of the lot came to 16.27 c.c."; **Falke: "On the 9th of March she measured the nine surviving pipettes against a calibrated burette at Farland"** — no mean given). Vaught's method; his mean 16.27.
- **Bulletin No. 214** (1911, bog hay; Ivy/Vaught ≤0.05 in winter, nothing in summer; **Grigg and Kepp: "two tenths in the winter feeding"**; Nym Item 12; Kepp: "a feeding trial upon bog hay… under control… I commend it to every man here"; **Falke obtained it from "the library at Farland" after his report went to type and prints its summary "entire": "Bog hay fed as the whole of the roughage depressed the butter fat test by not more than five hundredths of one per cent in winter, and by nothing that could be measured on summer pasture"; "that trial being cited in the 1958 bulletin without its figures"**) and **Bulletin No. 471** (October 1958; **Chronicle: "under two names: Professor Leland Vaught… and Miss Ivy Keddie"; acknowledgments name Vaught, the state dairy office, Mrs. Rosalie Cudd**; **Falke: "issued in October of last year… prepared jointly… temperate in tone and does not… overstate its case"; "the bulletin treats [1911] as decisive"; "as the bulletin now fixes it" (0.19); "The bulletin's conclusion is that a short charge of milk into a bottle whose neck is ruled for the full charge yields a reading short in the same proportion; that this and nothing else is the explanation; and that no milk was watered and no cream was stolen at Ostrey Hollow"**; **Orra's editor: "Ansel Keddie's name was cleared in 1958, twenty-four years after his death"**). **The college press release** (Chronicle: "generous about it in the way press releases are").
- **Jerome Cudd's Alder Corners test book** (Duncan: "Jerome Cudd kept it from the founding of his company; it passed to his daughter Rosalie in 1941… in 1958 she had the figures copied out and sent to Ivy at the college. The book itself has never been out of Vessey County"; 1970 to the Vessey County Historical Society — Nym Item 13, Duncan's 1970 addition; Chronicle: Rosalie "went into her family's records on a stranger's say-so") and **Rosalie's 1958 letter** with the table.
- **Ivy's "ten years… in print"** (1958–68 — Duncan; Bulletin 471 and "What the Crate Held" are the two in hand; **the 1964 diary extracts, if Ivy edited them, would be a third**; the Chronicle piece and Falke's report are about her, not by her).
- **Roy Brant's 1955 account** (postscript 1958). **Dell Rundle's pamphlet** (1939; 1959). **Frick's 1946 article.** **Sill's 1934 remarks.** **Grigg's 1919 letter.** **Kepp's 1926 paper "The bog meadows and the milk"** (Farm Club; secretary's discussion notes; "printed for the members"). **Hazel Brant's 1981 tape** (two afternoons; interviewer's prompts in brackets; no papers). **Duncan Keddie's 1968 "Notes for the family"** (five sections I–V, "the sections are his own"; addition of 1970; written for Ivy and her children). **Vira's 1979 tape.** **Effie's 1941 Notes.** **Teague's 1948 chapter.** **Strawn's 1936 extracts.** **Nym's 1972 notes.** **Ivy's 1959 talk.** **Selby's 1926 address.** **Keddie's 1925 statement and letter.** **Rosalie's 1958 letter.** **Vaught's 1958 bulletin sections.** **The Ashlin Chronicle's front page, 3 October 1958** (headline, subhead, seven sections: "A quarrel older than the automobile," "The hearing, and the man who paid for it," "A crate in the loft," "What it cost," "And the house that sold them?", "The girl from Ashlin"; Ivy's telephone quotation). **Junia Ammon's "Sixty Years of Ammon & Sons" (Tarnet, 1949), chapter IX** (two facsimiles: the invoice and the letter). **Cleve Oram's 1977 tape** (back room of the Larrow Green feed store; "Transcribed as spoken"). **Orin Falke's "The Ostrey Hollow question: a report for the county board" (1959)** — thirteen numbered paragraphs, "page two" (¶2, the great-niece error) and "page four" (¶9, the hay concession), the "Note added on receipt of Bulletin No. 214," filed "with the county's dairy papers" / "the county's permanent file"; signed "— O. Falke, county agricultural agent, Ordell County." **Bertram Kyle's "What I saw of the office" (1953)** — written at sixty-eight for the Ordell County Historical Society; the outer desk, the general ledger and station accounts, the annual statement, the creamery inventory, the fair-hand copy for the annual meeting, the typed circular, the 1923 order entry, the list of four books "checked… off twice," the moral. **Orra Keddie's "A Farm Woman's Book, 1897–1930" (extracts, 1964)** — preface; thirty-four dated entries from 4 April 1897 to August 1930 (April 4 1897; May 1 1897; July 19 1897; February 3 1898; September 12 1899; March 1902; June 6 1904; January 1908; March 1908; August 30 1910; May 8 1911; October 1911; April 1914; November 1917; June 1919; June 1919 two days after; Christmas 1919; February 1921; July 1923; November 1924; January 1925; March 17 1925; April 1925; April 30 1925; [May 1925]; June 1925; [1926]; October 1927; Spring 1929; July 1929; August 1930); the loose leaf of February 1934; the editor's close.
- **The Association's order book** (Selby: 1923 replacement "with a date and a price"; **Kyle: "I entered the order"**). **The 1926 annual report.** **The Association's old ledgers** (Rundle 1939; Teague; Nym Item 6; **Kyle: "a general ledger and a set of station accounts… carried forward… laid before the annual meeting in a fair hand, which was mine"; "the station account for Ostrey Hollow"; "the general account"; "a book that stood on an open shelf, in a room that anybody might walk into"**). **The annual statement (winter), the creamery inventory ("when the ice went out"), the station accounts (summer), the annual meeting** — Kyle's clerk's year.
- **Hazel's marriage certificate** ("in the front room"). **Ansel's good coat** ("which he has had since Duncan was married"; ironed twice, 17 March 1925). **Ansel's apron** (Cleve: canvas, "the strap over one shoulder"; Orra: "rolled under his arm… hung it behind the shed door," 30 April 1925). **The ham** (30 April 1925: "There was ham. He ate it").
- **The Vose Jerseys** (4.56 in 1910). **The Brant herd** (38 cows; 200,000 lb in 1910 at 3.42). **The Bottom herds** (Grigg 1908: thin in the flank, rough coats; Sill: "good cows"; **Kepp: "thin over the loin and rough in the coat… slow to come to condition… ready subjects for every disorder of the digestion in the fall… breed later. Their feet are worse"**; Chronicle: "the cows were ordinary"; **Falke: "The herds, the pasture, the season, the men and the machinery were the same throughout"**; **Orra (1902): "it is not your cows"**). **Hill herds vs bottom herds** (Kepp). **Ansel's "four or five cows" after 1925** (Cleve) and **the cow shed "which has wanted doing since his father's time"** (Orra, June 1925). **The Keddie mare** (lame, April 1897). **The farmers' hogs** (Cleve: skim "to take home to their hogs"). **Kyle's "perhaps four cows in twenty-five years, and two of those were in a photograph."**
- **Scales**: state-tested true (Strawn; Selby; 1908 committee; Frick "We saw the scales tried"; Chronicle "The scales were true"); Brant's barn scale agreeing with the station's (Roy; **Hazel: "His scale and theirs agreeing"**); Rosalie's platform scale; Sill's cold-room scale; Cleve: "the intake room, with the scale and the vat."
- **Money**: $3,800 award (Vira, Cleve $3,500; Falke, Kyle $3,800; Orra silent); $19,000 unpaid (76,000 lb × 25¢; Vaught 118,000 lb; Rundle and Chronicle $19,600; Frick and Cleve 67,000 lb); $77.93 outfit (Chronicle and Junia's prose $77.39); pipettes 35 cents apiece (Chronicle "at thirty-five cents apiece"); 24¢ (1910) / ~25¢ (**Kyle: "averaged twenty-five cents the pound, taking the fat years with the lean"**) / **27¢ (Sill; Kepp)** butterfat; monthly cash checks from 1891; Kepp: "a fifth of a point taken off every hundredweight a farm ships… will pay for a great deal of tile"; Chronicle: "in the money of the eighteen-nineties and the years after"; Ivy's work "largely at her own expense"; Junia: the freight rate that kept Ammon's out of Ordell County; **Falke: "Under payment by weight nothing that follows in this report would have cost any man a dollar"**; **Orra: carpentry "pays a quarter of what the other did" (1927); "the price of things" (Duncan, of her letters).**
- **Station plant**: intake and cream scales; boiler; wash room; cold room; separator (Duncan: Ansel "tester and separator man"; **Falke likewise; Orra's editor likewise**; Cleve: "the separator going in the back of" the intake room); vat (Cleve); loft (**over the weigh room** — Hazel; over the intake room — Rundle, **Duncan**, Chronicle, Cleve, **Falke**; "Twenty feet of ladder" — Duncan; "Twenty feet, if that" — Cleve; **the ladder "nailed to the wall by the door," the trap "a piece of plank with a rope on it," sacks, a broken churn, wire** — Cleve); slope desk by the window; **weigh room** (Frick; Hazel — Emmet in its doorway; Kepp — "shouting… across a weigh room"; Duncan — the hearing room "underneath" the loft; **Cleve — "that is the intake room, the same room, with the scale in it"; Orra — "in the weigh room, with the men standing outside in the road"**); **the covered platform, waist high, on the road side** (Cleve; Orra: Ansel "would not… leave them on the platform"); **shingle roof; board building, two floors** (Cleve; **Orra: "the roof is still bad… never yet seen them mend a roof before it fell in"**); Ansel's **canvas apron** (Cleve; Orra); **new pails** (Orra, July 1923). **Larrow Green plant**: churn room, platform, cold room, Sill's bench; **the feed store's back room** (1977); **the Cooperative's office at Larrow Green — outer room, Kyle's desk, the manager's door, the window, the open shelf (Kyle)**. **Alder Corners plant**: make room; little room off the intake; platform; hoops; vat. **Ammon & Sons' plant**: four stories and a basement, blowing floor, packing room, lehr, annealing oven, the old third floor (burned 1931), railroad sidings (Junia).
- **The cream wagon** (Ivy: the 1911 borrowed measure "came up from Larrow Green with the cream wagon"; Strawn 1912: cream shipped same day; **Cleve: "Two bays and a flat wagon with a rack built onto it to keep the cans standing, and a canvas over them in July"; the box seat, Cleve on the left, the ditch on the right**; **Orra: "the wagons" heard from the yard, April 1925; "the milk stood" in the February 1921 ice storm**); **the truck** that replaced it (Cleve: "the milk went down the valley in a truck by that time," 1946). **The Oram barn door** (hung by Keddie after 1925; "swung true for thirty years"). **The Loomis gate, the stairs "at the far end of the hollow," "half the doors in this hollow"** (Orra, October 1927). **Hay tools in the old station** (mowers, a side rake). **Teague's dinner tin** (Kyle: "ate the same dinner out of the same tin"). **Grigg's sleigh** (Orra, January 1908).
- **The land**: Hessel Bottom's springs (Hazel: cold spring at the upper end where the cans stood; Kepp: springs at the foot of the slope, year round); the soft place by the alders (Roy); acid soil poor in lime and phosphates, sedge, rush, tussock grasses (Kepp); "the aftermath" (Kepp — summer pasture off the wet ground); tile, ditches, lime, seeding, mineral supplement (Kepp's remedy); "the creek" (Duncan; Cleve: "the flat by the creek where the road bends"; "where the creek comes in from the north"); **Falke: "the hay and pasture of Hessel Bottom, which is wet ground"; "the bog meadows of that bottom… the coarseness of their hay"; "the mangers."** **The Keddie land (Orra): "the home piece" (cut July 1897), "the low piece" ("under water again," May 1911), "the lower place" (the hired girl's, 1910), the yard, the shed, the cow shed, six apple trees "below the house" (1914), currants, hollyhocks, the eggs set 4 April 1897 with the ground "still frozen a spade down"; threshing 30 August 1910; "Snow before the corn was in" (November 1917); "Hay in good and not a drop on it" (July 1923); "Killing weather" (November 1924); "Rain every afternoon this week at four o'clock" (June 1904); "Twenty below at the door" (February 1898); wild strawberries "thick along the station road" (July 1897); "Ice storm" (February 1921).**
- **Redlow Ridge road**: four miles of switchback. **The Hollow road**: seven miles; three hours in mud; impassable in March; cream wagon daily but Sunday; "fifty Junes… fifty Januaries"; **Cleve: the pitch two miles below the hamlet; mud to the hub; ruts frozen "in the shape your wheels left them"**; **Orra: "the station road" — strawberries along it, "impassable two days" in February 1921; Ansel "walked it both ways" (April 1897) and "broke the path to the station himself at four o'clock" (February 1898); Duncan "drove… down" to Ashlin (October 1911); Ansel "went down to Ashlin" (17 March 1925); "He came up the road at his ordinary hour" (30 April 1925); Emmet "came up" (June 1919); Orra "went down" to the Brant place (spring 1929); "neither of them would go up the road first."**
- **The schoolhouse at Larrow Green.** **The corridor at Ashlin.** **The Cooperative's office at Ashlin (Cleve; "where the lawyer was") — or at Larrow Green (Kyle).** **The crossroads stores** (**Orra: "Talk at the store," March 1902**). **The Farm Club room (1926).** **The board of supervisors' meeting room (autumn quarter, 1959).** **Hazel's kitchen at Ashlin (1981); the Brant kitchen at Hessel Bottom (Ivy "with her coat on," 1958; Orra: "that kitchen where I was a girl," where the 1908 committee "gave them their dinner" — Emmet's — and where Orra "sat an hour" in 1929); the Keddie kitchen (Duncan, summer 1911: "I remember the kitchen… at supper and again by the door"; Orra: fourteen threshers to dinner, 1910; ham, 30 April 1925; Duncan "listening at the door," 1899); the Keddie yard (Emmet "stood in the yard," June 1919; Orra "could hear the wagons from the yard," April 1925); the Keddie Sunday table (Emmet there "for thirty-six years" — Duncan; "ate at his table" — Hazel; "Sunday dinners before" — Vira; Orra: "He has not been in this house since" June 1919); the Oram kitchen ("how my father swore coming in the door" — Cleve); the supper table of the Chronicle's readers ("too young to have had it argued at them across a supper table"); the Ashlin school (Duncan from October 1911).**
- **1896 first-season intake**: ~900,000 lb (Sill; Nym: no figure; **Kyle: "a good eleven hundred thousand pounds"**).
- **The telephone** (Chronicle: "reached her by telephone at Farland on Thursday evening"; "two telephone calls"). **The notebook-carrying visitors** (Cleve 1977: "every second car that comes up the valley has somebody in it with a notebook"; Vira 1979: "a young man from the county who asked three questions in an hour"; Hazel 1981: "the machine"; **Kyle 1953: "everybody who comes to see me wants the Ostrey Hollow line"; Falke 1959: "three separate patrons within one month"**). **The typewriter** (Kyle typed the 1919 circular). **The photograph** with two cows in it (Kyle). **The three ruled notebooks bought at Ashlin** (Orra's). **Ansel's hat** (Orra, June 1919: "came home at eleven at night with his hat still on"; Vira's father "read it twice with his hat on"; Emmet "with his hat still on" at the kitchen table — Roy; "in the doorway of that weigh room with his hat on" — Hazel; "fifty farmers with their hats on their knees" — Kyle).

*End of Segment 20 notes. Segment 20 added nothing to the story: its seven chunks were the tail of the long-24 miscellany (lines 5,008–6,681 — BolMynt Rounds 85–118, ledger 1865-10-01 through 1867-01-27, gossip Blocks 24 and 28, gibberish ¶202–¶234), and the file stops there, mid-gossip, with no "End of document." Long-24 is finished; it carried one retelling only (Orra Keddie's diary, its "Chapter 20"). Twenty-four sources stand. The next segment's material is unknown — expect any shape, read every chunk; the retelling can be anywhere, or absent.*
